Murder Over Mars

The conservatory breathed.

That was the only word that would do it. At fourteen minutes past midnight, in the deepest quiet of the Ares Meridian's long night-cycle, the Hydroponic Conservatory did not merely exist. It breathed. The low electromagnetic hum of the ultraviolet grow-lights threaded through the recycled air like a second pulse, steady and indifferent, while the thick green smell of crushed chlorophyll hung suspended in the stillness, so dense one might have tasted it on the back of the tongue. Above, the curved glass panels overhead were beaded with soft condensation, each droplet catching the violet glow and holding it a fraction longer than it should, so that the ceiling seemed to drip with slow, luminous tears that never fell.

And in the centre of all this quiet, green exhalation, Dr. Yuki Tanaka stood with her back to the long bench and her fingertips trailing along the spine of a seedling shelf.

She was not browsing. One must be very clear about that. A woman who tends plants for eleven years—eleven years of coaxing root systems from inert substrate, of whispering encouragement to photosynthetic cells that will not, in truth, hear her—does not run her fingers along a shelf in the manner of a woman browsing. She touches. She adjusts. She murmurs a small correction to a leaf angle. What Yuki was doing was counting. Her lips moved in a silent tally, barely a shaping of the air, and the numbers they formed had nothing whatsoever to do with the inventory of *Arabidopsis* cultivars or the nitrogen-fixing legumes in the third bay. Her eyes, behind the thin wire frames she wore for the grow-light's glare, were not admiring. They were measuring. Distances. Angles. The precise interval between the shelf's mounting bracket and the wall paneling behind it, where the station's internal conduit ran. The width of the gap beneath the shelf where a hand might pass but a camera's field of view might not.

She spoke to the seedlings. One would almost say she spoke to them with a kind of tender apology, the way a mother might explain to a small child why she must, for a few minutes, set aside the bedtime story. "Forgive me, little things," she murmured, and her voice was barely above the electromagnetic drone, soft and rounded, the voice of a woman who has spent a decade cultivating the language of patience. "I must be thorough. I shall return to you in the morning." The plants, naturally, offered no objection. They swayed, almost imperceptibly, in the thermal current rising from the nutrient reservoirs below, and the gesture was so perfectly in keeping with the ordinary rhythms of a botanist's evening round that any observer passing through the glass corridor would have seen only a woman checking on her charges.

But Yuki's jaw was set. One could see it in the faint tension at the hinge of her cheek, the way her lower lip pressed just slightly inward, as though she were holding a number in her mouth and refusing to let it out. Her breathing was shallow and measured, the controlled respiration of a woman who has decided, some hours ago, precisely what she requires of the next six, and is now executing the first of many small, quiet steps toward a conclusion she has already reached. There was no haste in her. That was the most remarkable thing. A woman who is stealing does not move slowly. A woman who is stealing does not apologise to the vegetables. And yet here she was, in the violet half-dark, running her fingertips along a shelf with the gentle deliberation of a calligrapher selecting the correct brush, while her mind performed the cold arithmetic of lock mechanisms and shift rotations and the precise moment at which the security rotation in the hydroponic wing would leave the vault's access corridor unmonitored for a window of ninety-three seconds.

She moved along the bench. The wood was cool under her palms, the grain worn smooth by years of her own hands, and she did not pause to admire the small scratches she had made herself over the years—nicks from a scalpel, a pressed thumbnail, the faint crescent of a ring she wore in her first month aboard. Her hands were the hands of a botanist. Slender, unhurried, with the short, practical nails of a woman who worked with soil and substrate and the occasional stubborn root. They were the hands that could, in the same unbroken motion, cradle a seedling no larger than a thumbnail or grip a pruning shear with the flat, certain pressure of a woman who has used the same pair of tools for four years. And as her fingers found the shears on the workbench—nestled in their usual place, parallel to the bench edge, where she had set them down that afternoon after trimming the lower leaves of the *Nepenthes*—she lifted them with the easy familiarity of a woman picking up a pencil.

She turned them in the grow-light.

The blades caught the violet glow and threw it back in two thin, parallel lines. There was a streak of chlorophyll across the cutting edge, green and wet, the ordinary residue of an afternoon's work. And beneath it, just beneath it, a darker film. A rust-brown smear, thin as a whisper, that might have been old sap oxidised in the heat of the grow-lights, or might have been something else entirely. Yuki looked at it for a moment. Two moments. Her fingers did not tighten. Her expression did not change. She set the shears back down with a care that was almost surgical, aligning them parallel to the bench edge, the blades facing the wall, exactly as they had been. As though restoring an order that had never been broken. As though the dark smear were a smudge of dirt on a kitchen counter that one wipes away without a second thought and does not examine further.

She did not call anyone. She did not reach for the intercom panel mounted on the far wall, the one that would connect her to the station's medical bay or to the duty officer in the Grand Rotunda. She did not log the anomaly in the conservatory's maintenance terminal, a small act that would have placed her in the room at this hour with a timestamp and a reason. She simply turned away from the bench, her spine straight, her shoulders level, and walked the three steps to the second shelf, where her fingers resumed their silent counting. The dark smear on the shears was, in her mind, already filed. Already rationalised. A small cut from a thorn, perhaps. A nick from the *Nepenthes*' own sharp petiole. The kind of minor, bloodied inconvenience that a botanist encounters so frequently that to make a record of it would be to make a spectacle of the ordinary. And so it was let go. Let go with the same gentle, apologetic efficiency with which she set aside a discoloured leaf. Forgive me, little thing. I must be thorough.

The station hummed around her. Beyond the glass panels, the void was absolute and black, pricked here and there with the fixed stars that the Ares Meridian's rotation carried slowly through the field of view. Somewhere in the structure's vast, rotating spine, other compartments held other lives in their own small pockets of light and purpose. She knew this. She did not need to confirm it. The station's ambient vibration, the faint tick of thermal expansion in the metal framing, told her that the world beyond her glass wall was still turning, still occupied, still alive with the small, private activities of the seven souls who shared this particular slice of the Martian sky. And yet the conservatory was hers. The glass was sealed. The air was hers. The green, thick, chlorophyll-sweet air, and the low violet hum, and the soft creak of the bench as she shifted her weight from one foot to the other. Total and perfect isolation. No sound but the lights. No voice but her own, murmuring its small apologies to the seedlings.

And then, without turning, without the slightest acceleration of her breathing or the faintest tremor in the fingers that still rested on the shelf's edge, her gaze drifted. Past the workbench. Past the rows of seedlings in their translucent growth trays, their white roots threading the nutrient gel like the veins of a translucent hand. Past the secondary bench where her field notes lay open to a page she had not yet reached. Her eyes travelled the length of the conservatory, along the far wall where the panels were darker, where the grow-lights thinned to their lowest setting, where the architecture of the station's interior gave way to something more functional, more sealed. There, at the terminus of the long room, set into the wall with the heavy, unadorned authority of a thing that was not meant to be looked at by anyone who had no business looking, the hydroponic vault's sealed bulkhead door sat dark and silent. Its surface was unmarked. Its lock was a small, circular mechanism she knew by heart, the combination of which she knew by heart, the camera above which she had, that afternoon, noted was angled eleven degrees to the left of the door's centre, leaving a narrow, triangular blind spot on the right-hand side that a person of her height could occupy without being seen. She did not look at the lock again. She did not need to. She had seen it enough.

She stood. She looked. The grow-lights hummed their low, violet note. The condensation beaded on the glass above and held its slow, luminous tears. The seedlings swayed in their thermal current, patient and green and utterly indifferent to the woman who stood among them, her hands at her sides, her breathing shallow and measured, her eyes fixed on a point at the far end of the room that was not, in any meaningful sense, a point at all but a question. A question that would not be answered tonight. A question that would be answered in the morning, or the night after that, in a window of ninety-three seconds that she had already, in the quiet arithmetic of her own mind, accounted for down to the second.

The shears lay on the bench, parallel to the edge, their dark smear catching the violet light in a thin, rust-coloured thread. The seedlings breathed. The station turned. And Dr. Yuki Tanaka stood in the green, humming silence of her own conservatory, looking at a door, waiting for the morning to come.

***

The sound was very small, and very wrong for the silence of the room.

It was the dry, metallic rasp of a filing-cabinet drawer sliding open on its ball-bearing track, a sound that in the pressurized quiet of the Mining Archive carried the particular quality of a bone being cracked. There was no other noise. The station's rotation generators hummed their low, subsonic note through the floorboards, a vibration one felt in the teeth and the soles of the feet rather than in the ears, and the recycled air carried its faint, perpetual tang of oxidized iron and old polymer binder, the smell of a place where documents went to be forgotten. A single overhead panel cast its amber glow across the rows of labeled crates and steel cabinets, throwing long, narrow shadows that made the room seem deeper than it was, further, as though the walls receded into some geological stratum of the past.

Dr. Cassius Wren did not look up.

His fingers, long and ink-stained at the tips, ran along the tabbed spines of assay reports with the unhurried precision of a man who had spent eleven years lecturing to the Interplanetary Trade Council on the mineral composition of Martian basalt. His left hand gripped the edge of the shared workstation for balance, knuckles pale, and his right hand worked the cabinet with the particular economy of motion that belongs to a man who knows, to the exact quarter-inch, which drawer holds what he requires. He did not need to read the labels. He did not need to count the drawers from the top. His fingers simply stopped at the third drawer from the bottom, the one marked Q3, and he pulled it free with a soft, decisive click.

There was a quality about him in that moment that would have been unrecognizable to the colleagues who had watched him, the previous evening, deliver a forty-minute address on isotopic differentiation with the easy, expansive charm of a man who had never once in his professional life worried about the contents of a filing cabinet. His shoulders were rigid. His breathing was shallow, a thin whistle of air through the nose that he did not appear to notice. His jaw worked once, twice, a small grinding of the molars against the back teeth, and then he was still again, his eyes moving along the folder spines with the flat, appraising gaze of a man reading a menu in a restaurant where he had dined a hundred times and knew, to the last garnish, what each dish contained.

He did not find what he was looking for in the third drawer.

He closed it. He opened the fourth. He opened the second. Each time the same small, metallic rasp, each time the same careful, unhurried passage of his fingers along the manila spines, and each time a small, almost imperceptible tightening of the muscles in his forearm. At the workstation behind him, he paused, turned, and ran a hand across the surface of the shared desk with the brisk, dismissive gesture of a man clearing crumbs from a table he had no patience for. His fingers traced the edge of a cold coffee mug, the flat top of a stylus, the smooth glass of the terminal screen, still dark. He pressed his thumb into the corner where a sheet of paper might have been tucked, might have been left, and then withdrew his hand as though it had burned him. Nothing. The desk was bare. He turned back to the cabinet.

"This is an extraordinary state of disorganization," he said, to no one, in the clipped, slightly condescending tone he reserved for junior researchers who misfiled their core samples. He snapped his fingers once against the cabinet frame, a sharp, dry crack in the silence, and the sound seemed to hang in the air for a moment, an unnecessary noise, a small performance of impatience that did not quite match the stillness of the rest of him.

Through the archive's single porthole, set high in the far wall above the topmost shelf of geological surveys, the rust-colored disc of Mars hung vast and indifferent against the black. It filled the glass almost entirely, a swollen eye of oxide and dust, and beyond it the void stretched in every direction, featureless and absolute. The room was small. The room was very small. The porthole was perhaps two feet in diameter, and yet what it showed was so enormously, so incomprehensibly large that the contrast made the cabinets and the crates and the single amber overhead panel seem like the interior of a matchbox, a tiny, sealed, pressurized nothing suspended in an ocean of dark.

In the Stakeholders' Salon, three corridors away and two decks down, Felix Drummond was shaking Margaret Ashworth's hand with the particular vigor of a man who has rehearsed the gesture in a mirror and is still not certain he has gotten the angle right. His smile was broad and slightly too quick. "Margaret, I must say, the preliminary figures are most encouraging," he said, and his voice carried the faint, high-pitched urgency of a man speaking a little faster than his thoughts, as though he were trying to outrun a question he could not quite formulate. Margaret's grip was firm, brief, and entirely without warmth. She regarded him over the rim of her glass with the patient, unblinking attention of a woman who had been reading his quarterly projections for three years and had learned, long ago, to listen to the silences between his words rather than the words themselves.

In the Captain's Study, Theron Blackwood was on his knees.

He had removed his shoes. This was not, in itself, unusual; the captain was a practical man, and the carpet in his study was old and thin and the kind that showed every scuff. But the position of his body, low and angular, his fingers probing the gap between the bookshelf and the wall, his head tilted at an angle that suggested he was listening for something beneath the floor, gave the act a different quality. He was not looking for a lost pen. He was looking for something that should not be there, and the fact that he was looking for it on his knees, in the privacy of his own study, with the door locked and the communication panel dark, suggested that he had not expected to find it.

In the Hydroponic Conservatory, Dr. Yuki Tanaka knelt beside the gene-seed vault's access panel with the gentle, deliberate care of a woman checking the soil moisture of a potted fern. Her pruning shears lay on the bench beside her, and the faint smear on the blades caught the green-tinged light and held it, a small dark mark that did not quite match the chlorophyll. She ran her thumb along the panel's seal, feeling for the faintest irregularity, the smallest deviation from the smooth, manufactured surface. Her expression was calm. Her hands were very still.

In the Spoke Gallery, where the alternating walnut and birch floor ran the full length of the station's rotating arm and the portholes were set close together in a long, unbroken row, Sera Okafor crouched beside a scuff mark that ran perhaps four inches long and bore the diagonal impression of a heel dragged across the grain. She held her recorder at arm's length, its small red light blinking, and said into it, in her flat, unhurried tone, "Scuff mark, approximately ten centimeters, consistent with a lateral drag, heel-first. Porthole seal, third unit from the Rotunda end, shows a hairline fracture along the lower gasket. Condensation present. I will note that there should be no condensation." She paused. She looked at the glass. She looked at the dark, curved surface of Mars beyond it, so close one could almost read the rust. Then she stood, brushed the dust from her knees, and moved on to the next porthole.

And in the Mining Archive, Dr. Wren opened the fifth drawer.

He did this with a speed that was almost aggressive, the drawer flying open on its track with a clatter that would have drawn a sharp look from any colleague, a sound too loud, too sudden, too much like a door being kicked. He recovered. He closed it. He opened the sixth. His right hand moved now with a different quality, a quick, raking passage over the folder spines, fingers splayed, pressing, testing, as though the manila covers might be warm, might yield under the pressure, might give up their contents to a man who asked them firmly enough. His left hand had left the edge of the desk. It rested now on his own thigh, fingers curled into a loose fist, and he did not appear to notice that he was holding himself up.

He found it in the seventh drawer.

His hand closed around a manila folder, and for one frozen, absolute beat he did not move. His jaw tightened with the particular, locked stillness of a man who has made a decision and is in the process of unmaking it, or perhaps of making a different one, and the muscles of his neck stood out in thin, taut lines beneath the skin. His breathing stopped. The subsonic hum of the generators went on, indifferent, threading through the floor and up through the soles of his shoes and into the bones of his feet. The amber light fell across his face and made his eyes look very deep, very dark, the eyes of a man in a cellar.

Then his gaze drifted. Slowly, almost against his will, it moved past the folder in his hand, past the rows of cabinets, past the amber panel, up to the porthole. Mars filled the glass. The rust-colored disc, the vast and patient indifference of it, the billions of years of wind and silence and the slow, grinding patience of geology, all of it looking down at this small, sealed, pressurized room where a man was holding a folder in his hand and could not bring himself to open it. His reflection was there, small and pale and very still, a thin, angular figure in the curve of the glass, and behind him the long, narrow shadows of the archive stretched out like the ribs of some vast, sleeping creature.

He did not open the folder.

He set it back on the shelf. His fingers lingered on the cover for a moment, a light, almost tender pressure, and then withdrew. The drawer slid shut. The sound it made, that soft, final, metallic click, echoed in the empty room and seemed to go on and on, a small, precise, definitive note, the last syllable of a sentence that no one else in the station would ever hear.

Dr. Wren stood very still for a long time. His left hand was still curled on his thigh. His right hand hung at his side, fingers slightly spread, as though they were still reaching for something that was no longer there. The amber light held him in its narrow pool, and the shadows held the rest of the room, and beyond the porthole Mars turned, slow and rust-colored and utterly without interest in the small, sealed, pressurized nothing where a man had just decided, in the silence, to walk away from a thing he had built with his own hands.

He straightened his tie. He checked his cuffs. He turned, and walked, with the measured, unhurried step of the Chief Geologist of the Station Scientific Division, toward the door. The door closed behind him with a soft pneumatic sigh.

The archive was empty. The subsonic hum went on. The porthole showed Mars.

And in the seventh drawer, the manila folder sat in the dark, patient and unopened, waiting for the morning to come.

***

The sound was almost nothing. A thread of air, no wider than a hair, threading its way through a fracture in the porthole's outer seal and singing a note so faint that in any other room on the station one might have dismissed it for the settling of a bolt, the tick of cooling metal, the ordinary mechanical respiration of a ship in motion. But the Spoke Gallery was empty. The Spoke Gallery was, at this hour, the quietest place on the Ares Meridian, and in that particular quality of silence the whistle made itself known the way a single dropped pin announces the presence of a hand.

Sera Okafor heard it before she saw the cause. She was already on her knees, or rather she was already low, crouched with the economical grace of a woman who had spent three years pressing herself flat against bulkheads in the Communications bay while the station groaned through its daily thermal cycles. Her left hand pressed flat against the alloy floor, fingers spread, and she felt the vibration of the rotation beneath her palm, that constant, sub-audible thrum that lived in the bones of the structure and in the soles of one's shoes. The lateral pull of the spin made her kneecap ache in a way it had not ached for three days. She noted this. She noted everything. Her fingertips, still faintly grey with the ink of her morning dispatch, traced the edge of a mark in the floor.

It was a scuff. A shallow crescent, no more than two fingers wide, gouged into the polished alloy with a force that had bitten through the topmost layer of finish and exposed the duller metal beneath. It ran perhaps four feet, tapering to nothing, and it was angled. Not random. Not the casual scuff of a boot heel or the drag of a dropped tool. It was the mark of weight, of resistance, of something—or someone—being pulled across a surface against its will, or else of a body that had been set down with more urgency than gentleness. Sera followed the line with her eyes, then with the pad of her thumb, and she thought, with the cold and unhurried precision of a woman who had buried three inconvenient truths for a better headline, that someone had left in a hurry. Or had been taken.

She rose, slowly, to her full height, and turned her attention to the porthole.

The glass was gone. Not shattered in the theatrical sense of a hundred glittering fragments scattered across the floor, but broken in a single, decisive blow that had sheared the outer pane from its frame, leaving a ragged crescent of metal and a gap through which the thin, cold air of the void pressed its faint, persistent whisper into the gallery. Beyond the gap, Mars hung. Not the Mars of the promotional holos that had sold the mining colony's subscription shares, not the Mars of the glossy quarterly reports with their careful infographics and their promises of iron-oxide abundance. This Mars was rust-red and patient and utterly indifferent to the small mechanical wound in the hull of the station that orbited it. It simply was. It had been there before the Ares Meridian and it would be there long after the last of them had been recycled into the void. Sera looked at it for a moment, and then she looked at the broken seal, and she did something that a Communications Officer of the Ares Meridian was not supposed to do.

She did not reach for the maintenance intercom.

She did not key the structural damage report. She did not log the breach in the station's engineering log, nor did she transmit the standard alert to the engineering crew in the lower decks. She stood instead in the thin whistle of escaping atmosphere, with the ozone tang of the broken seal mixing with the faint, stale smell of old coffee from the service alcove at the gallery's far end, and she considered. She considered the way the scuff mark angled toward the porthole, as though whatever had been dragged across the floor had been dragged toward the glass. She considered the fact that no one else had been dispatched to this gallery. No one else had seen. The station's rotation carried them all through their respective duties in their respective corridors, and the Spoke Gallery, that long, narrow annulus between the Rotunda and the outer rim, was a place one passed through, not a place one lingered in. It was, in the taxonomy of the Ares Meridian, a non-place. A corridor with a view.

And yet. And yet someone had broken the porthole. Someone had dragged something—or someone—across the floor. And in the four feet of gouged alloy, Sera could see, if she knelt low enough and tilted her head, the faintest impression of a sole. Not a boot. A shoe. A dress shoe, perhaps, or a soft-soled crew slipper. The tread was shallow, the pattern worn thin. It did not match the heavy treads of the engineering staff, nor the lugged soles of the mining division. It was, she thought, a shoe belonging to someone who walked the upper decks. Someone who attended the Stakeholders' Salon. Someone who shook hands with a firm grip and spoke quickly, trying to project a confidence that the tightness around the eyes betrayed as something closer to anxiety.

Sera knelt again. She pressed her thumb into the scuff mark, feeling the rough edge of the gouge, and she was aware, with the quiet certainty of a woman who has spent her career translating other people's words into other people's stories, that the mark could be read two ways. It could be an accident. A porthole seal that failed under thermal stress, a maintenance panel that gave way, a body that stumbled and fell and was dragged to safety by a well-meaning colleague. A story of mechanical failure, of the station's aging infrastructure, of the kind of small, unglamorous tragedy that made a solid paragraph in the morning dispatch but nothing more. Or it could be a struggle. A violent one. The kind in which a hand was pressed over a mouth and a body was pressed against a wall and the glass went in with a sound like a cracked egg. The kind in which the scuff mark was not a fall but a dragging, and the broken porthole was not a failure but a weapon, or a consequence of a weapon.

The two stories were not, of course, mutually exclusive. But they were, in the hands of a journalist, very different animals. One was a column. The other was a lever.

Sera stood. She brushed the fine metallic dust from her sleeve with a quick, dismissive flick of the wrist, the way one brushes a speck of lint from a coat before entering a room where one does not wish to be noticed. The gesture was so small, so unremarkable, that it might have been nothing at all. But her other hand, the one that had not been touching the floor, moved to her breast pocket, and her fingers closed around the small, cool weight that lived there. The encrypted data-chip. The one she had lifted, in the small hours of a night three weeks past, from a drawer in a study that was not hers, from a desk that was not her desk, where it had lain beside a glass of brandy gone cloudy amber and a journal whose last entry had been a single, underlined sentence. The chip held transmissions. Encrypted, unlogged, unreported. Transmissions that, if they were to reach the hands of the Colonial Authority, would unmake a great many carefully constructed careers and, more likely, a great many carefully constructed silences.

She had not reported the chip. She had not reported the chip to the station's security office, nor to the editorial board of the Interplanetary Press, nor to the small, grey-haired woman who sat in the Grand Rotunda and asked questions in a tone that suggested she already knew the answers. She had kept it. She had kept it the way one keeps a letter one has not yet sent, the way one keeps a match one has not yet struck, the way one keeps a truth one has not yet decided whether to tell. And now, in the Spoke Gallery, with the thin whistle of the broken seal and the rust-red patience of Mars beyond the gap, she held the evidence of a second secret in her other hand. A sliver of porthole glass, no larger than a thumbnail, still warm from the impact that had freed it from the frame. She turned it between two fingers, feeling the sharp edge, the way it caught the dim ring-light of the gallery and threw a small, cold star across the alloy wall.

A struggle. Or a very expensive argument.

She said it to no one. The words left her mouth in a whisper, barely a vibration of the lips, and they dissolved into the thin, whistling air as quickly as they had formed. But the choice behind them was not so easily dissolved. The glass shard went into her breast pocket, sliding down to rest against the data-chip, the two secrets pressing together in the dark like two stones in a pocket, cold and smooth and heavy with the weight of what they might do in the right hands. Or the wrong ones. Or the hands that had not yet been decided.

The whistle from the crack grew a fraction louder. The air was thinning, though not enough to matter. Not yet. The station's rotation hummed in the walls, in the floor, in the soles of her shoes, a constant and indifferent pulse. The service alcove at the far end of the gallery still carried the ghost of its old coffee, that particular burnt-and-bitter smell that meant the machine had not been cleaned in a week and the water reservoir was past its fill line. Mars turned, slowly, beyond the broken porthole. The scuff mark waited in the floor, patient and unspoken.

Sera Okafor straightened her collar. She adjusted the strap of her satchel, the one that held her dispatch pad and her stylus and the three drafts of the morning's column, none of which mentioned the Spoke Gallery, none of which mentioned a broken porthole or a drag mark in the alloy or a shoe sole pressed into the metal like a signature. She walked to the far end of the gallery, where the corridor opened back into the Rotunda, and she did not look back. The Spoke Gallery was empty again. The thin whistle continued, unheeded, unlogged, unreported. And in the broken frame, a single sliver of glass that had not quite fallen caught the dim ring-light and held it, a tiny, indifferent eye, watching the empty floor where the mark had been, waiting for the morning to come.

***

The hand came first. It always came first, with Felix Drummond, as though the man had been assembled in a workroom somewhere behind the sternum and the fingers were the first component bolted into place. His grip closed around Margaret Ashworth's with a pressure that lasted a half-second too long, a half-degree too tight, and the small radius of air between them filled with the particular sweetness of a cologne that said, in the language of perfumers, *I have a yacht in Monaco*, which was a statement so far removed from the truth that one almost pitied the nostrils required to receive it. Margaret did not match the pressure. She let his fingers release. She let them release first, which was the whole point, and only then did she open her mouth.

"Good morning, Mr. Drummond."

The sentence was a single, flat, perfectly level thing. The kind of sentence a woman uses when she has already decided what a person is and is merely waiting for them to confirm it. Felix's throat worked. One swallow. Two. His smile, which had been in transit since the corridor, arrived at his face a full beat after his words, and Margaret noted the discrepancy with the same quiet thoroughness she would have applied to a column of figures that refused to reconcile.

"The quarterly figures," Felix began, and then, as though the words had startled him, he began again. "The quarterly figures, you see, I was looking at them this morning, and there's a rather peculiar—well, I wouldn't say irregular, no, that's too strong, but a certain... fluctuation. In the dividend allocations. Quite troubling, really. One must ask questions, I think. One simply must."

He said this while smoothing his lapel with the left hand and gesturing vaguely with the right, as though the quarterly earnings report were a small, skittish animal he was trying to corner in the drawing room. His eyes, Margaret observed, had flicked to the door twice. Not once. Twice. The particular flick of a man checking his exit, which is not the same thing as checking his watch.

She held her teacup with two fingers at the rim, the way one holds a scalpel, and said nothing. The Stakeholders' Salon hummed around them with the low, subsonic vibration of the station's rotation, a sound felt less in the ear than in the molars, a constant and indifferent pressure against the inner skull. Through the far glass, Mars was not a landscape. It was a colour. A rusty, indifferent smear of ochre and umber, beautiful in the way that a bruise is beautiful, and utterly irrelevant to the two people standing between the polished walnut paneling and the artificial warmth of the overhead lights, which rendered everyone a shade more tanned than they had any right to be.

"You have my attention, Mr. Drummond," Margaret said. It was not a question. It was not an invitation. It was the equivalent of a door being opened a precise three inches, no more, no less, and the man on the other side being permitted to step through or not, as he chose, while the woman on this side remained exactly where she was.

Felix stepped through. He talked. He talked about allocations and projections and the peculiar way in which certain line items appeared, to his untrained eye, to be a little... he searched for the word, his fingers drumming a small, arrhythmic pattern against the back of his chair, a little *off*. Not wrong. Off. A distinction he clearly believed was important. Margaret listened. She listened the way a surveyor listens to a wall, feeling for the hollow place behind the plaster where the foundation has shifted, where the load-bearing member has been, over the course of several quiet years, carefully and methodically removed.

In the Hydroponic Conservatory, three compartments down the starboard corridor, Dr. Yuki Tanaka set down her pruning shears with the particular care of a woman placing a knife on a table after she has decided, finally, not to use it. The green, humming silence of the seed vault closed around her. She had looked. She had looked at the access points, at the seal mechanisms, at the small brass keyhole that required a specific rotation and a specific pressure and a

***

The glass sat on the leather blotter as though it had been placed there by a careful hand, and perhaps it had been. Single-malt, half-finished, the liquid catching the amber cone of the study's overhead lamp in a way that made the small room feel lit from within, as if the brandy itself were a slow-burning coal. Captain Theron Blackwood had not touched it in three days. He was certain of this. He had poured it on the evening of the last consortium briefing, set it down, and walked to the porthole to watch Mars turn its rusted face toward the station's slow rotation. Three days. And yet the surface of the liquid had developed a faint iridescent film, a thin sheen that should not have been there, and at the very rim, where the glass met the air, there was a whisper of bitterness that was not malt, not oak, not the clean burn of a well-aged whisky. It was the smell of bitter almonds, faint as a memory, but unmistakable to a man who had spent twenty years in the Colonial Service and had once identified aconite in a chowder at a garrison mess in the Outer Territories.

Blackwood set the glass down.

This was the thing that would later be noted, in any account, by anyone who had the misfortune of being in that room in the minutes that followed. He set the glass down with a hand that did not quite stop trembling. Not a shake, not a tremor of cold or fatigue. A fine, persistent vibration in the fingers, as though the muscles had been told to release the glass and had not entirely received the instruction. He placed it on the blotter with the deliberation of a man setting down a live grenade, and then he turned his back. He walked to the narrow window that looked out over the station's outer hull, where the stars were fixed and indifferent, and he stood there with his hands clasped behind him, and he said nothing. The air recycler hummed its low, mechanical hymn. The rotation pressed against his eardrums, that constant, almost subliminal reminder that they were sealed in a steel drum spinning above a dead planet, and that no one could come to their aid, no one could walk out, no one could call for help, because there was no one. There was only the station. There was only the room. There was only the glass on the blotter, and the man with his back turned, and the silence that had a texture, a weight, almost the density of the Martian dust beyond the hull.

Three beats passed. Or it was four. Time in a sealed room is a relative thing, measured not in seconds but in the intervals between heartbeats. His jaw worked. A muscle feathered beneath the skin at the angle of his cheek. And then, without turning, in a voice so low it was almost a private conversation with the air itself, he said: "Aconite. I know aconite."

In the Stakeholders' Salon, three corridors away and two decks below, Felix Drummond was doing something that Felix Drummond did not often do, which was to be still. He stood beside Margaret Ashworth with his hands clasped before him, his knuckles white, his smile fixed at the precise angle of a man who has rehearsed concern in a mirror and is now performing it for an audience of one. He had been talking about the quarterly figures, about the consortium's projections, about the need for vigilance, and he had said the word "vigilance" four times in the space of a minute, and Margaret had said nothing, had merely listened with the patient, unblinking attention of a woman who had been chairing rooms full of men for eleven years and had learned that the most dangerous man in any assembly was the one who spoke most urgently about the safety of everyone else. She had filed his nervousness away. She had not yet named it. But she had filed it.

And in the Grand Rotunda, where the station's axis turned them all slowly through the dark, Dr. Yuki Tanaka stood very still with her hands folded in front of her, watching the crew gather in small, wary clusters. Sera Okafor was moving through them with the easy, observant gait of a woman who was always, always writing, even when her pen was not in her hand. Tanaka had come from her conservatory, where the pruning shears still sat on the workbench, their blades wet with chlorophyll and something darker that she had not yet named to herself. She had come to be seen. To be present. To be, in the language of the station's social architecture, *accountable*. And she watched Blackwood's retreating figure disappear down the corridor toward the Rotunda, and she thought, with the quiet precision of a woman who grew things for a living: *He is afraid. And he is not afraid of Mars.*

It was in this climate of distributed suspicion that Dr. Cassius Wren arrived at the Captain's Study.

The station turned.

It was the first thing one noticed in the Grand Rotunda, and the first thing one forgot, in the way one forgets the sound of one's own breathing. The lateral pull was barely perceptible—a half-degree, perhaps less—enough to tip the wine in each of the seven glasses set along the perimeter tables a fraction off level, enough to make the surface of the liquid lean against the right-hand wall of the glass as though it were a small, obedient animal pressing its nose to the pane. The smell was the second thing: old varnish, the particular sweetness of lacquer that had been polished and re-polished over years of orbital maintenance, underscored by a faint metallic tang of ozone that clung to the curved upper walls where the junction panels met the ceiling in their long, silvered seams. Beneath both, the station hummed. Not a sound, exactly. A vibration. A patient, geological thrumming in the soles of one's shoes, as though the floor itself were a tuning fork struck long ago and never quite permitted to fall silent.

Into this ambient murmur—this low chord of rotation and recirculated air and the soft tick of the climate regulators—one voice cut, unhurried, without any visible effort, as though it had been waiting for a silence that had not yet arrived and simply decided to occupy one.

"I trust the evening's hospitality is to the standard I expect from a station that wishes to retain its operating license."

Margaret Ashworth did not raise her voice. She did not turn to face the room. She stood at the head of the long table, her hands resting on its surface with the flat, absolute stillness of a woman who had long since ceased to fidget, and she spoke to the varnish, to the ozone, to the slow tilt of the wine, to no one and everyone simultaneously. The sentence was delivered in the register one might

***

The word came out of the stillness of the salon like a scalpel finding the precise line beneath the skin. Not loud. Not even, one might have thought, quite a word at all. A single syllable, placed with the unhurried care of a woman setting a teacup down on a saucer she had already decided was slightly too thin.

"Felix."

And before the sound of it had quite settled into the velvet upholstery, before the low, subsonic hum of the station's rotation had carried it into the adjacent corridor, the other man had already shifted. Not in his chair. In his skin. The way a man shifts when a draft finds the one seam in his coat he has not yet patched. His cologne hit first, as it always did in that room, a cloying, expensive wave that seemed to arrive three seconds before the body it belonged to. The curved observation glass beyond the settee held Mars in its frame, vast and rust-amber, the colour of dried blood pressed against a windowpane, and the slow, imperceptible sway of the crystal decanter on the low table between them marked the passage of a rotation that neither of them could stop.

Margaret Ashworth did not move. Her hands were folded in her lap, fingers interlaced with the mechanical precision of a woman who had long ago decided that stillness was a more effective instrument than speech. Her spine was a straight line from the small of her back to the crown of her head. She looked at Felix Drummond the way a surveyor looks at a fault line in a foundation: not with alarm, not with curiosity, but with the cold, professional assessment of a structure that has begun, however slightly, to give.

Felix, for his part, was already performing. His right hand rose to adjust a cufflink that required no adjusting. His left hand found the decanter, tilted it, and poured a measure of whisky into a glass that already held a measure of whisky. The liquid swirled, catching the Mars-light, and he smiled. It was a good smile. The kind that had opened boardrooms and closed negotiations in three time zones. The kind that cost, one suspected, rather more than a technician's monthly ration of protein bars.

"Margaret," he said, and the way he said it, warm and unhurried, was precisely the way a man says the name of a guest he is grateful to see, which made it all the more remarkable that neither of them had been, in the preceding hour, anything so generous as a guest to the other.

"The quarterly figures," Margaret said. Not a question. A statement. The way one might say, *the weather is turning.*

Felix set the decanter down. He had not, in fact, needed to drink. "They are, as always, in the process of being reconciled. You know how these things are. The colony's output, the shipping manifests, the—"

"I am not asking about the process, Felix."

A silence. The station hummed. Somewhere in the deep architecture of the Ares Meridian, a ventilation cycle shifted, and the recycled air, faintly metallic, carried the ghost of a hundred other conversations that had taken place in that room before this one. Margaret's eyes did not blink. She did not lean forward. She did not, in any way that a lesser woman might have, perform the small, human gesture of tilting her head, as though to say, *please, explain yourself.* She simply waited. And the waiting, in a room of that particular quality, was worse than any question.

Felix's hand found the glass again. He set it down. He did not drink. His eyes moved, briefly, to the corridor beyond the salon's arched doorway, to the long sightline that led, past the Spoke Gallery, to the Grand Rotunda. A fraction of a second. A man checking the exit. A man confirming that the door was where he believed the door to be.

"The reports," he said, and the word came out a shade too carefully, as though he were placing it on a shelf among other reports, other documents, other perfectly ordinary papers in a perfectly ordinary filing cabinet. "They are what they are. The consortium's position is strong. I am certain you see that."

"I see that certain figures do not reconcile with the source data." Margaret's voice had not changed. It had not risen, or sharpened, or taken on the particular edge of a woman who has been denied. It was, if anything, gentler. The gentleness of a surgeon who has already decided which organ must be removed and is merely confirming, for the record, that the patient is still breathing. "I see that the yield certificates from the restricted shaft do not match the assay returns filed with the Colonial Authority. I see that a line in the third quarter has been redacted, and that the redaction is not, in my experience, the practice of a consortium that has nothing to hide."

The word *manipulated* had not been spoken. It did not need to be. It hung in the recycled air between them, faint and metallic, the way the taste of copper lingers on the tongue after a small cut. Felix's smile did not waver. It could not waver. It was structural. Remove it and the entire performance, the too-tight suit, the cufflinks, the cologne, the careful, rehearsed cadence of his speech, would collapse into the man beneath, and the man beneath was, Margaret had decided in the three weeks since she had first opened the ledgers, a liability. A line item. An asset that, upon closer audit, had been quietly, methodically, siphoned into accounts that did not appear on any balance sheet.

"I am sure," Felix said, "that you will find, upon further review, that the discrepancy is merely a matter of rounding. A clerical oversight. These things happen."

"Cleryical oversights," Margaret repeated, and the word sat in the air like a stone dropped into still water, "do not, in my experience, require a specific rotation of a brass key and a specific pressure of the seal to open the cabinet in which they are filed."

Felix did not blink. This was, one gathered, the moment. The moment at which the smile, however good, became a thing that required effort to maintain. His jaw worked, once, a small, almost invisible contraction, the way a man chews a word he has decided not to swallow. His hands, which a moment before had been steady, which had poured the whisky with the easy confidence of a man who owned the glass and the liquid and the room, were now, beneath the edge of the table, where Margaret's eyes did not quite reach, trembling. A fine, rapid vibration, the kind that comes not from cold or from caffeine but from the particular, private terror of a man who has been, in the space of four sentences, identified. Not named. Not accused. Identified. Which is, in the taxonomy of threats, the more dangerous category.

"Margaret," he said. And this time the name was different. Warmer. More urgent. The voice of a man who is, very carefully, very precisely, closing a door. "I think it would be wise for us to table this. The figures are in the hands of the auditors. The Colonial Authority has its own process. You would not wish to—"

"I would not wish to what, Felix?"

He did not answer. He did not need to. The question had been a courtesy, a social obligation, the way one offers a guest a second glass of wine in a room where the bottle is already empty. And Felix, to his credit, accepted the courtesy. He stood. The movement was smooth, practised, the rise of a man who has stood ten thousand times in ten thousand rooms and knows, to the half-degree, the angle at which the body should leave the chair. His hand extended.

Margaret did not take it. Not at once. She let the hand hang in the air between them, the fingers slightly open, the thumb resting against the index in a way that was almost, but not quite, a gesture of reassurance. And in that half-second of suspended contact, the cologne thickened, the Mars-light shifted a fraction of a degree as the station turned, and the crystal decanter swayed, just barely, just enough to make the whisky in the glass ripple.

Then her hand rose. Met his. The grip was firm, brief, and entirely without warmth. A handshake. A business transaction. The closing of a file.

Felix's fingers were warm. Slightly damp. He held the handshake one beat longer than propriety required, and in that extra beat, Margaret felt, or imagined she felt, the faint tremor travel up through his knuckles, a small, mechanical vibration, like a watch that has wound down and is still, out of habit, trying to tick.

"I do hope," Felix said, and his voice was the voice of a man reciting a line he had rehearsed in a mirror, "the consortium's books are as tidy as you imagine them, Margaret."

He released her hand. He turned. He walked toward the arched doorway, toward the corridor, toward the Grand Rotunda where, in this same minute, Sera Okafor was speaking in low, careful tones to Dr. Yuki Tanaka, their voices a hushed murmur beneath the hum of the station, while Captain Blackwood stood a step behind them, listening, his face set in the particular expression of a man who has been listening to the same conversation three times and is still not certain he has understood it. And in the Captain's Study, two corridors away, Dr. Cassius Wren stood very still before the locked drawer, his fingers resting on the cold brass of the handle, the glass of brandy on the desk beside him gone cloudy amber, its surface catching the dim light in a way that suggested, to a mind already inclined toward suspicion, a faint and bitter sweetness at the rim.

But none of this mattered to Margaret Ashworth. She did not watch Felix go. She did not follow his silhouette through the archway, did not track the soft click of his shoes on the alternating walnut and birch floor as he moved down the corridor, did not listen for the distant, muffled sound of the Grand Rotunda's fountain, where a single droplet of something dark had been partially dissolved in the trickling water, a small, indifferent detail that would mean nothing to anyone who had not yet learned to look for it.

She was already calculating. Her gaze was fixed on the decanter, on the glass Felix had set down, on the faint ring of amber liquid at the base where the whisky had been, and where it had not. Her lips moved. Once. A single, silent syllable, or perhaps a number, or perhaps the name of a file in a cabinet that required a specific rotation of a brass key. The arithmetic of a woman removing an asset from a ledger. The quiet, surgical subtraction of a line item that no longer reconciled.

The chair where Felix had been sat was empty. The velvet was still faintly warm. The cologne lingered in the recycled air, thick and sweet and faintly cloying, hanging in the rust-amber glow of Mars like an accusation that had not yet found the words to finish itself. And the decanter swayed. And the station turned. And the night, out there beyond the curved glass, went on being red.

The drawer yielded to his fingers with the particular resistance of a mechanism that had not been opened in some considerable time, and Dr. Cassius Wren noted this fact with the detached, cataloguing precision of a man filing an observation under "irrelevant" in the private ledger he kept of everything that might, in some improbable configuration of events, become relevant to his defense. His long fingers—still bearing the faint orange residue of Martian regolith samples, the pigment worked into the whorls of his fingerprints as though the planet itself had pressed its thumbprint upon him—slid the brass handle with an unhurried gentleness that suggested nothing more than a scholar's idle curiosity. The station's rotational hum vibrated through the soles of his shoes, a low, sustained B-flat that he had always found soothing, and for one half-second his jaw tightened because the sound reminded him of the centrifuge in Lab Three, the particular whine of its motor at four thousand revolutions, the afternoon in November when he had first set down the chromite yield figures that did not correspond to the rock in his hands. Then the composure reasserted itself, as it always did, with the smooth, muscular certainty of a man who had spent twenty-two years performing the role of a calm man in rooms where calm was expected, and he was merely a distinguished geologist examining a colleague's papers, and nothing more, and the drawer opened, and the smell of old paper and pipe tobacco rose from within like a small, private exhalation.

He did not reach in. He did not grab a folder or a sheaf of documents with the eager, sprawling hand of a man who had not thought through his movements. Instead, he extended two fingers—index and middle, the same two he used to turn the pages of a mineralogy text in the reading room back on the Earth side—and drew out a single manila envelope, setting it on the blotter with a soft, dry whisper. He turned it over. He read the label. He set it back. He drew out a second. A third. Each one examined with the same unhurried, almost ceremonial patience, as though the act of handling them were an end in itself, as though the Captain's private correspondence were a geological formation he was dating by stratigraphy rather than a man's secret life laid bare in typed carbon copies. His breathing, which had been diaphragmatic, which had been the deep and even respiration of a man at rest, shifted. He felt it shift. The air now entered and left through his upper chest, shallow and quick, and he was aware of the awareness with the cold, clinical detachment of a man watching himself from a considerable distance, noting the symptom, filing it, continuing.

The porthole was to his left. He had passed it on his entry, and he had not looked, and now, as he set the third envelope back into the drawer with its precise, two-fingered release, his eyes caught the cold blue-white glow that framed the rust-colored curve of the world below. Mars. The ochre sweep of the Tharsis plateau, the long shadow of Olympus casting its dark thumb across the plain. His life's work. The colony. The mine shafts he had charted, the strata he had named, the twenty-two years of patient, meticulous observation that had made his name a small but respected thing in the geological community of two worlds. He turned his head away. His left hand, which had been resting on the desk edge, tightened. The knuckles whitened. He unclenched it, smoothed his fingers over the blotter as though wiping away a smudge, and looked at the drawer again.

Three floors below, in the Grand Rotunda, the conversation had taken its third turn. Felix Drummond was leaning forward on the marble table, his voice pitched at the particular volume of a man who wishes to be overheard, speaking of the quarterly figures with an anxiety so carefully calibrated that it registered, to the trained ear, as performance. Sera Okafor listened with the stillness of a woman who had learned, in nine years of embedded reporting, that the most useful sentences were the ones one did not ask for but was handed, and she said nothing, only tilted her head a degree, and Drummond, mistaking silence for engagement, poured himself another measure of water from the carafe and continued. Dr. Tanaka appeared at his elbow, her botanist's hands still faintly green with chlorophyll, and asked a question about the consortium's soil-reclamation budget that was, on its surface, an inquiry about hydroponic allocation and, beneath the surface, a test of how much Drummond would volunteer before the topic shifted. And then Margaret Ashworth entered the Rotunda from the Spoke Gallery, her footsteps measured, her expression the particular blankness of a woman who has already decided what she will say and is merely waiting for the correct moment to say it, and the four of them arranged themselves around the table with the unconscious choreography of people who have been in the same enclosed space for eleven days and know, without being told, the exact number of degrees of separation between their own interests and those of the others.

Wren did not hear any of this. Wren was on the fourth floor, in the Captain's Study, and he was looking for a set of documents that existed in triplicate, three identical copies of the original ore-yield certifications, signed and sealed by the Colonial Authority, one held in the Mining Archive, one in the Consortium's own vault, and one—the one that mattered, the one that would, if produced before the Interplanetary Trade Council at the morning rotation cycle, either confirm or annihilate the figures he had set down in Lab Three on a Tuesday afternoon in November—was, he was almost certain, in this drawer. Or in the next drawer. Or in the steel cabinet against the far wall, the one whose lock bore a fresh, shallow scratch, a mark made by a key that had been turned with slightly too much force by a hand that was not, he suspected, the Captain's. He crossed to the cabinet. The key was still in the cylinder. He did not touch it. He stood before it for a moment, his reflection ghosting in the polished steel, and then he turned back to the desk, because the key in the cylinder was a fact that would be noted by whoever came after him, and he was not, in his private and terrified accounting of the evening, a man who wished to be noted.

The journal lay on the blotter, open to a page he had not intended to read. The Captain's hand, that broad, slightly slanted scrawl he had seen a dozen times in signed directives and crew manifests, filled the last three lines of the entry. I will not sign the extension. The sentence ended without a period. Wren looked at it for the length of one slow breath, and then he looked away, and the words remained on the page, patient and indifferent, waiting for the morning rotation cycle and the Trade Council and the particular arrangement of testimony that would make them either a footnote or a verdict. He did not take the journal. He did not close it. He set his two fingers upon the blotter, pressed them flat, and felt the dry recycled air move across his chapped lower lip in a small, constant draft, and he thought, with the detached clarity of a man observing a symptom in another person, that he was breathing too fast.

He opened the bottom drawer again. He had not finished. There were more envelopes, more folders, a small leather case that he lifted with both hands and set back down without opening, a sheaf of correspondence tied with twine that he untied and retied with the same number of turns. His movements were those of a surgeon, precise and economical, each gesture calibrated to leave the minimum trace, the minimum disturbance, the minimum evidence that he had been here, that he had been looking, that he had been looking for a specific thing and had not found it. The pipe tobacco smell was stronger now, or perhaps his nostrils had simply adjusted, and the dry air of the station clung to the inside of his throat, and the B-flat hum continued its endless, subliminal vibration through the floor, through the soles of his shoes, through the small bones of his feet, and he thought, with a sudden and unwelcome clarity, that he had been in this room before, in some earlier and less urgent configuration of the evening, and that the Captain had been here too, and that the brandy glass on the side table had been set down with a hand that did not quite stop trembling, and that his own fingers had found the cold brass of a locked drawer in the same breath, and that two men in a sealed room had each been holding a secret that would destroy the other, and that neither had known the other's hand was already reaching.

He closed the drawer. The brass handle clicked home with a small, final sound, and the silence that followed was not silence but the B-flat, the hum, the constant rotational thrum of the station turning in its slow arc above the rust-colored world, and in that silence he was aware of the calendar on the desk, the small desk calendar with its printed dates and its single annotation. Tomorrow's date was circled in red ink. Beside the circle, in a hand that was not the Captain's—smaller, more angular, the loops of the letters carrying a particular pressure that he recognized without being able to say why—there were three words. He did not read them. He looked at the red circle, at the angular handwriting, at the small, patient fact of the annotation, and he understood, with the cold and absolute certainty of a man who

The grow-lights had shifted to their night frequency an hour ago, and the conservatory now breathed in a low, amber hum that Dr. Yuki Tanaka felt less with her ears than in the soles of her shoes, a subsonic pulse rising through the composite floor plating, through the thin soles of her boots, into the small bones of her feet. It was the station turning. It was always the station turning, that slow, inexorable rotation that pressed the air against the curved walls and kept the water in its channels and the soil in its beds. At two in the morning, with no one else in the Conservatory, the turning was the only sound that mattered. Or rather, the only sound besides the faint, almost imperceptible trickle of the ceramic fountain at the room's centre, a thin ribbon of recirculated water sliding over a lip of white glaze and falling, drop by patient drop, into the shallow basin below.

She had been standing at the far end of the room for some minutes now, hands clasped loosely behind her back, watching. The air was thick with the green, vegetal smell of wet loam and crushed chlorophyll, a smell so dense and alive it almost had a texture, a weight, as though breathing it in filled the lungs with something slightly thicker than air. The rows of seedling trays stretched in their orderly ranks on either side of the central walkway, their translucent canopies glowing faintly under the amber cycle, and beyond them the taller specimens—the fruiting vines, the flowering perennials, the single, precious orchid whose roots she had wrapped in silk ribbon three days prior and whose bloom she had not yet been able to name—stood in their quiet, patient rows. Everything was alive. Everything was growing. Everything was, by every measure she had spent eleven years learning to apply, perfectly, undeniably well.

And then there was the drop.

It sat on the rim of the fountain, on the inner lip where the water's thin sheet broke and fell, and it was the single most violent colour in a room composed entirely of green and amber and the soft white of ceramic. A drop of blood. Not a smear, not a splash, not the generous, theatrical spatter one might expect from a drama. A single, round, impossibly bright crimson bead, partially dissolved already by the trickling water, its edges softening into a pale pink halo that spread in the thin film of moisture. It caught the blue-green glow of the night-cycle lights and held it, a small, dark jewel, a punctuation mark in a sentence she did not wish to read.

Dr. Tanaka's breath fogged slightly in the cool, recycled air. She noticed this. She noticed that her hands, clasped behind her back, had begun to tremble in the smallest, most peripheral way, a vibration in the fingertips that she could feel but would not, in any professional context, have admitted to. She unclasped her hands. She let them fall to her sides. The fingers were soft, the knuckles slightly swollen, the nails short and clean, the pads of the thumbs and index fingers carrying the faint, permanent stain of dark soil that no amount of washing could fully remove. The fingers of a woman who spoke to seedlings. The fingers of a woman who had, that very afternoon, coaxed a stubborn tomato vine back into its trellis with a patience that bordered on devotion.

She crossed the walkway. Three steps. Four. The composite floor was cool under her boots, and the rotation's pull was barely perceptible, a gentle inclination to the left, as though the ground itself were leaning slightly, listening. She stopped at the fountain's edge. She did not kneel. Kneeling was a gesture, a posture of reverence or supplication, and she had no intention of performing either. Instead she crouched, lowering her weight onto the balls of her feet, bending at the knee in the practical, economical way one does when one is reaching for a tool on a low shelf or examining a root system at soil level. Her knees pressed against the cool floor. Her back was straight. Her head inclined forward.

The blood droplet was perhaps two centimetres from where her right hand hovered. She extended her thumb, and the pad of it stopped one centimetre above the rim of the fountain, above the pale pink halo, above the small, dark centre that was still, even now, still wet. She did not touch it. A scientist does not touch the evidence with bare skin. A scientist observes, measures, records. She tilted her head a fraction of a degree, and in the amber-and-blue light the drop resolved into something more particular: a slightly irregular oval, the edges where the water had begun to dissolve it showing a faint, feathery diffusion, the centre still dense, still opaque, still carrying the particular weight of haemoglobin that no other fluid in this station could imitate.

She exhaled through her nose. A long, slow, controlled exhalation, the kind she used when calibrating a pH meter or when a seedling's leaves had yellowed for no apparent reason and she needed to think before she reached for the shears. The breath fogged again, and in the fog she could see, very faintly, the reflection of her own eyes in the water's surface in the basin below. She did not look at the reflection.

Her gaze shifted. To her left, perhaps thirty centimetres from the fountain's base, the engineered soil was disturbed. She had noticed it from across the room, of course. One notices such things. A botanist notices such things the way a sailor notices a change in the wind, before the conscious mind has registered the shift, before the word for it has been found. The loam here was turned, flipped, the dark, compacted surface broken to reveal the paler, drier substrate beneath. And in the turned earth, pressed into the soft, freshly exposed layer, was the faint impression of a shoe sole. Not her own. She knew the pattern of her own boot, the shallow tread, the particular wear on the left heel. This impression was deeper, more defined, the tread pattern angular, the size larger. Someone had stood here. Someone had pressed their foot into this soil with a weight that spoke of force, of urgency, of a body that had been moved rather than placed.

She reached out, and this time her fingers did touch the soil. Not the blood. The soil. The pad of her index finger traced the edge of the turned patch, feeling the difference in texture between the compacted surface and the loose, disturbed layer. Cool. Slightly damp. The smell of it was different from the rest of the bed, sharper, less alive, as though the microorganisms in that small patch had been killed by the sudden violence of the turning. She withdrew her finger and held it up, examining the faint dark smudge on the pad. Soil. Only soil. And beneath the soil, perhaps, something else. She would test it. She would test it properly, with the reagents in her kit, the one she kept in the drawer of her workstation at the far end of the room. The one she had not yet opened. The one she would open, in due course, in the quiet, methodical way that was her nature, and the result would be what it was, and she would accept it, and she would file it in the small, private archive of her own knowledge where certain truths were kept not for the record but for the self.

The blood was the victim's. She knew this. Not from the reagent, not yet, but from the small, private certainty that settled in her chest like a stone dropped into still water, sending out its slow, expanding rings. She knew this the way one knows the season has turned, not by the thermometer but by the quality of the light, by the particular angle of the shadow, by the way the air sits differently against the skin. The blood was the victim's. The soil had been turned here, in this room, by this fountain, by a foot that had pressed down with the full, unthinking weight of a body that was no longer upright, no longer breathing, no longer anything at all.

She remained crouched for a moment longer. The fountain trickled. The grow-lights hummed. The station turned, and the faint inclination to the left pressed against her shoulder, her hip, the small of her back, a gentle, persistent reminder that she was not on the ground, that she was in a metal shell spinning through the void above a red and silent world, that the air in her lungs was recycled, measured, rationed, that the green things around her were alive only because someone, every day, made them so.

She stood. The motion was slow, deliberate, her knees protesting with the small, familiar ache that spoke of years spent bent over seedling trays, years of crouching, of reaching, of the particular, cumulative strain on the joints that no amount of stretching could fully repair. She straightened. She was still, for a moment, facing the fountain, the blood droplet, the turned soil. And then her gaze drifted. Past the fountain. Past the rows of seedlings. To the far wall, where the heavy, sealed door of the gene-seed vault stood, closed, dark, indifferent. A presence. A fact. Behind her, if she turned. She did not turn. The access key hung at her hip, on a short chain, and she could feel it there, cold against her thigh, a small metallic weight that had nothing to do with the blood on the fountain and everything to do with the reason she was here at two in the morning, in a room that belonged to no one and to everyone, in the quiet, chlorophyll-thick dark.

She did not look at the vault. She did not reach for the key. She did not, in any way that the eye could follow or the mind could parse, connect the two facts: the dead thing on the fountain's rim and the living thing behind the sealed door. They were separate. They were, in the architecture of her own careful, compartmentalised mind, entirely, irreconcilably separate. One was a problem to be solved, a data point to be recorded, a small, cold fact in the ledger of the night. The other was a purpose, a need, a quiet and persistent hunger that had been growing in her for three weeks, since the first whispered conversation in the Crew's Mess, since the first name and the first figure and the first, terrible, liberating thought that the seeds she had coaxed from dormancy into fragile, trembling life were worth, in the right hands, at the right price, more than the station's entire quarterly yield.

She knelt again. Not crouched. Kneelt. Both knees to the floor, her palms flat on the cool composite plating, her back curved in a way that was not quite reverence and not quite submission but something in between, something that belonged to the small, private grammar of her own body. She reached for the disturbed soil. Her right hand, the one with the faint dark smudge on the index finger, extended over the turned patch. And she smoothed it. The palm of her hand pressed flat against the loam, and she drew it, slowly, firmly, across the disturbed surface, pressing the loose, pale substrate back into the dark, compacted layer, erasing the impression of the shoe sole, erasing the evidence of the turning, erasing the small, violent fact that someone had stood here, pressed here, broken the surface of the earth in this room, in this hour, in this silence. She smoothed it the way one smooths a child's blanket, the way one tucks in the corner that has lifted, the way one makes the surface even and whole and undisturbed, so that the sleeper does not wake, so that the small, fragile order of the thing is restored.

Her hand moved. The soil settled. The impression faded. The surface was even. The surface was, to any eye that might pass over it in the morning, perfectly, undeniably, naturally flat.

She withdrew her hand. She held it for a moment, palm up, in the amber glow, and she looked at it. The soil-stained fingers. The small, soft hands. The hands that had, that very afternoon, placed a single, perfect seed into a single, perfect groove of prepared earth, and spoken to it, very softly, in the quiet, private language of the one who tends.

And then she whispered. The word was so small, so thin, so barely a sound above the trickle of the fountain and the hum of the lights, that it might have been the water, might have been the rotation, might have been the station itself, turning, turning, turning in the red and silent dark.

"I'm sorry."

She did not say to whom. She did not say for what. The word hung in the thick, green air and dissolved, indistinguishable from the smell of loam, from

***

The click was small. A thumbnail, pressed once against the rim of the sherry glass, producing a sound so faint it might have been imagined, a single dry tick in the vast and breathing silence of the Stakeholders' Salon. Captain Blackwood did not repeat it. He had no need to. The sound had served its purpose, which was to confirm that the glass was still there, still on the table, still bearing the faintest condensation line where the amber liquid had pooled three hours ago and then, slowly, evaporated into the recycled air.

His fingers traced that line. Calloused at the tips, the pads flat and deliberate, the way a man might trace the seam of a cartridge case or the scored edge of a ration tablet. Not a touch.
