The Mining Archive smelled, at this hour, of two things that should not have coexisted: the sharp, electric tang of ozone leaking from the ventilation grilles overhead, and the slow, sweet decay of old varnish on the shelving units that lined the walls in their long, orderly rows. It was the particular silence of a rotating station at its slowest point in the spin cycle, that half-second of weightlessness the gyros produced before the counterweight swung the deck back into its familiar, gentle curve. Dr. Cassius Wren stood in the centre of the room with his back to the mineral display board and his face turned toward the steel filing cabinet in the far corner, and in that silence he could hear nothing but his own respiration, slow and measured, and the faint subsonic hum of the station's rotational engines threading through the hull like a pulse felt in the bones rather than the ears.

His fingers, still enclosed in their starched white cotton gloves, hovered above the cold surface of the cabinet. He did not touch it. He had not touched it. He had been standing precisely where he stood for eleven minutes, he estimated, though the estimation was a geologist's habit more than a clock's reading, calibrated against the slow migration of his own shadow across the floor as the station turned and the amber lamplight shifted its long, thin bands across the polished specimens in their glass cases. The archive was the only lit room on the deck. Beyond the narrow porthole set into the far wall, the void of Martian orbit pressed its absolute darkness against the glass, and the red dust of the colony below was invisible, a fact that seemed to Wren, in that moment, to carry a significance he could not quite articulate. His shadow stretched long and singular across the basalt samples and the folded iron ore cross-sections, and for a time it was the only moving thing in the room.

He had come to review the quarterly yield projections. That was the purpose. That was the purpose he would state, if stated, to anyone who asked, and the words were already arranged in his mind with the clean, frictionless precision of a well-cut diamond: *I was in the archive at half past two, consulting the Q-three ore-yield certificates, cross-referencing the extraction depth logs against the projected tonnage figures for the southern shaft.* A reasonable statement. A geologist's statement. The statement of a man who had spent eleven years cataloguing the mineralogical strata of a world two hundred million kilometres from the one he was born on, and who had no reason, no motive, no particular urgency to be in a locked room at the small hours of the station's rotation. He arranged the words again in his mind, smoothing them, testing their weight, and found them adequate. They would hold. They would hold until they did not.

The cabinet was a standard issue, the kind the Colonial Authority supplied in identical grey units to every research division on every station in the Belt. Four drawers. A locking mechanism of the older type, the kind with a physical key cylinder rather than the electronic keypad, and Wren preferred it for the simple reason that a key could be held, turned, felt in the fingers. A key was a fact. A key left a trace. He reached for the top drawer first, because the top drawer was where the current quarter's assay sheets should be filed, and his thumb found the cold steel of the handle and turned it with the small, practiced rotation that required no more force than a whisper. The drawer slid open.

It was not empty. That was not the problem. The problem was the order.

Wren's mind, even in its agitation, worked in the taxonomy of the rock. He catalogued what he saw with the same unhurried precision he would have applied to a thin section under a petrographic microscope: the filing tabs, the paper stock, the particular shade of ink in the handwritten annotations, the sequence of the sample numbers. And there it was. The filing convention. Not his. He had filed his own assay sheets by extraction date, primary then secondary, a system he had maintained for nine years without variation because it was the system that suited a man who thought in chronological strata. These sheets were filed alphabetically by ore type, haematite to magnetite to siderite, and within each alphabetical grouping, by extraction depth, the deepest samples at the top of the tab, the shallowest at the bottom. A system of cross-reference. A system designed not for retrieval but for comparison. For proving, by the juxtaposition of adjacent entries, that one number did not match the number that should have been there.

He knew the system. He had seen it used before, in a single instance, in a single meeting, when a woman with close-cropped grey hair and a voice like dry sand over glass had laid a sheaf of papers on the table in the Stakeholders' Salon and said, without raising her volume, *I think you will find the haematite figures in the southern shaft do not correspond to the tonnage in the Colonial Authority's public ledger, Dr. Wren.* He had not answered. He had not needed to. He had seen the filing system in the arrangement of those papers, and he had understood, with the cold and absolute certainty of a man who has identified a mineral by its cleavage plane, that the woman in question had been in his files before. Had been in his files, and had found what she was looking for, and had gone away to prepare a presentation for the Interplanetary Trade Council that was now, by his own reckoning, no more than four hours from being delivered.

He closed the top drawer. He opened the second. The third. The fourth. Each one he examined with the same surgical attention, the same unhurried turning of the wrist, the same quiet cataloguing of what was present and what was absent. And in the second drawer, where the original ore-yield certificates should have been, the signed and sealed documents that constituted the legal proof of every tonne of iron ore extracted from the Martian regolith, he found the space where they had been. An empty rectangle in the filing sequence. A gap the exact width and depth of a sheaf of forty-seven documents, their absence a negative shape in the paper, a hole in the record that said, without words, that someone had taken them, or moved them, or was in the process of moving them, and that the process had been interrupted, or completed, or was about to be resumed, and that in any of those three cases, the gap was now a fact that could not be unmade.

He stood very still. The gyro-hum threaded through the soles of his shoes, through the thin soles of his shoes, and into the floor, and the floor into the hull, and the hull into the vast and indifferent machinery of the station's rotation, and he stood in the middle of that machinery with his gloved hands at his sides and his face turned toward the cabinet, and he did not breathe for a count of seven, and then he breathed, and the breath was the same as the one before it, and the one before that, and the one that would follow, because Dr. Cassius Wren did not alter his respiration. He had not altered his respiration in eleven years of geological survey, and he would not begin now, in a small room at the bottom of a rotating station, in the presence of a filing cabinet with a fresh scratch on its lock and a key still in the cylinder, turned to the position of open.

He reached for the second drawer again. His fingers found the handle. His thumb, in the small and inevitable motion of gripping the steel, caught on something that should not have been there. A bend. A slight, almost imperceptible distortion in the edge of the latch mechanism, the kind of bend that could only be produced by pressure applied from the inside of the drawer, by a hand that had reached in and forced the catch back against the direction of its natural spring. He had seen it before. He had seen it in the Captain's Study, in the red circle of the annotation, in the small, patient fact of a mechanism that had been opened and closed by a hand that knew precisely where the catch engaged and precisely how much force was required to release it. He knew the hand. He had seen the hand. He had seen the hand place a sheaf of papers on a table in the Stakeholders' Salon and withdraw it again with the unhurried grace of a woman who had no need to hurry because the truth, once assembled, required no further effort to make it undeniable.

His left hand trembled. Once. A single, fine vibration in the fingers, so brief that it might have been the resonance of the steel cabinet, the sympathetic oscillation of a metal struck at a particular frequency. He corrected it. He withdrew his hand from the drawer handle and set it, flat-palmed, on the desk beside the cabinet, and he stood there for the space of three heartbeats, feeling the vibration die in the cotton of his glove, and then he lifted his hand again, and it was steady, and it was his, and it was the hand of a man who was reviewing quarterly yield projections and had no reason to be in a locked room at the small hours of the station's rotation and no reason to be trembling, and no reason to be afraid.

He removed his left glove. The motion was slow, deliberate, the fingers peeling the cotton from the thumb, the index, the middle, the ring, the little, each one freed with the same small, precise attention he would have given to the separation of a mineral vein from its matrix. He set the glove on the desk. Flat. The palm facing up, the fingers aligned, the thumb at a slight angle, as though the hand it had

***

The smell reached him before the sight did. It was the smell of turned earth, wet and faintly sweet, the particular loamy exhalation of soil that has been disturbed within the last hour, and it filled the sealed bay with a thickness that had nothing to do with the station's recycled air. Above, the ultraviolet grow-lights hummed their low, sixty-hertz drone, and the light they cast was not light at all but a kind of chlorophyll-green phosphorescence, the colour of a memory one has almost forgotten, the colour of a fever dream in which everything is slightly too saturated, slightly too alive. In that green wash, Captain Theron Blackwood's face was the face of a man asleep, and yet his fingers were already in the dirt.

He was kneeling. His shoulders were level. His spine was a straight line from the base of his collar to the crown of his head, the architecture of command maintained even in a posture that had no business being maintained by a man on his knees in a garden. Sector Four, row nine, no anomalies. The thought arrived clipped, procedural, the kind of notation one would make in a logbook and then move past. But his eyes were not scanning for anomalies. His eyes were measuring. They tracked the root structure of the dwarf tomato vine, not its leaves, not its fruit, but the dense tangle of white filaments that threaded the upper two inches of the engineered substrate, and they were measuring the precise depth at which a small object, no larger than a thumbnail, would settle into the loam and remain invisible to any casual glance.

He pushed the roots aside. Not roughly. There was a quality to the motion that was almost tender, the careful parting of a curtain, the lifting of a tablecloth to inspect the surface beneath. He pressed his index finger into the soil and withdrew it, then pressed it again, a half-inch deeper this time, and held it there. The rhythm of his breathing was even, regular, the metronomic cadence of a man who had decided, some time before this moment, that his breathing would be even and regular, and who was now enforcing that decision upon his own lungs. It was not calm. It was control.

He tested the depth a second time. Then a third. The specificity of the attention was the tell, though in this empty bay there was no one to whom it might be a tell. He was not discovering the condition of the soil. He was performing the discovery, shaping it, giving it the particular texture of a finding that would surface later, organically, in the morning briefing, in the record, in the eyes of a board of directors who would nod and say, yes, of course, the captain found it, the captain is thorough, the captain is the one who sees. And the file name that floated through his mind for a single, suppressed instant was not a name at all but a designation, a string of letters and numbers in a column he had memorized, the kind of reference one makes to a drawer in a cabinet, not to a person one has ever called by their Christian name.

He withdrew his hand. He brushed the dark soil from his knuckles with a dry, deliberate stroke, the way one might clear a smudge from a ledger page. Then he replaced the vine, guiding the roots back into their tangle with a precision that was, if anything, more careful than the initial parting. The grow-light above him flickered. A single half-second of darkness, a minor electrical hiccup in the station's grid, and in that half-second his jaw tightened, a small muscular contraction no one would have seen, no one would ever see.

The light steadied. The green phosphorescence resumed its slow, suffocating saturation. He straightened. He squared his shoulders into the full, unapologetic architecture of command, and the loam crumbled from his knees in fine, dark threads that hung in the still air like the last notes of a held breath.

"Noted," he said to the empty bay, to the tomato vine, to the hum of the lights, to no one at all. "No irregularities."

The word settled into the chlorophyll-thick silence and did not dissolve. It stayed. It stayed the way a seed stays in turned earth, waiting, patient, for the particular hour when it is meant to be found.

The glass was still warm.

That was the first thing one noticed, standing at the curved railing of the Grand Rotunda where the station's ambient panels cast their dim, power-conserving amber across the polished floor. A crystal tumbler, half-filled with a burgundy residue that had begun to oxidise at its edges, sat as though someone had set it down only moments before and walked away in a hurry. The lip of the glass bore a thin ring of wine-dark stain, and if one brought it close enough to the nose, there was a faint sweetness to it, a cloying trace that spoke of a vintage far beyond what the station's stores would normally permit. And there, wound around the brass fitting of the railing with the casual permanence of a stray thread caught on a button, a single strand of black hair. No longer than a finger. Not belonging, by any visible account, to the person whose glass it was.

The rotunda was vast. Curved. The kind of large, circular room that feels empty in a particular way when the people in it are not, in any meaningful sense, talking to one another. The air recyclers hummed at a frequency just below the threshold of conscious hearing, a low mechanical drone that one felt in the teeth more than in the ears. The lights had been dimmed. Everything was a shade darker than it should have been at three in the morning, as though the station itself were conserving not merely power but the will to look at what the light might reveal.

Felix Drummond arrived in the room the way a weather system arrives: all at once, and with a pressure that made the air shift. His cologne was too much. It was always too much. A thick, sweet, over-hydrated perfume that announced him three paces before his body did, a perfumed vanguard of a man who had not yet decided what he wanted to say. His right hand was already extended, fingers slightly spread, the gesture of a handshake offered to a room that had not requested one, when his eyes found Sera Okafor standing near the railing with her notebook closed and her posture arranged in the particular slouch of a woman who has been listening for twenty minutes and is now, deliberately, no longer listening.

"Okafor." He said it the way one says a word one has been rehearsing in a mirror. "Okafor, I need to speak with you. Quietly. I have noticed something." His hand was still extended. His other hand had found his left cuff and was adjusting it, though the cuff was already in perfect order. "Something about the financial reports. The quarterly projections. They don't add up."

Sera leaned in. A fraction. The physical on-switch, the slight forward tilt of the jaw that told the speaker she was now, and only now, receiving.

"Go on," she said.

Felix Drummond, Senior Stakeholder of Drummond-Voss Capital Partners, a man who had sat in boardrooms on three continents and negotiated the terms of a Martian mining colony, leaned in a full two inches closer and dropped his voice to a register that was less a whisper than a leak. "I think someone is trying to manipulate the numbers. The dividend allocations. The assay certifications. They don't reconcile. I have checked them four times, and they do not reconcile, and I do not understand how a man in my position, with access to the primary ledgers, should not understand, and yet I do not, and that is what frightens me." His hands were wringing now, the right one twisting the left, the fingers working the skin at the knuckles as though the cuff were a rope he might need to climb. "It is not a rounding error. It is not a clerical oversight. The numbers have been *moved*, Okafor. Deliberately. Structurally."

Sera leaned back. The off-switch. Her eyes did not leave his face, but something behind them was cataloguing, filing, assigning a value to the particular frequency of his tremor. The way his Adam's apple worked. The way his gaze kept flicking to the railing, to the glass, to the hair, and then immediately away, as though the room had collectively agreed to look at the wall instead. She noted the cologne. She noted the handshake that no one had requested. She noted the way his left eye twitched in a small, involuntary spasm that he did not seem to register. She filed it all with the same clinical detachment she applied to a byline, the same careful arithmetic of a woman who understood that a man's nervousness, properly quantified, was a currency more liquid than any dividend.

"Manipulated numbers," she repeated. Not a question. An echo. A small, precise stone dropped into still water.

Felix nodded. His hands found his collar and adjusted it, though it was not askew. "Yes. Yes, exactly. The column on page forty-three of the Ashworth Consortium's projection sheet, the one with the ore-yield certifications, the totals are off by a margin that should not exist in a sealed system, and I cannot—" He stopped. Swallowed. Started again. "I cannot determine who is doing it. I cannot determine whether it is being done *to* me or *through* me. And that, Okafor, is the part that I cannot sleep over."

Dr. Cassius Wren's hand, which had been reaching toward the star chart pinned to the bulkhead, froze in mid-air. He did not turn. He did not need to. The geologist's posture. The lecturer's posture. In the Grand Rotunda, three corridors away and separated from this conversation by a wall of reinforced aluminium and the constant low thrum of the station's atmospheric processors, Captain Theron Blackwood stood with his back to the leather armchair, facing Sera Okafor, who stood at a precise distance of two paces, her journalist's notebook closed in her left hand, her right hand resting on the brass railing. Sera nodded. She did not write anything down. She had already written down everything that mattered, in the particular shorthand of a woman who had spent three years embedded in the Interplanetary Press and had learned that the most dangerous information was the information a reader believed was being told to them.

"And what of the Consortium's internal transfers, Mrs. Ashworth? The quarterly remittances to the Earth-side accounts. I raise it because a geologist, examining a deposit, must understand the full provenance of the ore. The context. The provenance."

Margaret's eyes drifted, for the space of half a second, toward the porthole where Mars hung in the black. A micro-hesitation. A calibration. The pause of a woman selecting, from a shelf of prepared statements, the one that would land with the precise force she required. The left hand was the first thing Elara Voss noticed. Not the words—never the words, for words were the cheapest currency a man could spend, and Captain Blackwood was, by all appearances, a man who had spent a great many of them in his time. It was the hand. The fingers were steady. Trained. The kind of steady that came not from calm but from a long, disciplined war against the tremor of uncertainty. And yet the hand moved with a purpose his mouth was not authorizing.

It was half past three in the station's local reckoning, and the Grand Rotunda wore the dim, blood-amber glow of the Mars-facing observation ring like a bruise that would not fade. "Nine days is irrelevant." He said it the way one might say *the weather is adequate*. Flat. Dismissive. A sentence no longer than three words, delivered with the clipped finality of a man sealing an envelope.

Elara Voss's pen clicked. A small, dry sound, the cap engaging with the barrel. She did not look up from her ledger. She did not need to. The sound was for her own benefit, a small mechanical acknowledgment that the moment had registered, that it had been filed.

Blackwood's right hand moved to the armchair's base. He pressed his palm flat against the wood, feeling for it—what? A screw loose? A panel displaced? His fingers splayed, then drew together, then splayed again. The motion was methodical. The motion was also, Elara Voss noted with the quiet precision of a woman who had spent twenty years reading rooms, entirely unnecessary for a man inspecting a piece of furniture for signs of forced entry. One did not kneel before an armchair in a sealed, pressurized rotunda and press one's palm to its base as though expecting to feel a hidden latch. One did not do that unless one was looking for something specific. Something small. Something that had been placed there, deliberately, by a hand one intended to point at later.

"The numbers I am referencing," Sera continued, and her tone had shifted, just a fraction, from conversational to something closer to the patient recitation of a woman reading a menu she had already decided upon, "are not, strictly speaking, mine to be discussing. I am a journalist. I report what I am shown. But I am also, as I am sure you are aware, a woman who has read the station's internal correspondence with a degree of thoroughness that might, in a less charitable light, be described as *thorough*. And in that correspondence, Captain, there is a certain... discrepancy. A gap between what was reported to the shareholders and what was, in fact, extracted from the Martian regolith. A gap that grows rather wider each quarter. And I confess I find myself wondering whether that gap is a question of arithmetic or a question of *intent*."

Blackwood's jaw worked. A small, almost invisible tightening of the masseter, the kind of micro-expression that a man who had spent a decade in command would normally suppress and failed, in this instance, to suppress entirely. His left hand had moved. It was beneath the cushion now. Elara Voss saw the fabric lift, just a fraction, just enough to catch the amber light from the observation ring and throw a small, distorted shadow across the armchair's arm. The cushion was displaced. Not dramatically. Not in a way that would be visible to a casual observer. But it was displaced, and the angle of the displacement was wrong. It had been pushed aside from the left, not the right, and the crease in the upholstery ran in a direction that suggested it had been moved recently. Very recently. By a hand that knew exactly where to press.

"Intent," Blackwood repeated. The word came out flat, stripped of inflection, as though he were testing its weight in his mouth before deciding whether to swallow it. "You are speculating."

"I am *observing*," Sera corrected, and the gentleness of the correction was more dangerous than any accusation. She was still standing. She had not sat. She had not shifted her weight. She was, in every visible respect, relaxed. Her arms were loose at her sides. Her expression was that of a woman watching a particularly interesting insect navigate a maze. "There is a difference, Captain. A speculator guesses. An observer notes. And what I note is that the numbers do not add up, and that the person who arranged them for this station's benefit is, I suspect, not the person who will be found to have arranged them when the inquiry is formalized. And I note, further, that you are kneeling before an armchair in the middle of the Rotunda at half past three in the morning, pressing your fingers into the upholstery as though you expect it to yield a confession."

A silence. The air recycler hummed. The status panels ticked. Somewhere in the station's deeper architecture, a pipe contracted in the cold and let out a single, thin ping that traveled through the floor and up through the soles of one's shoes.

"I am conducting an inspection," Blackwood said.

"Of the armchair."

"Of the area.

***

The hour was four, and the station turned.

It was not a sound one could isolate, not a noise that announced itself to the ear. It lived lower than that, in the architecture of the floor, in the marble-veneer panels that had been laid with such confidence in the salon's original fitting-out that one had long since stopped noticing them. But at four in the morning, with the other occupants of the Ares Meridian asleep in their bunks or their cabins, with the ventilation system dropped to its nocturnal whisper and the Mars-simulation panels dimmed to their lowest amber register, the hum became almost audible. A subliminal vibration, felt more in the teeth and the fingertips than in the middle ear, the slow and eternal rotation of the great axis that kept the gravity at a steady one-point-two and the stars in their fixed, indifferent arcs beyond the glass.

And in that amber, half-light, the Stakeholders' Salon lay empty. The armchairs with their wine-dark velvet upholstery sat in their prescribed positions, the brass railings caught the glow and threw it back in long, still shadows. The faint scent of synthetic sandalwood drifted from the air recyclers set into the ceiling panels, that inoffensive, expensive perfume the Consortium had installed to make the space smell of a London drawing room rather than a pressurised tube in the void. The decorative sculptures of Martian ore, cast in a pale terracotta composite, stood on their plinths with the patient solemnity of objects that had never once been moved. Everything was in its place. Everything was, or appeared to be, in its place.

And then there were the hands.

They were bare. This was, in itself, a small and extraordinary fact. Margaret Ashworth did not go bare-handed. Her fingers were, in the waking hours, perpetually ringed, perpetually gloved in kid leather the colour of pale cream, perpetually still in their posture of mild, aristocratic disinterest while the world fidgeted and stammered around her. One observed her hands at a board meeting and saw only the slow, deliberate tap of a ring against a glass, the unhurried turning of a page. One did not see the knuckles. One did not see the tendons.

Now the gloves were off. Now the rings were off, set on the sideboard with a soft, precise click that spoke of a woman who had removed them with the same methodical care she would have applied to uncapping a fountain pen. And the hands were in the wood. The right hand pressed flat against the lower panel of the sideboard, fingertips spread, feeling the grain. Not touching. Feeling. The left hand followed, trailing along the seam where the panel met the baseboard, searching for a seam that should not be there, a false bottom, a drawer that the furniture catalogue would never have listed. Her breath was controlled. One could tell it was controlled because it was slightly too fast, the in-and-out a fraction more urgent than the rhythm of a woman who had never in her life needed to be anything other than in command.

She was crouched. The word was, for Margaret Ashworth, a small violence. Her spine remained straight, the line from the crown of her head to the small of her back unbroken, as though the geometry of her posture were a matter of personal law. But the angle of her knees, the slight forward lean of her shoulders, the way her jaw was set so that the muscles at her temples worked in slow, visible pulses beneath the skin—these were the betrayals. The body's small, unavoidable confessions. She was doing the thing she could not do in daylight. She was doing the thing that, if any of the other stakeholders were to see her doing it, would crack the decade of careful authority she had built into a thousand small, manageable pieces. In daylight she sat at the head of the table. In daylight she signed documents with a steady hand and a steady voice. At four in the morning, in the amber half-dark, she ran her bare fingers along the baseboard of a room designed to impress investors and felt, for the first time in months, the cold, granular texture of the composite material against her skin and the faint, gritty residue of something that was not quite dust.

She moved. Not quickly. Never quickly. Margaret Ashworth did not hurry; hurrying implied that the outcome was uncertain, and the outcome was never uncertain in the matters that concerned her. She rose from the sideboard with the unhurried grace of a woman who had spent forty years learning that the world was watching, even when it was not. She crossed the salon in six measured steps, her shoes making no sound on the alternating walnut and birch floor, and paused before the Persian carpet that lay beneath the central console. The hem of the carpet was weighted with brass strips at its corners. She lifted the hem. Not dramatically. Not with the desperate, clawing urgency of a woman who has seen a ghost. She lifted it the way one lifts a page, with the thumb and forefinger, and looked beneath.

Nothing. A faint smear of the red Martian dust that had, she knew, been tracked in from the airlock in the first weeks of the station's habitation and never fully cleaned. She let the carpet fall. She turned.

The Mars-ore sculptures. Three of them, on their plinths, representing the iron, the copper, and the rare-earth deposits that had brought the stakeholders to this station and, she suspected, to this particular murder. She ran her fingers along the base of the first, then the second. The terracotta composite was smooth, seamless, professional. The third she turned, slowly, feeling the contact point where it met the plinth. There was a gap. Not a large one. A sliver, perhaps the width of a thumbnail, where the sculpture's base did not sit flush against the plinth's surface. Her fingers found it. They pressed into the gap. They found, behind the sculpture, a space no deeper than two inches. Empty. She withdrew her hand.

She was breathing now in a way that was not quite controlled. The in-and-out had quickened by a degree, and she could feel the heat of it in the small of her back, where the fabric of her coat had grown damp. She would not permit herself to be seen in this state. She would not permit herself to be seen at all. The suspicion that had driven her here, into this room, into this crouching and feeling and the slow, humiliating inventory of every surface, was not a suspicion she could articulate in a boardroom. It was the quiet, corrosive knowledge that the data she possessed—the numbers, the percentages, the elegant little architecture of the falsified assays—was not enough. Data could be challenged. Data could be argued, cross-examined, buried under a avalanche of technical objections from lawyers who had never set foot on a mining shaft. What she needed was the thing in the envelope. The physical, admissible, unarguable object that would survive the legal machinery of the other stakeholders and the quiet, patient pressure of the Colonial Authority. The object that said, in the language of the court, not in the language of the spreadsheet, that the numbers were lies.

She moved to the central console. The brass and dark wood, the same furniture that had hosted the quarterly briefings, the stakeholder dinners, the small performances of consensus that she had orchestrated for ten years. She placed both hands on its lower surface and felt the wood with the pads of her fingers, searching for the seam, the joint, the place where the console's interior did not match its exterior. Her knuckles whitened. She could see them whitening in the amber light, the tendons standing out like wires beneath the skin. She pressed. She felt. She moved her hands along the panel in a slow, deliberate sweep, the way one reads a page, line by line, not missing a word.

And there.

Behind the console. Not in a drawer. Not in a compartment. Behind the console itself, in the narrow gap between its rear panel and the wall, where the air recycler's duct met the station's hull. Her fingers found it in the dark, in the cold, in the narrow space where the air tasted of recycled oxygen and the faint, metallic tang of the composite wall. A slim object. A rectangle. The weight of it was wrong for a loose sheet of paper, right for a sealed envelope. The wax. She could feel the wax before she saw it, the slight raised texture of the seal, the particular thickness that meant it had been pressed with a proper stamp, not a hasty thumb.

She drew it out. It was slim. It was sealed. And the crest pressed into the wax was not the Voss Mining Consortium. It was not the Ashworth Mining Consortium. It was the Interplanetary Trade Council, the slow, bureaucratic, infinitely patient institution that had, for three years, been watching the colony's output figures with the detached interest of a man watching a river he suspected was being dammed. The crest was small, precise, official. The kind of seal that meant the document inside had passed through at least four layers of institutional review before it reached this station.

Margaret Ashworth held the envelope in her bare hands. She did not open it. She turned it over once, feeling the weight of the contents, the slight give that suggested more than a single sheet. She felt, in the small of her back, the damp patch where her coat had grown warm, and she thought, with the detached precision of a woman conducting a post-mortem on her own composure, that she had spent the last three years trying to outmanoeuvre this institution. She had spent the last three years feeding it carefully selected numbers, steering its inquiries, ensuring that its slow, grinding machinery pointed in the direction she required. And here it was. Here, in the gap behind a console in a room designed to make investors feel important, was the physical evidence that it had been working. That it had been working, perhaps, without her. That her entire secret architecture, the careful, private, midnight strategy of a woman who could not afford to be seen searching, might be redundant. Or worse. It might be a step behind.

She slid the envelope into the inner pocket of her coat. The motion was precise. Unhurried. The motion of a woman filing a document, tucking a receipt into a ledger, closing a book. There was no triumph in it. No relief. Only the small, cold satisfaction of a task completed, and the larger, colder question of what came next.

She straightened. She smoothed her skirt. She turned, and walked toward the door.

The viewport was dark, or nearly so. The Mars-simulation panels had dimmed to their lowest register, and beyond the glass, the actual Mars hung in the black. Not the warm, rust-coloured disc of the simulation. The real thing. Smaller. Colder. A fixed, indifferent eye, watching the station turn, watching the small figures within, watching the woman in the wine-dark coat walk across the salon with her hands clasped behind her back and her spine straight and her face, in the faint reflection caught in the darkened glass, utterly, absolutely unreadable.

The hum went on. The station turned. The Mars watched.

And in the inner pocket of her coat, the sealed envelope lay against her ribs, warm with the heat of her body, waiting.

***

The smell reached her before the sight did. Wet loam, yes, and the sweet green exhalation of crushed mint from the bordering planter, and beneath both, the faint mineral tang of the fountain's recirculating water — that particular iron-and-limestone flavour she had come to associate with the Conservatory's early hours, when the grow-lamps dimmed to their 04:15 cycle and the room settled into its slow, vegetative breathing. She had known the smell. She had known it for three weeks, the way one knows the particular creak of a door that has not been oiled in a year. It was, in its way, a comfort.

It was the wrongness that undid her.

A single drop of blood. Rust-brown, tacky, sitting on the pale limestone lip of the central fountain like a seed that had no intention of growing. Not a spatter. Not a smear. One precise, rounded bead, partially dissolved where the trickling water had begun its slow work of dilution, its edges softening into the pale stone. Dr. Yuki Tanaka stood before it with her hands still damp from her soil-pH test, the green rubber gloves clinging to her fingers, and she did not touch it. Not yet. Her knuckles were white where they pressed against the fountain's rim — not from the cold, for the Conservatory held its temperature at a steady twenty-two degrees, but from the particular, quiet shock of a woman who has found a thing she did not wish to find.

The room hummed around her. The misting nozzles above the orchid beds ticked in their slow, rhythmic cycle, each pulse releasing a fine green-tinted spray that caught the low phosphorescence of the grow-lamps and scattered it into a hundred tiny rainbows. Somewhere deeper in the soil beds, a stem was growing — she could hear it, that faint creak of cellulose stretching, the sound a plant makes when it is adding a millimetre to its height in the dark. The water in the fountain murmured against its limestone channel. And she was the only living thing in the room besides the plants. The only breathing thing. The only one whose heartbeat thudded in her ears like a second, faster pulse.

She knelt. This was a detail she would remember later, or perhaps not remember at all, the way one does not remember the precise angle of one's knees when the ground has suddenly become very important. Her gloved fingertips hovered a centimetre above the blood droplet, and she studied it the way she studied a leaf with an irregular vein — clinically, without sentiment, cataloguing what was there before deciding what it meant. One drop. The diameter of a lentil. The colour of dried iron. It had not been splashed; it had been placed, or pressed, or allowed to fall from a height of perhaps two centimetres. The person who bled it had been close to the fountain. Very close. Close enough to feel the coolness of the stone on their fingertips. Close enough to be cut, or to bleed deliberately, at the very edge of the water.

Her eyes moved. Past the droplet, along the fountain's rim, to the section of engineered soil where the gene-seed plots waited in their numbered trays, three weeks' planning pressed into the precise geometry of the arrangement. And there — there was the disturbance. A section of the substrate, perhaps the width of her palm, had been turned over. The dark, compressed loam lay in loose clumps where it should have been level, and in the centre of the disturbance, half-hidden beneath a flap of torn mycelium, was the faint impression of a shoe sole. Not a boot. A shoe. The tread was shallow, the pattern a simple herringbone. She had seen that tread before. She could not, in this moment, say whose foot had worn it.

Her hands trembled. Not from fear — she would not permit herself the luxury of fear, not here, not in the green silence where the misting nozzles ticked and the orchids breathed their slow chlorophyll breath. It was recognition. The terrible, quiet recognition of a woman who has spent three weeks planning, in the precise and methodical way of her profession, to lift the gene-seed samples from the vault at the Conservatory's far end. Three weeks of soil-pH tests conducted at hours no legitimate botanist would choose. Three weeks of mapping the misting cycle, of memorising the grow-lamp dimming schedule, of standing in this very room at 04:15 with her gloves on and her field notes in her coat pocket, measuring the distance to the vault with the same careful attention she gave to a root system. And now the soil was disturbed. And the blood was on the stone. And someone had already been here. Someone had already tried to reach the trays. And they had left their blood on the fountain like a signature.

Behind her, the vault waited in the green half-shadow, its steel door sealed, its contents arranged in the order she had planned to rearrange. She did not turn to look at it. She did not need to. She knew where it was. She knew the exact number of steps from the fountain to its threshold. She had counted them eleven times.

Her gloved fingertip brushed the disturbed soil, lifting a clump of dark substrate to examine its texture. Compressed. Recent. The mycelium had not yet begun to re-weave its threads. Whoever had turned this earth had done it within the last hour. Perhaps within the last twenty minutes. Her brush caught something half-buried beneath the loose clumps — a strip of material, torn, no longer than her index finger. She lifted it. The proprietary gene-seed packaging. The rival corporation's watermark was faintly visible in the green-tinged lamplight, a ghostly interlocking of letters she had memorised three weeks ago when she first opened the vault's inventory manifest and understood, with the cold clarity of a diagnosis, what the samples were worth and who else wanted them.

She stared at the strip. The misting nozzles ticked. The water murmured. Her breath stopped in her chest for a count of three, four, five, and then resumed, shallow and quick, the way one breathes when one has stepped into a room where the air has been taken from it.

The implication settled over her like the green spray, cool and all-encompassing. Someone else. Someone who had come here, to this room, in this hour, with the same purpose she had nursed for three weeks in the privacy of her own calculations. And they had been hurt. Or they had hurt themselves. Or someone had hurt them. The blood was singular, precise, intimate. Not the spray of a wound in flight. The careful, close bleed of a person who was standing still, who was reaching for something specific, who was cut at the very moment of their reaching. The crime, if crime it was, had been small and targeted. A person, a fountain, a drop of blood. The precision of it was what undid her, more than the blood itself.

She folded the packaging strip into her palm. The green rubber glove made the gesture mechanical, clinical, the way one folds a specimen label. She tucked it into the inner pocket of her lab coat, where it pressed against her ribs like a second heartbeat. She did not bag it. She did not call for the Inspector. She did not, in this moment, reach for the communication panel mounted on the Conservatory's far wall, though she could see its small red indicator light glowing in the dim, steady and patient as a lighthouse. She simply stood, slowly, her knees aching in the particular way that comes from kneeling on the Conservatory's soft substrate for longer than one intends, and she looked at the blood droplet one final time.

It was almost gone now. The trickling water had carried most of it into the fountain's channel, where it swirled in a thin, rust-coloured thread before disappearing into the recirculation system. In another minute, perhaps two, there would be nothing. No evidence. No drop. No proof that anyone had stood here, at this hour, with their hand bleeding on the pale stone. The water would do its work. The misting nozzles would do their work. The green would swallow the red, as it swallowed everything else in this room.

She turned, slowly, and walked the eleven steps to the vault. She stood before its steel door for a moment, her reflection ghostly in the polished surface, her lab coat's collar turned up against a chill that was not there. Then she turned back, walked the eleven steps to the fountain, and knelt again. Her gloved hand hovered over the soil. The misting nozzles ticked. The orchids breathed.

"Not yet," she whispered, to no one, to the plants, to the water, to the eleven steps between the fountain and the vault. "Not while you're still bleeding."

The misting nozzles cycled on, and the fine green spray rose in the low phosphorescent light, and the last faint trace of iron in the water was swallowed by the green, and the Conservatory breathed, and breathed, and breathed, and the only sound in the room was the slow, patient growing of things that did not need to be found.

***

The sound was small. A soft, metallic click, no louder than the settling of a joint in a cold morning, but in the pressurized silence of the Captain's Study at half past four in the morning, it carried the weight of a verdict. The drawer latch had yielded. Not to force, not to the brute application of a shoulder or a wedge, but to something finer, something that required a steady hand and a patience that Margaret Ashworth had cultivated over thirty years of sitting across from men who believed they were the cleverest people in the room. The drawer opened perhaps two inches, then the rest, and the scent that rose from within was the particular mustiness of paper stored in a sealed environment—dry, faintly sweet, carrying the ghost of ink and the less ghostly trace of the Captain's cologne, which was not, Margaret noted with the detached precision of a woman cataloguing a crime scene, a cologne one would expect to find in the study of a man who claimed to have spent the previous evening in the Grand Rotunda conducting what he had termed, in Sera Okafor's presence, an inspection of the area.

She did not lean in. She did not snatch the papers to her chest as a thief might. Margaret Ashworth, Chairwoman of the Ashworth Mining Consortium, woman who had not raised her voice in a boardroom since 2031, stood perfectly still for three full seconds, letting the drawer settle, letting the air in the study adjust to the small displacement of air that the opening had created. The station hummed beneath her feet—that low, subsonic thrum of the rotation, the slow turning of the great ring around its axis, a sound so constant that one ceased to hear it and felt it only in the bones of the inner ear, in the faint pressure behind the eyes. The recycled air carried its usual metallic tang, a whisper of ozone and processed copper, and beyond the dark viewport behind the desk, Mars filled the glass with its patient, rust-coloured glow, a colour that was not quite red and not quite orange, a colour that had no true name in any terrestrial language because no terrestrial eye had ever been meant to take it in for so long, so unbroken, that the retina began to doubt its own testimony.

In the viewport's dark surface, she could see herself. Not the woman who addressed the Interplanetary Trade Council in her tailored charcoal suit, hair swept into a severe bun, fingers steepled above the mahogany. This was a smaller creature. A woman leaning close, lips slightly parted, reading by the amber pool of a single desk lamp while the rest of the world was the slow bleed of Martian red. Her eyes were very bright. Not with emotion. With the particular brightness of a surgeon who has found the vein she was looking for and now must decide, in the space of a breath, whether to cut or to cauterise.

"Well, Captain," she said, and her voice was dry, appraising, the tone one might use to comment on a slightly undercooked soufflé at a dinner party. "You have been keeping your little accounts."

The drawer contained three items. A leather-bound ledger, its cover worn soft at the corners, its spine cracked from frequent use. A smaller notebook, soft-backed, its pages dense with a hand that was not the Captain's—neat, angular, the looping of a left-handed writer. And beneath these, a sheaf of printed statements, their edges soft with handling, the stamps of the Colonial Authority faded to a ghost of blue. Margaret's fingers, bare, ungloved, traced the topmost entry of the ledger with the careful, deliberate pressure of a woman reading a letter she suspects has been written by someone she knows. The entries were not, she noted, in the Captain's hand. They were in the angular, left-handed script. They were dated. They were specific in their vagueness—the precise quality of a document designed to be understood by its intended reader and to mean nothing to anyone else. A sum. A date. A reference to a market adjustment in the iron-oxide futures. A second sum. A second date. A reference to the colony's quarterly yield projection. The numbers did not need to be itemised for Margaret to understand their architecture. She had built that architecture. She had sat in the trading desk in Geneva, in the old days, and watched those very futures shift in the narrow window between the colony's reported figures and the figures that the market, in its cold and rational way, actually priced. Someone had been feeding the market. Someone had been looking the other way. And the hand that wrote these entries was not the hand of a man who was looking the other way. It was the hand of a man who was steering the other way's gaze.

She turned a page. Then another. Her breathing did not change. This was the critical point, and she knew it. A woman who gasps, who presses a hand to her mouth, who feels the ground tilt beneath her feet—such a woman has been compromised. The moment of discovery is a moment of vulnerability, and Margaret Ashworth had not, in thirty years of navigating the treacherous shallows of corporate governance, ever permitted herself vulnerability in a room that did not belong to her. She read. She noted. The angular handwriting. The market adjustments. The dates that corresponded, with an accuracy that was either remarkable or damning, to the very fluctuations she had intended to weaponise before the Trade Council. The falsified mineral reports she had been hunting for in the Stakeholders' Salon two hours ago, crawling on her hands and knees with the dignity of a woman who had not yet decided whether she was a detective or a thief—those reports, those beautiful, damning lies, were not merely lies. They were the visible tip of a mechanism whose gears were turning in this drawer, in this study, in the locked and private space of a man who had, in Sera Okafor's presence, knelt before an armchair and declared himself to be conducting an inspection of the area.

And here was the thought that the desk lamp did not illuminate, the thought that sat in the dark beyond the pool of amber and would not be moved: the angular, left-handed script. The market adjustments that corresponded to the Ashworth Consortium's trading desk. The sums that, if traced back through the Colonial Authority's stamp, would lead not merely to the Captain's office but to the very accounts from which Margaret's own consolidation scheme drew its oxygen. The evidence in this drawer was a blade with two edges, and she was holding it by the middle, and the question was not whether she would cut. The question was who would bleed.

She was quiet for a long time. The station turned. The hum pressed against the soles of her shoes, a vibration so low it was more felt than heard, a reminder that she was suspended in the void, that the red glow beyond the glass was not a sunset but a planet, patient and indifferent, turning on its axis while the small, warm machinery of human ambition ground and shifted in the sealed rooms above it. Somewhere in the Observatorium, she knew, or rather she guessed, the telescope was being inspected. Somewhere in the Hydroponic Conservatory, the misting nozzles were cycling, and the green was growing, and the blood on the fountain's rim was being swallowed by the water. She did not think about these things. She thought about the ledger in her hands. She thought about the sealed envelope against her ribs, warm with the heat of her body, containing correspondence wax-stamped with the Colonial Authority seal, correspondence that, in the right hands, would revoke the station's mining licence and consolidate the colony under a single, profitable, and entirely Ashworth-managed administration. She thought about how the ledger in this drawer, if produced before the Trade Council, would not merely support that revocation. It would make it inevitable. And it would, simultaneously, make her the woman who had broken into the Captain's private study at half past four in the morning to steal his secrets.

Margaret Ashworth closed the ledger. The sound it made was a soft, deliberate snap, the sound of a book's spine releasing its tension, a small and almost tender sound, like the closing of a hand over a sleeping child's eyes. She replaced it in the drawer. She replaced the smaller notebook. She smoothed the sheaf of printed statements flat, aligning the edges with the precision of a woman who had, in a previous life, been a clerk in a solicitor's office in the City, before the City had become a memory and the solicitor's office had become a footnote in a very long and very successful career. She closed the drawer. The latch clicked. She reached into the inner pocket of her coat, withdrew the thin wire—she did not know where she had acquired it, or perhaps she did, and the not-knowing was a form of comfort she permitted herself in moments of professional necessity—and she turned the lock again. The click. The silence. The hum.

She straightened. She adjusted the single strand of hair that had escaped her bun, tucking it back with two fingers, a small and private gesture, the kind one performs in a mirror but which, in the absence of a mirror, one performs for the benefit of one's own dignity. The desk lamp threw her shadow long and thin across the alternating walnut and birch floor, a shadow that was not quite her shape, not quite her size, a shadow that belonged to the woman in the viewport's reflection, the woman who was leaning close, who was reading, who was deciding.

"The question is not who falsified the reports," she said. Her voice was quiet. Not a whisper. A statement, made to the empty room, to the red glow of Mars, to the sealed envelope against her ribs, to the intercom on the desk that she had not yet checked and would not check, because to check it would be to admit that she was afraid of being heard, and Margaret Ashworth was not afraid of being heard. She was afraid of being understood. "The question is who was paid to look the other way."

She reached out and switched off the lamp. The amber pool collapsed into a point and then into nothing, and the study was red, and the study was dark, and the only light was the slow, patient, indifferent glow of a planet that had been watching, for a very long time, the small and warm machinery of human ambition, and had never once

***

The smear was the size of a thumbnail, rust-red, sitting on the brass mount of the great refractor with the casual persistence of a bruise. It had no business being there. The Observatorium was sealed, hermetically so, every panel gasketed and every airlock cycled through three filtration stages before a breath of external atmosphere could so much as whisper against the glass. And yet there it was, that particular shade of iron-oxide red that belonged to no pigment in the station's inventory, no compound in the hydroponic solutions, no residue from the crew's daily routines. Dr. Cassius Wren's thumb found it by accident, or by the precise calibration of a man who had rehearsed the motion in the dark of his cabin while the rest of the station slept. He brushed it. The powder smeared beneath his nail, warm and granular, and he withdrew his finger as though the brass had been heated to a temperature that could not, in a sealed environment, exist. He did not look at the smear again. He did not mention it. His other hand, the one that had not touched the red, slid a folded slip of paper into the narrow crevice beneath the eyepiece ring with the unhurried competence of a man threading a needle by lamplight. The note was addressed, in a hand that was not his, to Margaret Ashworth.

She stood three paces away, arms folded across her chest, watching him with the particular patience of a woman who has already read the letter and is merely waiting for the sender to finish writing it. Her head tilted, a fraction, as the note settled into its hiding place. Her eyes narrowed by a degree so slight that anyone else in the room would have attributed it to the overhead lighting. It was not the lighting. Margaret Ashworth did not tilt her head. She narrowed her eyes. And then she was still again, and the stillness was the most dangerous thing in the Observatorium, more so than the telescope, more so than the red dust, more so than the two men who would, in the next thirty minutes, circle the same brass instrument from opposite directions and never once suspect that they were circling it for the same reason, in service of opposite verdicts.

Captain Theron Blackwood entered at the half-minute mark, not quite a minute after Wren, with the same set jaw and the same clipped, economical movement through the air that he had employed, three hours ago, in the Grand Rotunda, when he had knelt before an armchair and declared he was conducting an inspection. His posture was identical. His hands moved in the same short, decisive arcs. He ran a finger along the telescope's housing, checked the calibration dials, lifted the eyepiece and set it down again with a click that was not quite a click. He was looking for something. Not the note. Not yet. He was looking for the shape of a document, the ghost of a ledger page, the particular weight of a financial irregularity pressed flat against the instrument's frame. His jaw worked once, a single lateral grind, and then he was still, and the stillness was his own species of stillness, the stillness of a man who has already written the verdict and is merely confirming that the evidence will agree.

Sera Okafor stood to the left of the telescope, a notebook open in her hands, her pen moving in small, deliberate strokes. She watched Blackwood's hands. She watched the way his fingers found the same points on the brass, the same seams, the same joints, as they had three hours ago in the Rotunda. She noted the repetition. She said nothing. Her pen kept moving.

"You'll want to keep a close watch on the communications channels tonight, Agent Okafor," Blackwood said, not turning from the telescope. His voice was level, administrative, the voice of a man reading a maintenance schedule. "I fear we may be under surveillance."

Sera's pen did not stop. "Understood, Captain. And might I ask whether the surveillance you anticipate is inbound or outbound? Because the frequency logs I pulled this morning show a pattern that is, shall we say, irregular. I would not call it unusual. I would call it *specific*." She let the word sit. She did not elaborate. She did not need to. She held in her cabin, in a locked drawer beneath a false panel, a set of transmissions that had been routed through the station's relay array under a frequency that no longer existed, a frequency that had been decommissioned eleven years ago, and the origin point of those transmissions was not the mining colony below but a secondary relay that had been, until three days ago, assigned to a separatist cell operating out of the colony's restricted shaft. She did not mention this. She tightened the leash by one inch, no more. Blackwood's jaw worked again. The same single lateral grind.

Felix Drummond, who had been leaning against the far porthole with his arms crossed and his eyes on the rust-coloured curve of Mars filling the glass, straightened at that moment with the abruptness of a man who has remembered he left a stove on. He reached into his coat, withdrew a small bottle of cologne, uncapped it, and dabbed a drop onto his wrist with a speed that was not quite natural. The cushion was wrong.

Dr. Yuki Tanaka knew this the way a botanist knows a leaf is turning: not through logic, not through the apparatus of reasoning, but through the small, persistent signal in the fingertips. She pressed her thumb into the worn velvet of the armchair's seat, feeling the faint give where the stuffing had shifted. Not torn. Not ripped. Merely moved, as though a body had settled into it and risen again, leaving behind a softness that did not belong. The Grand Rotunda was silent except for the low, subsonic thrum of the station's rotation, a vibration one felt more in the molars than in the ears, and the brass railing along the curved wall caught a thin blade of artificial dawn-light that made the metal look, for a moment, like something cold and living.

"Someone was sitting here," she murmured, and the words were addressed to no one, carried on the recycled air with the particular quiet of a woman who has been alone among the breathing green for too long and has forgotten the social architecture of a room. "Recently. And they did not sit down the way a person sits when they are merely resting their legs."

She withdrew her hand. The velvet held the impression of her thumb for a moment, then released it. Tanaka touched the arm of the chair, then the grain of the wooden leg, then the polished stone floor where the chair's base met the ground, as though confirming, at each point of contact, that the world had not rearranged itself in the small hours while the station turned and turned and the red planet hung beyond the portholes like a slow, indifferent eye.

It was, as it so often is in a matter of this kind, a question of who was there.

Margaret Ashworth arrived at the rotunda's far curve at precisely the moment the artificial light shifted from its overnight amber to the pale, washed grey that the station's chronometer designated as pre-dawn. She did not walk so much as extend herself, a long, deliberate unfolding, and she stopped three paces from the armchair with the stillness of a woman who has made up her mind and will not be moved from it. Her coat was buttoned to the throat. Her hands were at her sides. She did not touch the railing. She did not touch the chair. She stood, and the quality of her standing was, in this room of curved brass and thin light, more threatening than any raised voice could have been.

Inspector Elara Voss was already there. She had been there, one suspected, for some time. She stood slightly apart from the others, near the junction where the rotunda's main corridor met the spoke gallery, and she was watching. Not looking. Watching. There is a difference, and Voss understood it the way a surgeon understands the difference between a clean incision and a careless one. Her stillness was a contrast to the room's low mechanical breathing, and in the thin light her face had the faint, hollow quality that the station's artificial dawn always gave to the unprepared, as though the light were not quite enough to fill the spaces between the eyes.

Captain Blackwood entered from the corridor on the port side, and he checked the entrance twice before he looked at the chair. The first check was a glance, quick and dismissive. The second was longer, his eyes tracking the far end of the corridor as though expecting a figure to materialize from the grey. "I need to keep a close eye on who enters and exits," he said, and his tone was the tone of a man reading a regulation aloud, though no regulation had been cited. "Someone might be trying to manipulate the situation." He said this to no one in particular. He said it to the room. The room, being a room, did not answer.

The hands were steady. That was the thing about them, the hands of Dr. Cassius Wren, that one might admire in a lecture hall or a laboratory and never quite understand in a drawing room. They moved with the unhurried precision of a man who had spent twenty-three years handling specimens that would shatter if one breathed too heavily, and now they performed an act of such modest mechanical complexity that a child could have done it: sliding a small slip of cream paper, folded once, beneath the rim of the crystal decanter on the salon table. The decanter was half-full of single-malt whisky, its distinctive cut pattern catching the flat, shadowless light of the station and throwing it back in small prismatic threads across the walnut surface. The note disappeared beneath the glass with the quietness of a petal settling on still water.

And then Wren's other hand rose, open-palmed, generous, gesturing broadly at the quarterly earnings report Felix Drummond had spread across the table as though it were a weather map.

"I quite understand your anxiety, Mr. Drummond," Wren said, and his voice was warm, almost paternal, the voice of a man discussing the quality of the morning's coffee. "One must always verify one's sources. The figures you are looking at here, I suspect, are merely... incomplete in their context. A single column, divorced from the broader assay sequence, is rather like reading a sentence without its paragraph. The meaning shifts. One might even say the meaning *invents* itself."

The Mining Archive at half past five in the morning was a room that had been designed by someone who considered silence a form of architecture. The ventilation system murmured its low, perpetual hymn through the ceiling grilles, a sound so constant that the human ear filed it away as nothing at all, the way one forgets the weight of one's own clothes. The fluorescent panels overhead cast their flat, clinical white across rows of steel filing cabinets, and the only other sound in the world was the faint, metronomic tick of the brass wall clock above the sealed door, its hands resting with absolute certainty upon the thirty-minute mark.

Margaret Ashworth stood before the fourth cabinet from the left, her gloved right hand extended, her index finger hovering a centimetre above a folder tab that should have been third from the left in the upper drawer. It was seventh. The crease along its tab was angled at a degree no filing clerk, no administrative hand, no routine maintenance procedure would produce. It was the crease of a thumb pressed deliberately, with intention, into the cardstock and then released. Her jaw tightened. One millimetre, perhaps two. The only concession to surprise she permitted herself, and even that she filed away with the same dispassionate efficiency she brought to quarterly balance sheets.

"Someone has been tidying my evidence," she murmured, in a tone one might direct at a particularly incompetent secretary who had misfiled the Henderson account for the third consecutive month. She did not raise her voice. She did not turn to the door behind her, though she was aware of its sealed state with the same quiet certainty with which one is aware of one's own heartbeat. The lock was engaged. The station's internal intercom, mounted at shoulder height on the bulkhead, showed no pending calls. She was alone in the Mining Archive, alone in the humming white silence, and the clock above the door continued its patient tick, and the clock said half past five, and the clock had always said half past five, and it would say half past five for another sixty seconds, and in that sixty-second window, someone else had been standing exactly where she now stood, and had moved the third folder to the seventh position, and had creased the tab with the precise pressure of a thumb that knew, with the absolute confidence of a woman who has memorised the contents of a drawer, exactly which page Margaret Ashworth would reach for first.

She withdrew her hand. She replaced it, this time on the edge of the drawer itself, and with two fingers she drew it open. The mechanism gave a soft, oiled whisper against the steel track, and the contents spilled into the flat white light in a neat, horizontal row of manila folders, each tab labelled in the station's standard typewriter font. The mineral assay reports for the third quarter. The ore-yield certificates, signed and sealed. The comparative density analyses for shafts seven through twelve. They were all present. Every document she had catalogued in her own meticulous ledger, every page she had intended to lay before the Interplanetary Trade Council as irrefutable proof of falsification, was here. And yet.

And yet the order was wrong.

Not scrambled. Not haphazard. Not the disorder of a hurried hand or a careless clerk. The order was wrong in the specific, surgical manner of a rearrangement. The falsified reports, which had been filed under the general heading of quarterly compliance, now sat in the second position from the left, where they would be the second folder her eye naturally fell upon. The genuine density analyses, which had been tucked near the back of the drawer beneath a stack of ventilation maintenance logs, had been elevated to the first position, creating a visual sequence that would lead any reader from the genuine to the falsified in a single, unavoidable sweep. Someone had not merely moved the documents. Someone had curated them. Someone had constructed a narrative out of the arrangement itself, a narrative that said: look here first, then look here, and the conclusion will arrive before you have finished drawing your next breath.

Margaret Ashworth removed her right glove. She did this slowly, with the unhurried precision of a woman who has never in her life performed a task without first considering whether the task was being performed for her benefit or for the benefit of an audience. The glove came away in a single, clean pull, and she set it on the cabinet's upper surface, the leather falling with a soft, final whisper. Then she reached into the drawer with her bare fingers and touched the tabs, one by one, feeling the grain of the cardstock, the slight warp of the paper beneath, the particular resistance of the third folder, which was the one she had intended to open first and which now sat in the seventh position, displaced, exiled, pushed to the back of the sequence as though its contents had been deemed too inconvenient for the story the rearranger wished to tell.

She did not open it. She did not open any of them. She stood with her hand resting on the drawer's edge, her fingers spread, her palm flat against the cool metal, and she thought, with the detached clarity of a woman conducting a post-mortem on her own assumptions, that the tampering had been performed in a window no wider than twenty minutes. The archive's access log, which she had checked before entering, showed no entries between five and ten and five and thirty. The door had been sealed from the outside during that interval, which meant the person who had rearranged the drawer had done so before the seal was engaged, or had done so from within, with the door already locked, or had done so with a key that did not appear in the log at all. Three possibilities. Three names, each of which she could identify with the same effortless precision with which she identified a misplaced decimal in a financial projection. She did not name them. Not yet. Naming them was a luxury she would permit herself in a quieter hour, in a room where the ventilation did not hum and the clock did not tick and the white light did not flatten every shadow into a single, undifferentiated grey.

Her fingertips, still bare, still resting on the drawer's edge, detected something. A faint tackiness. Not the dry, powdery residue of the archive's standard maintenance grease, which she knew by touch as well as she knew the grain of her own skin. This was smoother. Thicker. It had the particular, slightly waxy quality of a compound she had encountered once before, in the processing bays of the mining colony, where the sealants were formulated for the abrasive, oxidising environment of the shafts. A compound that was not stocked in the archive. A compound that was not stocked in any administrative or clerical station on the Ares Meridian. A compound that, if one wished to obtain it without drawing attention, required access to the processing bays, and therefore to the restricted equipment corridors, and therefore to a clearance that a filing clerk or an administrative aide would not, could not, possess.

Margaret Ashworth withdrew her hand. She replaced her glove, the leather sliding back into place with the same unhurried, ceremonial precision with which she had removed it. She stood very still. The ventilation hummed. The clock ticked. The white light fell, flat and indifferent, across the steel cabinets and the manila folders and the single, faint smear of industrial lubricant on the inner metal lip of the drawer, glistening with a dullness that the fluorescent panels did not illuminate quite enough to be seen, but which the pad of her thumb, pressed flat against the surface for the space of three heartbeats, had registered with the absolute fidelity of a woman who has spent twenty years reading the texture of the world.

She did not call for anyone. She did not reach for the intercom. She did not draw the drawer fully open to inspect the smear with a magnifying lens or to transfer the residue to a sample vial, as one might do in the tidy, procedural manner of an investigation conducted in full public view. Instead, she slid the drawer shut. The mechanism whispered against its track, and the steel met the cabinet's frame with a soft, deliberate click that she permitted herself to register as a small, private satisfaction, the click of a lid being placed upon a box whose contents one has decided, for the moment, to keep.

She turned. Her shoulders were level. Her spine was rigid. Her face, in the flat white light, was the face of a woman who has been told, in a meeting, that the quarterly figures require a minor adjustment, and who has agreed, in a tone so flat that the speaker has not yet registered that the agreement was not, in fact, an agreement. She walked to the door. Her footsteps made no sound on the alternating panels of the archive floor. The lock disengaged with a single, clean click, and the door swung inward on its pneumatic hinge, and beyond it the Spoke Gallery stretched in its long, curved silence, empty, the way a corridor is empty when the only person in it has just left it and the sound of her departure has not yet reached the far wall.

"Then we shall see which of you is the forger and which of you is the fool who hired her."

The words were low. They were not addressed to anyone. They were addressed to the room, to the steel cabinets, to the faint smear of lubricant on the inner lip of the drawer that she had left behind, undisturbed, uncollected, unacknowledged. They were addressed to the clock above the door, which now read five and thirty-one, and to the ventilation system, which continued its low, perpetual hymn, and to the white light, which fell, flat and indifferent, on the closed drawer and the empty space where Margaret Ashworth had stood, and on the single, faint smear that glistened, and glistened, and glistened, in a room that no one else was in, and would not be in for a very long time, and that she would not return to, and that she would never, under any circumstances, allow Inspector Elara Voss to examine.

The door closed behind her. The lock engaged. The clock ticked. The drawer was closed. The residue remained.
