The cologne arrived before the man did. It was not, in truth, a scent one would choose for a morning, or for a space station, or for any enclosed volume of recycled air that had already been breathing the same thin oxygen for eleven hours. It was heavy, amber, almost funereal, the kind of perfume a man applies when he wishes to be remembered rather than merely noticed. It rolled through the Grand Rotunda in a slow, deliberate wave, settling over the curved walls, the observation ring, the brass railing where a single strand of black hair caught the pale Martian light like a thread of dark wire. It settled over the armchair three paces from the centre, where the upholstery still bore the faintest drag-mark on the alternating walnut and birch floor beneath it, a scuff no wider than a thumbnail, as though something had been pulled free and set down again with great care.

Felix Drummond walked into that wave of his own making with the particular confidence of a man who has rehearsed his opening line in a mirror. His hands were already moving. They clasped, unclasped, clasped again, fingers working at the cuffs of a three-thousand-credit suit that had not been designed for the low-gravity sway of a rotating station. He smiled at Captain Blackwood. The smile was too bright, too complete, the kind of expression one wears over a wound.

"I've been reviewing the financial reports, Captain, and I must say, they seem a bit... off."

The words carried across the Rotunda with the clarity that a vast, curved space affords. Every syllable bounced off the observation ring and returned slightly altered, as though the room itself were considering the statement before delivering its verdict. Blackwood did not look at Felix. His eyes were on the armchair. His weight was on his left foot, his right heel raised a fraction, as though he might step back, or step forward, or step over the scuff mark in the floor and be somewhere else entirely. His fingers were already at the upholstery, testing the weave, running a thumb along the seam where the leather met the frame. He was looking for a tear. A stain. A thread of someone else's life worked into the fibres.

"Off, Mr. Drummond?" Blackwood's voice was clipped, authoritative, the voice of a man who has issued orders on a station for six years and has not yet learned that authority, in a room full of stakeholders, is merely a louder way of being ignored. "I should think you would find the reports satisfactory. They are, in fact, the reports I have filed."

"A simple observation, Captain. I need to keep a close eye on the financial discrepancies. They could be more than just a simple error." Felix's hands found a handshake that no one offered. He held it out, palm up, a gesture so open it bordered on theatrical. Blackwood did not take it. His fingers remained buried in the armchair's seam, and when he finally withdrew them, he pressed his thumb into the weave and held it there, as though feeling for a pulse.

It was at this moment that Sera Okafor entered the Rotunda. She did not announce herself. She simply appeared in the observation ring's cold, thin light, her jacket buttoned to the throat, her expression carrying the particular stillness of a woman who has already decided what she will write and is merely waiting for the words to arrive in the correct order. She leaned, slightly, as she spoke to Blackwood, as though she were about to share a secret and would not.

"Captain, I understand you are conducting a thorough review of the station's records. I merely wished to confirm that the communications log from last night remains sealed. For the record."

Blackwood's thumb left the armchair. His eyes flicked to Sera, then back to the chair, then to Felix, who was still holding his hand out, still smiling, still performing the role of the aggrieved stakeholder with the earnest dedication of a man who knows that silence would be his confession. "The log is sealed, Miss Okafor. As it has been since the incident."

Dr. Yuki Tanaka pressed her fingertips into the engineered earth with the patience of a woman who had spent eleven years coaxing chlorophyll from dead substrate, and she lifted a small clump of dark loam between her thumb and forefinger, turning it slowly as one might examine a stone pulled from a riverbed. The conservatory breathed around her in its slow, humid way. Condensation wept from the ceiling panels in long, patient threads, catching the soft bioluminescent glow of the hydroponic arrays above. The air was thick, green, alive with the smell of loam and the faint sweetness of overripe figs from the eastern trellis. "Dr. Tanaka. You are kneeling in my garden."

***

The sandalwood was the first thing one noticed, though one might prefer not to. It hung in the recycled air of the Crew's Mess like an obscenity in a chapel—thick, warm, insistent, belonging to no one who had any business in a room of bolted steel and cold ration trays. It belonged, in fact, to Felix Drummond, who stood at the long polished bulkhead with his collar half-adjusted and his reflection staring back at him with the particular blankness of a man rehearsing a speech to an audience of zero.

He was already sweating. Not the dignified perspiration of a man who has walked a long corridor in rotation-gravity, but the small, beading moisture at the temples and the dampness that gathered at the small of his back where the linen of his shirt pressed too close. He touched his chest pocket. There was no wallet there. He touched it again, as though checking for a wallet that ought to be there, and when his fingers found only the flat, unyielding surface of his jacket lining, he withdrew his hand with a speed that suggested he had touched something hot.

In his mind, the inventory ran in fragments, clipped and breathless, the way one counts one's fingers after a blow to the temple: *the quarterly report, the one with the redacted line, the one I need to find so I can hand it to Ashworth and let her scream about it while no one asks why the dividend figures in my own column do not reconcile with the Colonial Authority's ledger. The side-bet notebook, if it is here, if it is anywhere, the one with the half-erased names, because if Blackwood finds it first he will pin the shortfall on Wren and I will be the one asked to explain why my personal account received a transfer on the fourteenth. The filing cabinet. The one with the scratch on the lock. I need to see what is in that cabinet before anyone else does, and I need to see it in a way that can be described as routine, as administrative, as the natural duty of a senior stakeholder reviewing operational records for the colony's fiscal quarter.*

He straightened a chair. No one was sitting in it. No one had been sitting in it, perhaps, since the previous shift's dinner. He straightened it anyway, and the small creak of the leg against the alternating walnut and birch floor sounded, in the silence of the empty mess, like a confession.

The station hummed around him. That low, subsonic thrum of the rotation, the constant whisper of air recyclers working their endless circuit, the faint tick of cooling metal contracting in the thermal differential between the sunlit face and the shadowed rim. Through the single porthole at the far end of the room, Mars glowed its slow, rust-coloured glow, patient and indifferent, and the orange light fell across the mess tables in a long, flat stripe that made the cold steel of the surfaces look almost warm. It was not warm. Felix knew this. He had pressed his palm against the table an hour ago, in the Grand Rotunda, while Blackwood was talking about financial discrepancies in a voice that was almost too careful, almost too much like a man constructing a sentence in which the word *embezzlement* might, if the evening stretched on long enough, find its natural place. He had pressed his palm down and felt the cold seep into his skin and he had thought, very clearly, *I must find something. I must find something before he finds something else.*

Now he was here. Alone. The mess was empty in the way that utilitarian spaces become empty when the purpose for their occupation has passed—trays stacked in the dish bay, a grey ration tray abandoned at the far table with a single bite taken from a protein bar, the stamp on its corner reading a crew number that Felix did not recognise and did not wish to recognise. The coffee in the communal pot was old, black, and faintly bitter, and the smell of it underlay the sandalwood like a second, less dignified skin.

He moved to the small desk bolted to the port wall, where the station's administrative records were kept in a series of thin, manila folders clipped to a metal rail. He did not open them in order. He did not open them at all, not at first. He ran his fingers along the spine of each folder, feeling for the one that was slightly thicker, slightly misaligned, the one that would contain the quarterly earnings report with its single redacted line. His fingers were quick, almost impatient, and the motion had nothing to do with method and everything to do with a man who is afraid that if he stops moving, his hands will begin to shake.

They were beginning to shake.

He noticed this the way one notices a draft in a sealed room—a small, cold intrusion that does not quite make sense. His thumb trembled against the metal rail as he pulled the fourth folder free. He set it down. Picked it up again. The paper inside was stiff with age, the ink slightly faded, and he read three lines of the header before his eyes slid off the page and fixed on nothing in particular, the way a man stares at a word he has read too many times and can no longer parse.

*The Ashworth Mining Consortium. Fiscal Quarter Nine. Dividend allocation. Sub-accounts.*

He turned the page. Turned the page again. His lips moved without sound, tracking a column of figures that he had memorised three days ago in a hotel room on Earth, in a city whose name he would never speak aloud, in a room that smelled of cheap bourbon and the particular metallic tang of a man who has lost more money than he can account for to a house that does not ask questions. He had memorised those figures so that he could reconstruct them, later, in a ledger that would bear his name and his signature and a column of transfers that, if read by a competent auditor, would suggest a pattern of investment in a subsidiary that did not exist. He had memorised them so that he could un-memorise them, if it came to that, and tell himself he had never seen them at all.

He set the folder down. Picked up the next one. And the next. His search was not a search. It was a sifting, a desperate, selective combing, and every page he turned was a small act of self-justification delivered to an empty room. *I am looking for a discrepancy. I am looking for a discrepancy that is not mine. I am looking for a discrepancy that is Ashworth's, or Wren's, or Blackwood's, so long as it is not the one that lives in the fourth folder, in the column that does not add up, in the transfer dated the fourteenth, in the account that is not on the roster.*

It was in the seventh folder, tucked behind a sheaf of routine supply requisitions, that his fingers closed around the grease-stained maintenance log.

He almost did not take it. It was a small thing, a thin booklet of creased paper held together by a wire staple, and its cover was marked with a smear of dark oil that had worked its way through the printed text so that the words *CREW'S MESS – ENVIRONMENTAL CONTROLS* were half-legible beneath a brown, glistening stain. It smelled of machine grease and the faint, acrid bite of heated lubricant, and in the clean, sterile air of the mess it was a small, gritty obscenity, like a fingernail in a silk stocking.

He set it on the desk. Picked it up again.

The first three pages were unremarkable. Filter replacements. Airflow calibrations. A note, in a hand that was not his, about a loose panel in the ceiling grid that had been secured by a technician whose name Felix did not recognise. He turned the page.

The fourth page was different.

The ventilation schedule had been altered. Not replaced—altered. The original entries, printed in the station's standard typeface, listed the standard rotation of air exchange for the mess: intake at 0200, exhaust at 0230, intake again at 0400, a full cycle completing by 0445. But beside the 0200 entry, in pencil, someone had written *0130*. And beside the 0400 entry, the printed time had been crossed out with a single, firm stroke, and in its place, in the same small, careful hand, was written *0315*. The pencil marks were fresh. The graphite still held a faint, dusty sheen that had not yet settled into the paper's fibres.

Felix read it once. He read it again. His lips moved, silently, tracking the altered times, and his reflection in the bulkhead showed a man whose jaw had gone rigid, whose eyes had narrowed to a thin, bright line, whose hand had stopped moving altogether.

The mess's environmental controls. The ventilation schedule. Altered. At a time that was not in the printed log. At a time that, if one cross-referenced it with the station's general activity log—which Felix knew, because he had spent three years reading logs like this in boardrooms on Earth, in rooms that smelled of sandalwood and the particular confidence of a man who has never once been asked to explain where his money went—corresponded to a window in which no scheduled maintenance was logged, no technician was on shift, and no one, by the station's own records, should have been in this room at all.

His hand trembled.

It was not the tremor of curiosity. It was not the tremor of a man who has found an interesting puzzle and wishes to solve it. It was the tremor of a man who has, in the small hours of the night, been in this very room, in the very window of altered ventilation, at a time when the air recyclers were cycling in a pattern that would have masked the sound of a door unlocking, the sound of a page being torn from a ledger, the sound of a man breathing too quickly in a space where no one should be breathing. The tremor of a man who has just realised that the room he has been using as his private, necessary office has, for one unaccounted hour, belonged to someone else.

He did not think this through. He did not have the luxury of thinking it through. What he felt was a cold, clean shock, the kind one feels when a wire one has been relying on for years snaps without warning, and the entire structure it was supporting lurches, just slightly, just enough to make one aware that it had been leaning.

He looked at the grease stain on the cover. He looked at the pencil marks. He looked at the porthole, where Mars glowed its slow, patient orange, and he thought, with a clarity that was almost painful, *Someone was here. Someone was here, and they changed the schedule, and they knew this room, and they knew the hours, and they are not me, and I cannot account for them, and if anyone asks, if Blackwood asks, if Ashworth asks, if that woman Okafor with her journalist's eyes asks, I will have to say I do not know, I will have to say I was not here, and I will be lying, and I will be lying in a room that smells of old coffee and machine grease, and the lie will be in my hands, and my hands are shaking, and I cannot stop them shaking.*

He straightened his tie. He touched his chest pocket. He straightened the chair again, and this time the creak of its leg was smaller, almost inaudible, and he did not flinch.

His fingers closed around the fourth page of the maintenance log. The paper was thin, slightly soft with the grease that had seeped through from the cover. He held it for a moment, and in that moment the empty mess pressed close around him, the hum of the rotation loud in his ears, the orange light of Mars falling across the steel tables in its long, flat stripe. He thought of the Grand Rotunda, where he had been an hour ago, performing the role of the aggrieved stakeholder, asking Blackwood about financial discrepancies in a voice that was steady and concerned and utterly, completely a performance. He thought of the warm wine glass on the railing, still holding the ghost of the dead woman's last sip, and he thought, with a small, cold precision, *I will not be the one found in this room. I will not be the one who cannot account for the hour.*

He tore the page free.

The thumb pressed flat against the brass, and the grain of rust-coloured dust caught the cold Martian light like a thread of dried blood drawn across polished metal. Captain Theron Blackwood said nothing. He simply turned the smudge under his fingernail, watching the red particles scatter in a fine, almost imperceptible cloud that hung in the still air of the Observatorium before settling again, as though the station's faint rotational vibration were not enough to disturb them. Outside the crystal dome, Mars hung vast and red and utterly indifferent, its surface a smear of ochre and iron that had no interest in the five people who would, over the next hour, gather in this cold metallic room and circle that single smudge as though it were the only object in the universe worth examining. The telescope's brass mount smelled of ozone and old oil, and the velvet drapes behind it swallowed every sound the way a throat swallows a word spoken too loudly, leaving only the subliminal hum of the station's rotation grinding beneath their feet like a clock that had forgotten what time it was.

Dr. Yuki Tanaka arrived first among the others, though she had been walking for some time, her steps measured and unhurried, the slow deliberate pace of a woman handling a living specimen rather than a room. She stopped at the far side of the telescope, where the amber shadows cast by the dome's refraction fell longest, and she looked at the smudge. Not quickly. Not with the startled curiosity of a colleague noting an anomaly. She looked at it the way one looks at a letter written in a hand one recognises from years ago, and something shifted behind her eyes, a softening, almost an apology directed at no one in particular. She reached out, did not touch the brass, and simply held her fingertips an inch from the dust, as though the red particles might be a living thing that would scatter at the wrong breath. Then she withdrew her hand, smoothed the front of her coat with both palms, and said, very quietly, to no one, "It is not what it appears to be." She did not elaborate. She never did, not when the words she needed were too large for the room.

Sera Okafor came next, and she came with purpose, her heels striking the alternating walnut and birch floor in a rhythm that suggested she had already decided what she would say before she had opened her mouth. She positioned herself at Blackwood's left shoulder, close enough that the velvet drape behind her absorbed the whisper of her breathing, and she leaned in with the particular angle of a woman adjusting the focus of a lens. "Captain, I have been reviewing the quarterly figures from the consortium, and I must say, the discrepancies are rather... concerning." Her voice was warm, collegial, the tone of a journalist filing a routine piece. But her eyes were not on the numbers. Her eyes were on his face, watching the small muscle in his jaw, the way his thumb had stopped moving on the brass. She let the silence stretch. "The market fluctuations have been, shall we say, irregular. And certain communications, if I recall correctly, have been... extensive." The word hung in the cold air between them, and Blackwood's jaw tightened, and he said, in clipped sentences that allowed no gesture, no expansion, "The accuracy of the figures is a matter for the Trade Council, not for a communications officer." Sera smiled. It was not a large smile. "Of course, Captain. I merely wished to express my concern for the financial well-being of the station." She stepped back, out of the shadow of the drape, and her eyes flicked, just for a moment, toward the corridor where the Conservatory lay, as though she were confirming that no one else had overheard the particular frequency at which she had spoken.

Felix Drummond entered last, though he had been in the room for a full minute before any of them registered his presence, because his hands were already moving, already gripping the edge of the telescope's brass housing, already performing the small mechanical motions of a man whose fingers had learned, over years of counting and concealing, that stillness was a kind of

The dust had settled the way dust settles in a house where no one has opened a window in a great while: in thin, even layers, undisturbed, almost reverent, as though the gallery itself had drawn a long breath and held it. A fine grey mineral silt, the particular shade of old plaster and worn bone, lay along the full length of the Spoke Gallery in a coating so uniform it might have been laid down by a careful hand. And there, running from the far end where the Rotunda's broad archway opened into the corridor like a mouth half-formed in speech, there was a groove. A single, faint groove, no wider than a lead pencil's shadow, where the silt had been parted and pressed flat. Margaret Ashworth stood at the gallery's midpoint, her gloved right hand raised to the height of her sternum, and her index finger hovered above the mark at a distance of perhaps two centimetres. She did not lower it. She did not yet. She was measuring. The width of the groove. The angle at which the displaced silt curled outward, a tiny lip of disturbed powder along each edge. The way the mark thinned in the middle of the gallery where the floor's alternating walnut and birch planks ran beneath the dust, and thickened again where the grain of the wood had offered a faint resistance to whatever had drawn itself across the surface. She noted these things the way one notes a figure in a column of accounts: not with wonder, not with alarm, but with the quiet satisfaction of a number that resolves. The station hummed beneath her feet, that low, constant thrum of the great wheel turning in its orbit, a sound so persistent it had become less a sound than a quality of the air, a pressure against the eardrums that the body long ago ceased to register. The recycled ventilation whispered through its channels above, a faint, dry hiss, the only other voice in the gallery. And there was no one else. No one had entered since the last shift of the night watch. No one would enter for another twenty minutes, perhaps thirty, until the crew's morning routine brought them, one by one, into the Rotunda and down the spokes. Margaret had timed this. She had timed it the way one times a transaction: the precise moment when the room would be empty, when the mark would be unobserved, when the finding of it would belong, in the only sense that mattered, to her alone.

She lowered herself. Not a crouch, not a bend of the knees that would have announced the act to any onlooker. She simply became shorter, a controlled descent of the spine and the hips, the economy of a woman who had spent thirty years in boardrooms where the smallest unnecessary movement was a confession of uncertainty. Her left hand found the edge of a display panel along the gallery wall, fingers resting on the cool brushed metal, and her right hand descended until the pad of her gloved index finger touched the dust at the very beginning of the groove, where it emerged from the shadow of the Rotunda's archway. The glove was dark leather, supple, worn to a softness that made the contact with the mineral silt almost imperceptible. She drew the finger along the mark, slowly, with the even pressure one applies when testing the grain of a timber for a crack. The silt yielded under her fingertip with a faint, dry whisper, and the displaced powder fell in a fine trail along the groove's edge. She did not wipe it. She did not brush it. She let the small disturbance remain, a second mark within the first, and she followed it.

The gallery stretched in both directions, the Spoke Gallery's particular architecture unfolding in its long, narrow perspective: the alternating walnut and birch floorboards running the full length, the low display cases along the walls holding their specimens of ore and mineral in their glass-topped trays, the distant portholes where the red, rust-coloured disc of Mars hung in the black like a wound that would not close. The light was the station's own, the flat, sourceless illumination that came from no particular direction but seemed to rise from the surfaces themselves, casting no true shadows, making every object appear to float in a pale, indifferent glow. Margaret's eyes tracked the groove as it ran before her, thinning where the floor's surface was smooth, widening where a seam between planks had caught and torn the silt. She was counting. Not the length of the mark, though that too she registered: perhaps forty metres, perhaps fifty, running the gallery's full span from the Rotunda's threshold toward the far end where the corridor narrowed and darkened into the outer rim. She was counting the intervals. The regularity of the drag. The way it did not waver, did not double back, did not show the hesitation of a foot that had slipped or the lurch of a body dragged by an unsteady hand. It was, in her private and precise assessment, the mark of a single, continuous motion. A thing that had been pulled, or that had pulled itself, in one unbroken line. And it ended. Or rather, it led. It led toward the outer rim corridor, that narrow passage at the gallery's far end where the station's hull curved inward and the air grew colder and the light thinned to a greyish nothing. The corridor was visible from where she knelt, a vanishing point of shadow at the gallery's terminus, too far to resolve into detail. She did not look at it. She looked at the mark.

Her jaw was set. Not the rigid set of anger, nor the clenched tension of fear. The set of a woman who has found, in a long and tedious audit, the single line item that does not reconcile, and who is now, with the calm of absolute certainty, reaching for the pen that will make it reconcile in her favour. Her eyes were narrowed, not with suspicion but with the focused attention of a reader who has found the word that changes the meaning of the sentence. And there was, at the corner of her mouth, a small movement. Not a smile, precisely. The ghost of one. The almost-smile of a woman who has located, in a spreadsheet of two hundred rows, the one figure that, if quietly adjusted, would shift the entire column's total by a sum that changed everything. She withdrew her finger from the groove. The displaced silt fell in a thin, grey thread and settled, undisturbed, back into the dust. She raised her hand and, with two deliberate strokes of her other gloved fingers, brushed the powder from the pad of her index finger. The motion was clean. Clinical. The motion of a woman closing a file.

She rose. The same economy, the same controlled ascent, no wasted motion. And then she walked. Not quickly. Not with the purpose of a woman who had a place to be. She walked the length of the gallery in the direction the mark led, her heels striking the floorboards with a soft, measured tap, her gaze level, her hands clasped loosely before her. She passed the display cases, the low portholes, the faint smell of recycled air and the deeper, colder smell of the hull metal beyond the glass. She passed the point where the groove ran beneath her feet and she did not look down. She did not need to. She had already read it. She had already filed it. And when she reached the gallery's far end, where the corridor to the outer rim narrowed and the light failed and the station's rotation hummed louder in the thinner air, she stopped. She stood there for a moment, her back to the gallery, her face turned toward the dark corridor. She did not enter it. She did not call out. She did not reach for a communicator, or a light, or the small brass compass that hung at her belt. She stood in the grey half-darkness and she thought. Not about who had made the mark. Not about who had been dragged, or who had fled, or what had happened in the Rotunda at the hour when the station's lights were dimmed and the crew slept in their bunks with the low thrum of the wheel for a lullaby. She thought about the license. The station's operating license, the one that granted Ares Meridian its charter to exploit the Martian mineral deposits, the one that was due for renewal in four months, the one that, if revoked, would reduce the entire consortium's holdings to a collection of expensive rocks in a tin can orbiting a red planet. She thought about how a single unexplained death, a single unaccounted-for hour, a single drag mark in the dust that no one else had seen and no one else would think to look for, could become, in the right hands, the first entry in a report. Not a police report. An inquiry. A preliminary inquiry into the station's operational integrity, filed not by a detective but by a shareholder. Filed by the woman who sat on the Colonial Authority's oversight board and who had, for the better part of a year, been waiting for a reason to pull the thread.

She turned. She walked back the length of the gallery, the same measured pace, the same level gaze. And as she walked, her right hand went to the leather-bound volume at her hip, the small notebook she carried in a sheath at her belt, and she drew it free. The cover was dark, worn, the edges softened by years of use. She opened it to a page that was already half-filled with figures and abbreviations, and with a pen so fine its line was barely visible, she wrote. Not the width of the groove. Not the angle of the drag. Not the distance from the Rotunda to the rim. She wrote a date. A time. A single word: *rim*. And beneath it, in smaller script, a notation that would mean nothing to anyone who found the notebook and everything to the woman who had written it. She closed the leather cover. The sound it made was small, a soft click, the sound of a lock engaging.

Behind her, at the gallery's midpoint, the drag mark was still there. For the moment. The ventilation channels above exhaled their slow, dry breath, and the fine grey silt that had been disturbed by her fingertip began, almost imperceptibly, to shift. A grain here. A grain there. The small lip of displaced powder along the groove's edge softened, blurred, began to flow back into the channel like sand filling a crack. The station's air, patient and indifferent, was doing what it had always done: smoothing, erasing, returning the surface to its undisturbed uniformity. In an hour, perhaps two, the groove would be gone. The dust would lie as it had lain before, even and unbroken, and the only evidence that anything had been dragged across the Spoke Gallery at the seventh hour of the station's morning would be a single word in a leather-bound notebook, tucked into the sheath at the belt of a woman who was already walking back toward the Rotunda, her footsteps soft on the alternating walnut and birch, her face set in the expression of a woman who has closed a file and turned to the next. The mark faded. The gallery hummed. And Margaret Ashworth, alone in the vast, cold, acoustically alive silence of the Spoke, carried the weight of it forward, unshared, unreported, and entirely her own.

The decanter sat on the low table between the two leather chairs, and it was the decanter, and not the man, that first arrested the eye.

Captain Theron Blackwood held it in his right hand, his thumb tracing the cut crystal base in slow, deliberate arcs, as though he were reading the grain of a timber joint. The single-malt inside caught the low amber wash of the salon's overhead panels and held it there, a trapped sunset, a small liquid evening that refused to darken. He tilted the vessel two degrees to the left. The whisky did not slosh. It did not ripple. It sat, almost, as though the glass were sealed from within. He set the decanter back upon the walnut surface with a soft, precise click. Then he lifted it again, and tilted it.

A compartment. Or it isn't.

From the leather settee across the Persian carpet, Felix Drummond was very still. His expensive cologne—sandalwood and something beneath it that was chemical, synthetic, the smell of a man who had dressed in a hurry and not trusted the morning to be kind—cut through the peat and the oak of the whisky and filled the small salon with a sweetness that did not belong. Drummond's hands rested on his knees. They had been resting on his knees for some time. Before that they had been smoothing the front of his tie, and before that they had been gripping the armrest until the leather creaked, and before that they had been reaching for a glass of water he had not, in the end, drunk from. Now they were still, and the stillness was worse than the fidgeting, because the stillness was a held breath, and a held breath, in a room where two men are pretending to be curious about one another's ledgers, is a confession waiting for its punctuation.

"You've been at this a while, Captain." Drummond's voice came out too full, too round, as though he had been rehearsing the sentence in a mirror. "I mean, if I may be so bold, the decanter is a rather particular piece of glasswork, and I daresay you've examined it before. But the manner in which you hold it, you see, it suggests a certain, well, an interest that goes beyond the mere quality of the spirit."

"Beyond the spirit." Blackwood set the decanter down a second time. His hand did not linger. His hand never lingered. "What else might it contain, Mr. Drummond?"

"Well, you see, I was merely thinking, and I say this only as a stakeholder with a vested interest in the station's financial integrity, that the reports we've been receiving, the quarterly figures, they carry a certain, well, an inconsistency. A rounding, if you will. A tendency toward the optimistic. And I thought perhaps, and this is purely speculative, that one might find something tucked away. A document. A correction. Something that the numbers, as they stand, do not quite account for."

Blackwood looked at him. The look was brief, surgical, the sort of glance a man gives to a splinter he is deciding whether to extract. "The numbers."

"Yes. The numbers. Which, as I say, do not add up, and I confess it keeps me from sleeping, and I confess further that the recent fluctuations in the market have made the matter rather more, well, acute, shall we say."

"Who writes them."

Drummond's jaw worked. A small, private movement, as though the sentence had a taste he was tasting and not liking. "I would not, Captain, I would not be so bold as to speculate on the authorship of the reports.
