The brass railing caught the light in a long, slow arc, the way it did every four minutes and thirty-seven seconds as the station turned on its axis and the artificial sun swept its pale beam across the Rotunda's curved floor. And there, in that passing glow, a single grain of something fine and grey-white clung to the metal like a fleck of rust on polished silver—so slight that one might have mistaken it for a speck of dust from the recycled air, or a grain of the station's own slow, imperceptible wear. It was not, of course, either of those things. But then, one would not have known that from the woman kneeling beside the rail with her fingers already sliding beneath the panel seam, her lips moving in a low, rapid mutter that sounded less like prayer and more like a woman arguing with a ledger she did not wish to balance.

"No, no, that's not the frequency log, that's not the uplink record, that's not—" She pressed her thumbnail into the gap between the brass fitting and the composite paneling, feeling for a seam, a hidden channel, a data port that should not be there. "That's not what I'm looking for, that's not—"

"You are looking for something very specific, Miss Okafor."

The voice came from behind her, dry and measured, carrying the particular flatness of a woman who had been standing in one place for considerably longer than was comfortable and had decided, with the quiet finality of a cat watching a mouse hole, that the moment of intervention had arrived. Sera did not turn. She did not need to. She knew the cadence of that voice the way one knows the tick of a clock in a dark room—by rhythm, by the absence of ornament.

"And it is not what I would be looking for."

Sera's fingers stopped moving. For a half-second—no longer, perhaps, but in the Rotunda's low, honeyed gravity it stretched like a taffy pull—she remained crouched with her palm flat against the panel, her dark hair falling forward in a curtain that did not quite conceal the sharp, hungry set of her jaw. Then she straightened, slowly, and turned with the easy, slightly too-bright smile of a woman who had been caught rummaging through a neighbour's correspondence and was already composing the explanation she would offer.

"Inspector. I had no idea you were in the Rotunda."

"I have been in the Rotunda for eleven minutes." Voss stood with her hands clasped behind her back, her posture so perfectly still that one might have mistaken her for a figure in the station's decorative frieze. Her grey eyes were fixed on Sera's hands, not her face. "Eleven minutes, and you have examined the port-side paneling, the ceiling junction boxes, and the base of the central fountain. You have not once looked at the floor."

Sera's smile did not waver, but something behind it recalibrated. "The floor, Inspector, is a vast surface. One cannot simply—"

"One cannot simply look at the floor. Yes. I understand." Voss's tone was gentle in the way that a surgeon's scalpel is gentle. "One must be looking for something very specific. A frequency log. An uplink record." She let the words settle. "Blackwood's."

The name hung in the air between them with the particular weight of a word spoken in a sealed room, where the sound does not dissipate but presses against the curved walls and returns, faintly, from every direction. Sera's fingers, which had been resting on the brass railing with the casualness of a woman who had merely been admiring the station's architecture, tightened almost imperceptibly.

"I am a journalist, Inspector. I look for many things."

"You are looking for a data fragment. A communication log. Unauthorized contact with the separatist movement below." Voss's voice remained level, almost bored, as though she were reciting the contents of a manifest. "You have been looking for it since 06:30, by my count. You have been in the Observatorium, the Stakeholders' Salon, the Spoke Gallery, and now here. You are not examining the scene of a crime, Miss Okafor. You are searching a man's office for a letter he wrote to a woman he was not permitted to write to."

Sera opened her mouth. Closed it. Opened it again, and what emerged was a quick, layered sentence that carried the particular music of a woman deploying charm as a form of armour: "You know, Inspector, I find that your methodical approach to my professional duties is rather like a man explaining to a locksmith that one should not pick a lock. One simply does not need the explanation. One picks the lock, or one does not."

"No," Voss agreed. "One does not."

And in that single, flat syllable of agreement, Sera felt the ground shift beneath her in the way it did when the station's rotation completed its arc and the faint, almost subliminal lurch of artificial gravity reminded every body aboard that they were, in fact, suspended in the void above a red and silent world. She shifted her weight. The brass railing, warm from the station's internal heating, yielded slightly under her palm. And then her fingers found it.

Not a seam. Not a panel gap. Not the data port she had been pressing her thumbnail against for the better part of twenty minutes. A faintness. A whisper of texture against the pad of her index finger, as though someone had drawn a single, careful line across the metal and the metal had remembered the pressure. A powdering. Fine, grey-white, almost invisible against the brass, catching the light in a way that the surrounding metal did not. The metallic tang of it rose, faintly, into her nostrils—colder than brass, colder than the station's recycled air, carrying a subliminal bitterness that she could not name but her body registered with the old, animal certainty of a tongue tasting iron.

Sera's finger stopped moving.

And in the stillness behind her, Inspector Voss registered the change. It was not dramatic. There was no gasp, no exclamation, no sudden withdrawal of the hand. It was a cessation. A half-second in which the quick, purposeful motion of Sera Okafor's fingers—the kneel, the crouch, the running of her palm along the paneling in fast, searching bursts—simply stopped, as though a record had been paused mid-spin. And in that half-second, the woman who had been hunting a man's secret correspondence for three hours became, for the first time, a woman who had found something she had not been looking for.

Voss did not move. Did not shift her weight. Did not alter the angle of her clasped hands behind her back. She simply watched, with the patient, unblinking attention of a woman who had already decided what the powder was and was waiting, with the particular satisfaction of a chess player observing an opponent's forced move, to see whether Sera Okafor would name it aloud or swallow it.

Across the station, in the same four minutes and thirty-seven seconds, the world turned and other small dramas unfolded in their own sealed rooms. In the Spoke Gallery, Margaret Ashworth stood alone in the long, cold silence of the rim corridor, her fingers trailing the faint drag mark in the dust with the cold precision of a woman who had already decided what the mark meant and was merely confirming that her calculation had not been overtaken by the events of the morning. In the Stakeholders' Salon, Felix Drummond sat across from Captain Blackwood on the Persian carpet, both men leaning forward with the particular eagerness of men who are not listening to one another but listening for the sound of a door opening, and Blackwood spoke—something about quarterly yields, about the integrity of the colonial supply chain—and Drummond nodded and said, "Yes, Captain, quite, quite," with the over-eager precision of a man counting his own pulse to make sure it had not quickened. And in the Hydroponic Conservatory, where the air was thick with the green, living smell of chlorophyll and damp soil, Dr. Wren set a small folded paper on the edge of a workbench with the careful deliberation of a man placing a chess piece, while three metres away Dr. Tanaka bent over a row of orchid specimens with her magnifying loupe, her dark eyes scanning the roots, the leaves, the faintest discoloration of stem, looking for something she had not yet been able to name.

But in the Grand Rotunda, the light swept its long arc across the floor, and Sera Okafor held her powder-dusted finger up before her, studying it in the way a jeweller studies a stone she has not yet priced. The grain was visible on her skin, a single point of grey-white against the warm brown of her finger, and she turned it once, twice, in the passing light, as though she might be able to read its origin the way one reads a watermark in paper.

"Powder," she said, with the light, offhand tone of a woman commenting on the weather. "Brass dust, I expect. The station's a bit old."

Voss did not blink.

"The station is three years old, Miss Okafor."

A silence. The faint hum of the rotation filled it, the low, almost subliminal vibration that every crew member learned to ignore after the first week and then, in moments of stillness, heard again with the particular clarity of a sound one has forgotten is there. Sera's smile did not waver. But her eyes moved. A single, quick flick, lateral, toward the far corridor where the Captain's quarters opened into the Rotunda's eastern wall. A flick that lasted perhaps a quarter of a second. A flick that said,

***

The conservatory breathed. There was no other word for it, in the way the air moved through the glass panels with a faint, almost tidal rhythm, carrying the thick sweetness of damp peat and crushed mint, the green exhalation of a hundred species of flora that had never known a season but were, by the station's design, permitted to pretend otherwise. The UV grow-lights hummed their low, insectine note overhead, casting everything in a pale violet that made the leaves look simultaneously more alive and less real, as though one were viewing a painting of a garden rather than the garden itself. Condensation beaded along the interior of the glass and slid in slow, deliberate rivulets toward the drainage channels, each droplet catching the light and releasing it again in tiny, momentary constellations. The air was warm. It was thick. It was, in the way of enclosed spaces aboard a rotating station, the only air there was, and to breathe it was to breathe the breath of every other soul on Ares Meridian, recycled and returned, intimate as a shared secret.

Dr. Cassius Wren stood at the irrigation console with his back to the door, and his hands were doing what his hands always did. They folded. They smoothed. They creased and uncreased a single sheet of stationery with the patience of a man pressing a flower into a book, as though the act of folding paper into a precise rectangle were a form of prayer, or a form of penance. The note was addressed in a hand that was not his. This was the small, terrible fact of it: the loop of the capital M, the particular slant of the terminal strokes, the way the ink had been laid down with a pressure that suggested a left hand writing in a right hand's imitation. He set the folded rectangle on the edge of the console where the morning light, filtered through two panels of glass and the violet glow of the grow-lights, would catch the ink at precisely the angle a passing eye would register it. He did not look up. His jaw worked once, a single lateral grind of the masseter muscle, and then his hands resumed their small, purposeless folding of nothing, smoothing a crease that was not there. The note was a lure. It was, in the taxonomy of Wren's particular anxieties, a test. If Margaret Ashworth read it and came, she came into a private, unrecorded space where he might gauge, with the careful precision of a man reading a core sample, exactly what she intended to lay before the Trade Council. His true terror was not the meeting. His true terror was that she already held the evidence in her hand and had simply not yet chosen the moment to open it.

Dr. Yuki Tanaka entered the conservatory the way water enters a dry channel: without announcement, without the suggestion of force, with the soft, inevitable pressure of a body that had learned, over years of kneeling in soil and pressing its ear to the stems of things, that the world spoke in frequencies below the threshold of ordinary hearing. She did not walk so much as flow, her white coat brushing the lower leaves of a row of dwarf citrus with a sound like a whispered syllable. Her movements were those of a woman conducting an inventory of the living world, and in this she was, by her own private definition, more rigorous than any audit of ledgers or balance sheets. She knelt. She touched. She pressed her thumb into a section of engineered loam near the base of a fern cluster and brought it to her nose, inhaling with the unhurried attention of a sommelier examining a cork. The soil was, she noted, a fraction too dry. The root structure of the adjacent moss was a millimetre shallower than the previous morning's reading. These were not observations that would appear in a report. They were observations that lived in the body, in the muscle memory of a woman who had spent eleven years listening to plants.

It was in the third minute of her kneeling, her fingers tracing the rim of the marble fountain basin in a slow, circular gesture, that her thumb stopped. A small dark bead, half-dried, on the lip of the marble basin. She did not look up. She did not exclaim. She simply closed her fingers around the thought the way one closes a hand around a seed that has been saved for a season not yet come, and she turned her attention to the section of loam beside the fountain, where the engineered soil had been pressed and disturbed, where the faint impression of a shoe sole—too large for her, too deliberate in its angle—broke the uniform texture of the bed. She remembered the exact location. She would remember the exact location for a very long time.

"Dr. Wren." Her voice was soft. It was not a question. "I have been thinking about the recent financial discrepancies in the station's allocation. They seem to be affecting the hydroponic division's budget in a manner I find, shall we say, irregular. Have you noticed anything unusual in the supply requisitions?"

Wren's hands, which had been folding a second sheet of stationery with the same meticulous care as the first, stopped. Then resumed. Then stopped again. "I am not, I think, the appropriate person to consult on budgetary matters, Dr. Tanaka. I am a geologist. I deal in iron-oxide ratios and core extraction yields. The financial architecture of the station is, I confess, somewhat beyond my professional purview."

"Of course." She smiled. It was a gentle smile, the kind one gives a child who has miscounted his sweets. "I merely wished to express my concern."

And in the half-second of silence that followed, in the space between her smile and his folding, the word turned over in her mind like a stone in the palm. Nightshade extract. A compound she had catalogued herself, three weeks ago, in the reagent store behind the eastern glass panel. A compound that was, by every pharmacological index she had ever consulted, a substance that did not belong in a botanical research facility. She set the word down. She did not speak it. She did not, in that moment, connect it to the bead on the marble or the impression in the loam. But she held it. She held it the way one holds a breath underwater, knowing that the surface will come, that the surface will come, and that when it does, one will have to decide whether to speak or to swallow.

Wren, sensing the shift in the room's temperature—though the thermostat had not moved a fraction—folded the second note with a haste that was, to anyone watching, indistinguishable from the haste of a man who has not yet decided what to do with his hands. He placed it on the shelf above the irrigation console, a position slightly higher, slightly less obvious, as though the first had not been enough, as though he were trying to outpace his own doubt by doubling the bait. His fingers lingered

***

The first thing to notice, if one noticed at all, was the light. It entered the Observatorium not as a beam but as a slow, deliberate unfolding, the crystal dome above refracting the station's artificial daylight into prismatic shards that crawled across the obsidian floor with the patience of something alive. The telescope stood at the centre of the room like a great brass eye, its mount catching and holding those shards in the crevices of its threading, and it was into one such crevice, no larger than a pinhead, that Sera Okafor's thumbnail came to rest.

She had been tracing the brass ring of the mount for nearly four minutes. This was not the hurried examination of a woman who had been told to look for something. It was the unhurried, almost lazy pass of a fingertip that had learned, in a lifetime of reporting, that the truth preferred to hide in the residue others overlooked. Her thumb caught the smudge and she paused. Not sharply. Not with a gasp or a sharp intake of breath. She simply tilted her head, as one might tilt one's head over a particularly interesting word in a letter, and let the prismatic light catch the grit beneath her nail. It was red. Not the rust-red of old iron, not the oxblood of dried wine, but the specific oxide-red of Martian regolith, fine and granular, settling into the grain of the brass with the tenacity of crushed garnet. In a sealed, rotating station that had never broken its atmospheric seal, in a room three hundred kilometres above the surface where the air was recycled and filtered and counted, that dust should not exist.

"Well," she said, very softly, to no one in particular. "That's either a very thorough cleaning, or a very careless visitor."

The silence that followed was the particular silence of a cathedral after the last hymn has died. The dome above continued its slow refraction, indifferent. Sera withdrew her thumb, examined the grit for a moment longer, and then, with the unhurried deliberation of a woman filing a document she intended to re-read at a more advantageous hour, she tucked her hand into the pocket of her jacket and walked.

Three corridors away, in the hydroponic conservatory where the air hung thick and green and the light was the flat, sourceless glow of grow-lamps, Dr. Cassius Wren had been listening. Not to the water trickling over the marble fountain, nor to the faint creak of the station's rotation in the deep structural bones. He had been listening to Margaret Ashworth's voice, low and measured, speaking to him in the quiet way one speaks to a colleague whose one's one has already decided to silence. She had said very little. He had said less. The conversation, such as it was, had been a formality, a closing of a door they had both known was already shut. She had turned to leave, and he had watched her go with the steady, unblinking attention of a man who had just set a piece on a board and was now calculating the opponent's forced response.

Now, in the Observatorium, he stood before the telescope mount. His hands were steady. This was the detail that should have troubled anyone who knew him, for Dr. Wren was not a man of steady hands in moments of emotion; he shook, he fidgeted, he tapped his fingers against the nearest surface. But here, in the cold cathedral light, his fingers moved with the precision of a watchmaker setting a gear. He withdrew a small folded paper from his breast pocket. It was cream-coloured, slightly heavier than standard stationery, and the handwriting upon its face was a careful, almost loving imitation of Margaret Ashworth's own script. Not quite. A hairline deviation in the slant of the 'g', a fraction too much pressure on the terminal flourish of the 'y'. Not quite her. Not quite anyone's, if one looked closely enough. He placed it on the brass mount at a specific angle, the upper left corner just overlapping the ring, the lower right free to catch the prismatic light that would, at precisely 08:52, sweep across it in a slow arc of amber and violet. A chess piece, set with care. A thread, laid for a spider.

He did not look at it again. He turned, and his long coat swung with the particular silence of a man who has made an irreversible decision and is now merely executing it. His destination was the Mining Archive, three decks below, where Margaret Ashworth would, by his calculation, be by 08:47, having taken the corridor from the conservatory through the Spoke Gallery and down the service lift. He would be there at 08:46. He would be there, and he would be waiting, and the thirty seconds between her arrival and the moment she reached for the Interplanetary Trade Council's relay terminal would be his. Thirty seconds in which to be the concerned colleague, the worried friend, the man who had merely been passing. Thirty seconds in which to say the right words in the right order. He had rehearsed them. He had rehearsed them many times, in the small hours, in the particular silence of his quarters where the station's rotation hummed like a lullaby for the dead.

In the Grand Rotunda, Sera Okafor had not yet reached the armchair. She was still walking, her footsteps soft on the alternating walnut and birch floor, her mind already three steps ahead of her body. The dust. She would write it up. She would file it under an unremarkable heading, a minor atmospheric anomaly, a contamination event of negligible consequence. She would file it, and then she would not file it. She would let it sit in the drawer of her desk, in the small tin box where the stories that mattered were kept not for publication but for leverage, for the slow, patient fermentation of a truth that was worth more unspoken than spoken. The red dust was not a clue. It was a card, held face-down, and the hand that held it was her own.

In the Mining Archive, Margaret Ashworth was already at the filing cabinet. The drawer was open, its contents rearranged with the meticulous disorder of a woman who had been searching for a specific document and had not found it. Her fingers moved across the forty-seven documents bearing the dead man's name with the cold, practised efficiency of a woman who had audited a hundred ledgers and found a hundred discrepancies, and who had never, in her long and careful career, once permitted another person to see her hands tremble. She was not looking for the falsified assay reports. She was looking for the original ore-yield certificates, the signed and sealed documents that would, in the right hands, at the right moment, before the right tribunal, unmake not merely Dr. Wren's career but the entire financial architecture upon which the station's continued existence depended. She would find them, or she would not. Either way, she would not be found by him first.

And in the Observatorium, where the crystal dome still turned its slow, prismatic gaze over the obsidian floor, Dr. Yuki Tanaka had arrived. She did not look at the telescope. She did not look at the note, which by now caught the full sweep of the refracted light and glowed faintly amber in its corner. She looked at the dome. Her fingers, long and clean and smelling faintly of the chlorophyll and loam of the conservatory, found the seam in the crystal dome's lower housing. The seam that no maintenance schematic accounted for. The seam that had been there, she was almost certain, since before the station was commissioned, a hidden cavity in the structure's bones, large enough for a sealed canister, large enough for a thing that did not appear on any inventory or manifest. She touched it the way one touches a sleeping animal, with the tenderness of a woman who has been looking for a particular lock for six weeks and has finally, in the quiet of a morning when the others were scattered across the station's corridors like scattered seeds, found the key.

She withdrew her hand. She did not open the compartment. Not yet. She pressed her palm flat against the cold glass, felt the deep vibration of the station's rotation beneath her skin, the slow and certain turning of the world around the world, and whispered, so softly that the dome's refraction might have swallowed the sound before it reached the walls: "Not today, little things. Not yet."

Wren was already three strides from the telescope. His coat swung. His jaw was set in the particular tightness that meant the rehearsal was over and the performance had begun. He did not look back. He did not need to. The note

***

The sound arrived before the sight of it, as all things aboard the Ares Meridian did. A low, bone-deep thrum, rising from somewhere beneath the soles of the feet and settling in the molars, in the back of the sinuses, in that particular hollow behind the ear where a man feels the world turning around him. The rotation generators. Eight hundred and forty-five rotations per day, each one a small death of gravity and a small resurrection of it, and the mess floor carried the vibration like a tuning fork struck in a cathedral. Captain Theron Blackwood felt it in his teeth before he opened his eyes to the flat, institutional glow of the overhead fluorescents. He did not open his eyes. He did not need to.

His fingers, still encased in the thin regulation gloves he had not yet removed from the morning watch change, traced the seam where the galley's steam-piping housing met the bulkhead. He knew this seam. He had measured it twice already, in the small hours, when the rest of the crew slept in their narrow bunks and the mess was a tomb of cold reheat-lights and the ghost-smell of recycled coffee that no amount of ventilation could quite scrub from the alloy walls. He knew the width of the gap. He knew the exact pressure required to release the housing panel without triggering the internal interlock. He knew it the way a forger knows the particular slant of a signature he has studied for three nights in a row, the way a man knows the lock on his own door, or the way a husband knows the precise moment his wife's breathing shifts from deep to shallow. Intimacy, in the end, is merely repetition performed without witnesses.

He removed his left glove. Then his right. Folded them, aligned the cuffs, set them on the stainless-steel counter beside the galley's cold-water tap. The mess was empty. It was always empty at this hour, before the breakfast cycle began its slow, mechanical clatter. The chairs were pushed in too neatly, their metal legs tucked flush against the table's edge with a precision that suggested no one had sat in them, or that someone had sat and then corrected the disorder afterward. A single mug remained on the far table, the one nearest the porthole where the Martian dust hung in a perpetual rust-colored haze beyond the triple glass. The mug was from the previous shift. No one had washed it. No one had moved it. It sat there with the quiet accusation of a small, abandoned thing, its handle oriented at an angle that no one had chosen to straighten.

Blackwood did not look at the mug. He looked at the panel.

His right hand pressed flat against the housing's lower edge, the fingers splayed, the thumb hooking beneath the lip. He applied pressure. Not much. A surgeon's pressure. The panel yielded with a soft, almost apologetic click, and the gap widened by the width of a thumbnail. Behind it: the dark throat of the maintenance cavity, the tangle of steam conduits and coolant lines, the faint warm breath of machinery that had been running, as it always ran, without anyone to tend it. He leaned in. His nose was close to the metal. He could smell the grease, the mineral tang of the coolant, the particular metallic sweetness of recycled air that had cycled through this compartment a thousand times.

The cavity was empty. He had checked for that specifically. Not for evidence. Not for tampering. Not for the signs of a crime that had not, in any meaningful sense, been committed against him. He was confirming the absence of an obstruction so that, later, before the morning briefing, before the names were spoken and the ledgers opened and the quiet arithmetic of the colony's accounts was laid bare for all to see, he could place a single sheet of paper in this space. One sheet. A colony-fund transfer record, doctored in the hand of a man who was not a forger but who had, in his own particular way, signed his own name to numbers that had never existed. The station required a clear accounting. That was all. That was the whole of it.

His knuckles whitened on the panel's edge. The leather of his tunic creaked faintly at the shoulder. He held the grip for a moment longer than was necessary, longer than the task demanded, and in the silence of the empty mess, in the hum of the generators and the tick of a cooling steam valve somewhere behind the bulkhead, he whispered two words to no one. To the panel. To the dark cavity. To the empty chair where no one sat.

"For the station."

The words were small. Procedural. The cadence of a man reading from a checklist, ticking off an item. *Seal is clean. Housing is empty. Good.* He straightened. He replaced the panel with the same measured pressure, the same surgeon's attention, and it clicked shut with a sound no louder than a knuckle cracking in a quiet room. He did not check the seam again. He did not need to.

Then his thumb caught it.

A hairline scratch. Barely a whisper of displaced metal, no wider than the grain of a pin. Fresh. The alloy along its length was faintly warm, still retaining the heat of whatever had drawn it across the surface an hour, perhaps two hours, ago. The warmth seeped through his bare skin, a small and specific heat, like touching the rim of a cup that has just been set down.

Blackwood froze.

His jaw tightened. The muscles along his temple worked once, twice, a small mechanical grinding. He did not call it out. He did not reach for a pen to log it in the maintenance record. He did not photograph it, or measure it, or note its orientation relative to the steam conduit. He simply pressed his palm flat against the panel, felt the warmth spread across his lifeline, and thought, in the flat clipped cadence of a man issuing an order to himself across a quiet room: *Someone else has been here.*

He held the position for a half-second. A full second. The generators thrummed. The mug on the far table waited for a hand that would not come. Beyond the porthole, the dust of Mars turned in its slow, rust-colored orbit, indifferent to the small human dramas that played out in the sealed compartments above it. Somewhere in the station's upper ring, a woman was walking with purpose toward the Grand Rotunda, her footsteps a quick, measured percussion on the alternating walnut and birch floor. In the Hydroponic Conservatory, a man was bending over a patch of disturbed soil, his face unreadable, his hands moving with the careful deliberation of a man who knows exactly what he is looking for and is equally certain he will find it. In the Observatorium, a forged note sat on the brass mount of the great telescope, addressed in a hand that was not its own, waiting for the woman who would read it and follow the path it laid out. In the Mining Archive, another woman sat alone in the dim, mineral-scented dark, turning a page of her private notebook over and over, the crease deepening with each repetition, as though the paper itself were a question she could not stop asking.

None of this reached Blackwood. None of it mattered. There was only the panel, and the scratch, and the warmth of it, and the terrible, quiet knowledge that if he were to report it, to call for an independent examination, to invite a pair of eyes that were not his own to trace the metal and note the adjacent surfaces, those eyes would find what he had left there in the small hours. His own fingerprints. The particular pressure pattern of his thumb on the lower lip. The faint residue of the glove he had worn the first time, the one he had folded and set on the counter and not yet put back in his tunic pocket.

He removed his hand from the panel. He replaced the scratch's warmth with nothing. He straightened his tunic, smoothing the fabric over his hips with two flat palms, the gesture automatic, military, the gesture of a man who has been taught that the body must always be in regulation, even when the mind has not. He picked up his gloves. He put them back on, first the left, then the right, aligning the cuffs again. He did not look at the panel. He did not look at the mug. He walked to the mess's far door, the one that opened onto the Spoke Gallery, where the station's long axis stretched out in both directions and the stars beyond the glass were fixed and cold and utterly without interest in the small, sealed world of the Ares Meridian.

The door opened. The door closed. The panel, behind him, sat flush and silent in the wall, the scratch invisible to any eye that did not know precisely where to look, the warmth already fading into the ambient chill of the recycled air. The generators thrummed. The fluorescent overheads hummed their flat, toneless note. The mug waited.

And the mess was empty again, as it had been before, as it would be after, a room of cold steel and pushed-in chairs and the ghost-smell of coffee, holding its silence the way a mouth holds a word it will never say.

***

The thumbnail caught the seam first. That was the thing about Sera Okafor's hands, the thing that had made three separate editors at the Interplanetary Press describe her, in writing, as "a woman who edited with her fingertips." A small, precise instrument. The synthetic weave of the armchair's underside gave a fraction of a millimetre under the pressure of her thumb, and she felt it the way a pianist feels the resistance of a key before the note sounds. She did not pull. She did not yank. She worked the seam open the way one works a thread from a hem, with the patience of a woman who has all the time in the world and precisely none of it.

Behind her, three paces away and no more, Elara Voss stood with her arms loosely crossed over her chest. She had not moved in eleven seconds. Sera could feel the stillness the way one feels the particular quality of silence in a room where someone is waiting. It was not the absence of sound; the Grand Rotunda hummed with its usual subsonic thrum, that low rotational pulse one felt in the molars before the ears registered it, and the ventilation system exhaled its cool, faintly metallic breath across the floor in slow, measured intervals. It was the absence of *intention*. Voss had no intention to cross that three-pace gap. She had no intention to cross it for a very long time, if Sera continued to perform her small, precise surgery on the armchair's underside with such undisturbed concentration.

Around them, the Rotunda held its other occupants in various states of restless departure. Dr. Cassius Wren had already turned his back on the group, his long geologist's frame angled toward the corridor that led to the Mining Archive, his fingers drumming a quick, anxious rhythm against his thigh. He was moving with the particular speed of a man who has calculated a window of thirty seconds and is determined to be in the right room before it closes. Yuki Tanaka, smaller, quieter, stood near the far railing with her eyes on the brass fittings, her fingers trailing along the metal as though reading braille written by the station's engineers. She had been in the Observatorium twenty minutes ago, examining the crystal dome with the methodical thoroughness of a woman searching for a lost earring in a field of wheat, and now she was here, in the Rotunda, still thinking in terms of hidden compartments and concealed mechanisms. Captain Blackwood was already halfway to the door, his broad shoulders cutting a clean line against the amber glow of the interior lighting, heading toward the Hydroponic Conservatory with the purposeful stride of a man who has decided to interrogate the soil rather than the people who tend it. Felix Drummond lingered near the file cabinet with its fresh scratch on the lock, his stakeholder's eyes moving over the rearranged documents with the acquisitive hunger of a man reading a balance sheet in a language he half-understood. And Margaret Ashworth, Chairwoman of the Ashworth Mining Consortium, stood with her hands clasped before her in that particular way of hers—fingers interlaced, thumbs pressing into the webbing of the other hand—as though she were trying to hold herself together through sheer mechanical force. She had been in the file cabinet drawer. She had been in the drawer, and she had found what she had found, and now she stood in the Rotunda and breathed.

But none of this mattered. None of it, in the particular geometry of this moment, mattered more than the three inches of leather and synthetic fibre between Sera Okafor's fingertips and whatever lay beneath.

She had not been looking for a weapon. She had not been looking for the stiletto dagger, or the wine glass with its bitter almond trace, or the black hair caught in the railing. She had been looking for a thread. A specific thread, in a specific weave, leading to a specific name. Captain Blackwood's unauthorized transmissions. The separatist communications that had no business existing on a station whose entire legal framework depended on the Colonial Authority's continued sovereignty. She had come to the Rotunda at 08:50 with that single, surgical purpose, and the search had been, up to this point, a failure of the most irritating kind. The thread was not where she had expected it. It was not in Blackwood's journal, which she had read twice. It was not in the guest book with its crossed-out name. It was not in the quarterly earnings report with its single redacted line.

And then the armchair. The armchair, which she had passed three times without a second glance, because Sera Okafor was a woman of purpose and did not stop to examine furniture. And then, on the fourth pass, her hand had drifted to the cushion the way a hand drifts to a familiar pocket, and the seam had caught, and the seam had *given*, and now her fingers were beneath, working, finding.

The paper was small. Smaller than she had expected. A folded rectangle, no larger than a matchbook, creased twice along its centre so that it had the compact, dense feel of a letter that had been opened, re-folded, and hidden in a hurry. The paper was slightly discoloured at the fold—a faint yellowing, the kind that comes from being pressed against skin, from the oils of a thumb that had turned it over and over in the dark. It smelled of nothing in particular. Recycled air. The faintest ghost of something botanical, chlorophyll and cold soil, as though it had lain for a few hours in the Conservatory before finding its final resting place beneath this particular cushion.

Sera lifted it free. She held it between her thumb and forefinger, and for a moment—two seconds, perhaps three—she simply looked at it. The crease caught the amber light. The fold was old, deliberate, the work of a hand that had wanted this to be small, to be *hidden*, to be found by no one and by everyone and by the one person who would know what to do with it. The deceased. The man who had been dead since before 0247, before the pocket watch stopped, before the wine went cold in its glass. He had hidden this. He had hidden it beneath a cushion in the one room where everyone gathered, in the one piece of furniture everyone sat in, in the one spot no one would think to look, because no one would think to look *beneath*.

She unfolded it. One fold. Two. The paper lay flat in her palm, and she read the first line.

Her face did not change. This was the thing that Voss, three paces away, watched with the particular attention of a woman who has spent twenty years reading the micro-geography of human deception. No widening of the eyes. No tightening of the jaw. No small, involuntary intake of breath through the nose. Sera Okafor's face performed the function of a face. It was there. It was present. It was, if anything, a fraction more relaxed than it had been thirty seconds ago, as though the paper had answered a question she had been carrying so long she had forgotten it was a question.

She folded it again. This time more carefully. She aligned the crease with the crease, pressed the edge flat with her thumbnail, turned it over, pressed it once more. The small rectangle was compact again. Dense. A matchbook. A secret in the shape of a secret.

And then she slid it into the inner pocket of her jacket.

The motion was small. A flick of the wrist, a soft whisper of fabric against fabric, the paper disappearing into the dark lining as though it had never been. She did not extend it toward Voss. She did not hold it up between two fingers for inspection, the way one holds up a card at a poker table, the way one holds up a clue in a story one is telling. She put it away. She put it away the way a woman puts away a second knife, a backup, a thing she will use when the first one is not enough.

Voss watched the pocket. Voss watched the hand retreat. Voss watched the jacket settle back into its natural drape, the inner pocket holding its new, small, warm weight against the journalist's ribs.

"A journalist's instinct, Ms. Okafor." The voice was flat. Unhurried. The cadence of a woman reading a weather report, or a woman reading a eulogy she had already memorised. "Or a detective's."

Sera did not answer. She turned, slowly, and looked at Voss. Three paces. The amber light caught the line of Voss's jaw, the faintest shadow beneath her eyes where the station's artificial day had not yet fully broken. They looked at each other across the three paces, across the hum of the rotation, across the cool metallic breath of the ventilation system, across the entire unspoken architecture of what Sera had just done and what Voss had just seen.

Voss tilted her head. A fraction. A degree, perhaps two. The tilt was so slight that anyone else in the Rotunda—Wren, already gone; Tanaka, still tracing the brass; Blackwood, already a memory of broad shoulders at the corridor's end; Drummond, still reading his numbers; Ashworth, still holding herself together—would not have noticed it. But Sera noticed it. Sera always noticed it. The tilt was a question. The tilt was also an accusation. The tilt said: *I know what you have. I know what you are keeping. I know, and I will wait, and the waiting will be very, very long.*

Sera met the tilt. She held it. And in the Grand Rotunda, in the cool recycled air, in the soft amber glow that caught the weave of the armchair and the brass of the railing and the small, empty space where the cushion had been, the two women stood in their three paces of silence and said nothing more, because nothing more needed to be said, and because what had been said was enough, and because the paper was warm against Sera's ribs, and the tilt was still in the air, and the station turned, and turned, and turned.

***

It is a curious thing about silence in a sealed environment. On Earth, a quiet room is merely the absence of sound; one hears the wind in the eaves, the distant murmur of traffic, the creak of a house settling into its foundations. But here, aboard the Ares Meridian, in the Mining Archive at the station's midsection where the rotation is most gentle and the air is scrubbed so thoroughly of moisture that one's lips crack before the hour is out, silence has a *texture*. It presses against the eardrums like a thumb against a bruise. It is the silence of a room where only documents and dead rock samples exist, where the nearest living thing is the woman standing before the steel filing cabinet with her knuckles white and her breath shallow, and the nearest breathing thing is the hum of the ventilation system three decks above, a sound so constant it has become, for all practical purposes, indistinguishable from thought.

Margaret Ashworth's thumbnail caught the lower edge of a page that did not belong in that drawer.

She knew it the way a woman knows a misplaced stitch in a tailored coat: not by the error itself, but by the slight wrongness of the surrounding fabric. The assay sheet was filed between two legitimate ore-yield certificates from the Boreas shaft, its ink a shade too fresh, its seal a fraction too heavy. Her fingers traced the misprint along the margin with the slow, deliberate care of a woman reading a verdict she had already anticipated. The cold brass of the cabinet bit into her palm. The recycled air tasted of oxidised iron and the faintly sweet decay of old paper, and in the amber wash of the archive's low lighting, the dust motes turned in their endless, purposeless orbits above the specimen shelves, above the basalt cores and the iron samples and the geological hammer resting in its cradle among the drill bits, as natural and unremarkable as a teacup on a saucer.

Her lips moved once. Not a word. A shape, a gesture, the ghost of a sentence she had already composed and filed in the private cabinet of her mind. The transmission to the Interplanetary Trade Council was complete in every particular: the date, the references, the specific language of contractual breach that would, within six hours, render every assay certificate issued under her consortium's seal as worthless as the red dust she could see through the small porthole at the archive's far end, that thin rust-coloured smear of a world that owed her nothing and would, if she was not careful, owe her a great deal more.

She selected three more sheets. Photographed them with the small device at her hip. Filed them back. Drew the drawer closed with a click so soft it might have been a breath.

And then the footsteps.

Not a knock. Not the measured, professional tread of a colleague entering to request a sample. The slow, deliberate cadence of a man who had already decided, who was merely walking the distance between his decision and its execution. The grated floor of the archive carried the sound with a faint metallic ring, each footfall a small, clear note in the silence, and Margaret did not turn. She stood with her back to the doorway, her hand resting on the cabinet's edge, her spine straight with the particular rigidity of a woman who has rehearsed a confrontation and expects to win it with words. She was selecting which documents to photograph next. She was, in the architecture of her mind, already composing the second transmission.

"Margaret."

The name was not a greeting. It was a boundary being drawn. She turned, and her gaze was flat, assessing, the same level look she brought to a misfiled report or a junior analyst who had confused a tonnage figure by a factor of ten. She looked at Dr. Cassius Wren as one might look at a document that had been placed in the wrong folder: with the patient, cold intention of correcting the error.

Her hands, however, did not tremble. It was his that trembled, the fine, high-frequency vibration in the tendons of his fingers as he stood in the doorway with the specimen shelf's shadow falling across his face, and it was this trembling, this small mechanical failure of the composure he had maintained for three years in boardrooms and briefing rooms and the careful, measured conversations of the station's social hours, that told her everything. He was not a man confronting an enemy. He was a man confronting a deadline that had arrived without his permission and without his consent, and the deadline was her, and the deadline was the paper in her hand, and the deadline was the six hours before the Trade Council's morning session.

"Dr. Wren," she said, and her voice was level, and in it there was no fear, no threat, no appeal to their long and complicated professional history. "You will step back from that door, and you will tell me precisely why you have followed me to this archive, because I have a great deal of work to complete and I will not be interrupted."

He did not step back. He stepped forward. His mouth opened and the grand architecture of his speech, the careful cadences of the geologist who could fill an hour's lecture with the mineralogy of the Boreas deposit, collapsed into a single clipped imperative: "The documents."

"I will not be asking you again."

"Margaret. The documents. Now."

She looked at him. She looked at the specimen shelf behind him, where the basalt cores stood in their labelled racks and the iron samples gleamed with their dull, oxidised sheen, and where the geological hammer rested in its cradle with the unremarkable stillness of a tool that had been used a hundred times and would be used a hundred more. She looked at his hands. She looked at his breathing, which was shallow and quick and entirely unlike the controlled respiration of a man who had come to a room with a question.

She did not scream. She did not call for help. She did not reach for the small device at her hip, though it was there, though the transmission was already composed, though the Trade Council was six hours away and the Interplanetary Press was one corridor away and the station was full of people who would, in the next few minutes, be walking to this very room with their own small purposes and their own small suspicions. She simply looked at him with the same flat, assessing gaze she had used a moment before, as if he were a misfiled document she was about to discard, and in that look was the true and terrible thing, the thing that was not rage or fear or even hatred but a cold, administrative recognition that what was about to happen was a matter of record, a line item in a ledger she had already balanced.

His hands stopped trembling.

The hammer came off the shelf with the soft, almost domestic sound of a tool being picked up for its intended purpose, and the act was over before the thought of it had fully formed in the room, a single swift motion that found the space between her ribs with the precise, practised accuracy of a man who had driven ten thousand core samples into Martian rock and knew, to the millimetre, where the centre of mass fell. Margaret Ashworth gasped once, a small wet sound, and the assay sheet left her fingers.

The hammer clattered against the concrete floor of the archive, rolled with a short, dry rattle, and stopped. The sheet fluttered down and settled face-up on the grated metal, the misprinted margin catching the amber light. Margaret's hand went slack at her side. The ventilation hummed. The station turned.

In the Grand Rotunda, three decks above and two corridors away, Sera Okafor set down the small folded paper against her ribs and walked toward the Stakeholders' Salon with the unhurried gait of a woman who has found what she came to find and is now merely deciding what to do with it. In the Hydroponic Conservatory, the green and humid silence held its breath around two figures who had not yet spoken a word to one another, Captain Blackwood crouched before the engineered soil with the white-knuckled focus of a man confirming a hiding place, Felix Drummond's fingers working through the loam beside him with the careful, proprietary attention of a man who owns the ground beneath his feet and intends to know what is buried in it. In the Observatorium, the crystal dome caught the station's rotation in a slow, shifting play of light, and the telescope stood empty, its brass mount bearing a smudge of red that belonged to no one on the roster and to a world that was, at this precise moment, very far away.

But in the Mining Archive, in the amber half-dark, in the silence that pressed and pressed like a thumb against a bruise, there was only the breathing. Ragged. Shallow. The particular quality of respiration in a man whose composure has not merely cracked but been driven, with the full force of a geological hammer, through the centre of

***

The smell arrived before the man. It always did, in the Hydroponic Conservatory, because the grow-lights at their full amber cycle broke down whatever cologne a man wore into something more honest and less flattering. Felix Drummond's particular brand of amber and overripe citrus, designed to suggest a man who had just stepped out of a motor lounge in the outer colonies, resolved here into the faint, cloying sweetness of burnt sugar. It sat on top of the wet peat, the chlorophyll, the low green exhalation of a hundred dwarf wheat stalks photosynthesising under artificial sun, and the whole effect was that of a very expensive perfume bottle left too long in a greenhouse.

He was already on his knees. That was the first thing one noticed, standing at the conservatory's broad glass threshold and watching the morning's traffic of the station resolve into its small, particular dramas. Felix Drummond, Senior Stakeholder of Drummond-Voss Capital Partners, was pressed into the dark engineered soil beside the third row of wheat, his thumb working a groove in the peat with the focused, almost tender attention of a man reading a letter he had written and then lost. His jacket was immaculate. His cufflinks, a small gold crest he had adjusted twice in the last minute, caught the grow-light and threw a tiny star across the leaf of a tomato plant. His hands were never still. One finger traced the rim of a drainage channel. The other smoothed a fold in his trousers that had no fold to smooth. He did not look up.

"One must be thorough, you see," he said, to no one, to the wheat, to the soil. His voice was pitched a half-tone above the register his concern would naturally occupy, as though the words had been rehearsed in a mirror and the mirror had not quite cooperated. "Thoroughness is simply good management. Margaret was a friend, after all. And one does not simply... stop looking. No. One looks. One looks and one looks and one looks until the looking becomes the thing itself." He pressed his thumb deeper. The soil gave. His eyes, however, were not looking at the soil. They were looking at the base of the irrigation manifold, where a small filing cabinet had been disguised, by some department's eccentricity, as a planter box for trailing nasturtiums. His gaze flicked there, and back, and there again, with the quick lateral motion of a man checking a number in a ledger he had not finished balancing.

He did not see Captain Blackwood enter. Or rather, he saw him, because one could not miss a man who moved through a room as though his spine were a rifle barrel, but the seeing did not register as a social event. Blackwood did not kneel. He crouched, his shoulders squared, his boots flat on the grated floor, and he began to work his way along the row of dwarf wheat with the measured, unhurried sweep of a man checking a perimeter. His fingers did not dig. They pressed. They tested. They lifted a clump of soil, turned it over, examined the underside for a seam, a hinge, a hidden compartment, and then set it back with the exacting care of a man reassembling a lock he had already picked. His face was a face that had made a decision some hours ago and was merely now executing the logistics.

The smell reached her before the glass did.

It was a small, cloying thing, the particular sweetness of sherry that had been left too long in a crystal tumbler, the alcohol evaporating in the station's thin recycled air until only the grape-sugar and the faintest bitter undercurrent remained. It clung to the rim the way a bruise clings to skin, and in the pale, sourceless light that the Ares Meridian's panels cast in perpetual, shadowless uniformity, the dried residue caught the glow and held it, and for the space of a single breath it looked like something else entirely. Something darker. Something that had once been liquid and warm and was no longer either.

Sera Okafor stood at the centre of the Stakeholders' Salon and looked at her own reflection in the curved surface of the decanter. The glass distorted her. Made her jaw wider, her eyes smaller, her mouth a thin, uncertain line that did not belong to her. She did not break eye contact with this stranger. She did not blink. The station hummed beneath her feet, that low, subsonic thrum of the rotation mechanism turning the whole structure through its slow revolution around the red planet, a sound so constant it had become indistinguishable from silence, a second heartbeat she had long since stopped hearing.

Then she reached for the glass.

Her fingers closed around the stem with the particular, unhurried care of a woman handling a thing she intended to examine and then return precisely to its resting place. Not a grip. A hold. The way one holds a scalpel. The way one holds a round before chambering it, or, more likely, after. She turned the tumbler slowly in the light, letting the residue trace the inner wall of the crystal, and she watched it the way a jeweller watches a stone for inclusions. Not with the eye. With the whole architecture of attention.

Two sets of fingerprints on the stem.

She had known this before she touched it. She had known it the way one knows the shape of a room in the dark, by the negative space, by the memory of a hand that had been there and was no longer. But knowing and confirming are different things, and Sera Okafor had spent eleven years in the field confirming, and the confirmation was what mattered now. She tilted the glass. The prints caught the light. One set was larger, the pad of the thumb pressing with the casual authority of a woman who had poured herself a drink in her own salon and set the glass down without a second thought. The other set was smaller. The fingers were slender. The pressure was lighter, more tentative, the way one holds a glass that has been placed in one's hand by another person, or the way one holds a glass one has been given and does not quite trust.

She did not gasp. She did not widen her eyes. Her jaw, if it moved at all, moved only in the faintest tightening of the masseter, the muscle working beneath the skin as though she were chewing a word she would not let herself swallow.

The residue. A faint film, almost invisible, that did not belong to sherry. She brought the glass closer to her face, not to smell it—she would not put her mouth near a thing that had been in another woman's, and she was not so foolish as to taste it, not here, not now, not with the possibility of a second set of prints on the rim that she could not account for—but to watch the way the film caught the light. A slight opalescence. A faint, almost chemical sweetness beneath the grape. A sedative, then. Not poison. Not the bitter almond she had read in the preliminary report that Inspector Voss had circulated through the station's internal channels before turning, at precisely 09:30, toward the Mining Archive, where the body of Margaret Ashworth lay in a room that smelled of geological dust and old paper and the particular, metallic tang of a life interrupted.

A sedative. Not a murder weapon. A prelude to one. Or an alternative to one.

Sera set the glass down. Not on the table. On the same surface, the same position, the same angle. She aligned it with the edge of the walnut panel the way a man aligns a chess piece, and she did not move it a fraction of a millimetre from where she had found it. Then she turned her attention to the rest of the room, and this was the part that required the most care, because the rest of the room was, by and large, unremarkable.

No overturned chairs. No shattered glass. No scuff marks of the kind that described a struggle, a dragging, a body moved by force against its will. There was a faint abrasion on the alternating walnut and birch floor near the window seat, but it was small, and it was old, and it was the kind of mark that a high heel made when a woman turned on the spot in a hurry, not the kind that described a body being pulled across a surface. The drapes hung where they had always hung. The small side table held its single, unremarkable object. The porthole showed the slow, rust-coloured curve of Mars turning below, indifferent, eternal, a planet that had no opinion on the matter of who had poured a dead woman her sherry.

The absence was the thing. The absence was the clue. A woman who had been killed in her own salon, in the company of her own drink, in the company of a friend's hand on the stem of the glass, would not have left a struggle. Would not have left a sound. Would not have left anything at all that the station's ambient hum, that low, patient, mechanical drone that was the only music aboard the Ares Meridian, would not have swallowed whole.

And somewhere, in the Hydroponic Conservatory, Dr. Tanaka was speaking quietly to Felix Drummond, and Felix Drummond was saying the words one says in such circumstances, the words that are correct and useless and do not add up, and Captain Blackwood was standing at the edge of the soil, listening, or not listening, performing the particular grief of a man who has already decided what the grief means and is merely waiting for the rest of the world to catch up. And in the Mining Archive, Inspector Voss was bending over the body of Margaret Ashworth with the methodical, unhurried attention of a woman who has seen enough dead to know that the dead do not hurry, and Dr. Wren was searching the floor around the body for a sign, any sign, that would either save him or damn him, and his hands, Sera knew, were not steady.

She did not look at the porthole. She did not need to. She knew, with the quiet, unshakeable certainty of a woman who has already written the first line of a story in the private, unspooling theatre of her own skull, that the name she was considering was not a name she would print. Not yet. Not today. Not while the glass still held two sets of fingerprints and the residue still held its faint, opalescent promise and the station still turned, and turned, and turned, carrying them all in its slow, indifferent orbit around a planet that did not care.

She reached into the breast pocket of her jacket and withdrew the small notebook, the one with the plain grey cover that no one on the station would think to look at twice because it was the kind of notebook a communications officer kept for frequency logs and shift rotations and the names of crew members who had requested a second coffee. The pen was in the back, clipped to the last page. She uncapped it. The nib touched the paper.

She wrote one line.

It was short. It was a name, and a verb, and a single, unadorned detail that would, in the hands of a lesser journalist, have been enough to fill a column. In her hands, it was a thread. A single, taut thread that, if pulled, would unravel a great deal, and if left, would remain a thread, and no one would ever know it had been there.

She looked at the line.

She crossed it out.

One stroke. Firm. The pen did not waver. The ink bled slightly into the grain of the paper, and the word beneath the single black line was gone, was illegible, was a thing that had existed for the space of three seconds and was now a secret kept between a woman and a page and the slow, patient turning of a station in the dark.

She capped the pen. She closed the notebook. She tucked it back into the breast pocket of her jacket, smoothing the fabric over it with the flat of her palm, the way one smooths a letter before sealing it, or the way one smooths a stone before setting it down in a pocket where it will be felt, constantly, as a small, hard weight against the ribs.

She turned to the decanter. The stopper was askew by a fraction. She picked it up. She set it down. The click was small, almost inaudible beneath the drone, but it was there, a clean, definitive sound, the sound of a thing made whole, made right, made as it had been before she had touched it. She stepped back. She looked at the glass one last time, the two sets of fingerprints still on the stem, the faint residue still catching the light, the small, cloying sweetness still hanging in the recycled air like a sentence no one would finish.

Then she turned and walked toward the salon's exit, her footsteps soft on the alternating walnut and birch, her shoulders level, her face set in the particular, unreadable stillness of a woman who has made a decision and will not, under any circumstances, be dissuaded from it.

In the curved surface of the decanter, her reflection followed her. Distorted. Unreadable. A stranger in a room that was not hers, looking at a glass that was not her glass, in a station that turned, and turned, and turned, carrying the dead and the living and the secrets they kept in equal, indifferent measure around the slow, red eye of a planet that had no name for any of it.

And then she was gone, and the reflection was gone, and the glass sat on its table in the pale, sourceless light, holding its two sets of fingerprints and its faint, opalescent lie, and the station hummed, and the sherry smell faded, and the silence that was not silence, that was the sound of a machine turning in the dark, went on.

The compass was small. That was the first thing to notice, and the second, and the third. Small, green with the particular verdigris of age, its case engraved with a logo Wren recognised as the Ashworth Mining Consortium's crest—a pick and a gear interlocked in a circle. Its needle was frozen, pointing in a direction that meant nothing in a station that orbited a single point. A compass. In a station that orbits a single point.

It sat wedged between Margaret Ashworth's thumb and forefinger, the metal cold against the dead skin, the fingers still curled with the faint, involuntary tenacity of a hand that had gripped something in the last second of its usefulness. Wren knelt beside it. His charcoal trousers caught a faint smear of something dark on the Archive's polished floor, and he did not look down at the trousers. He did not need to. He knew what the smear was. He had been in this room. He had touched the filing cabinet, the display board, the edge of the desk where the woman's notebook lay open to a page of figures he had not meant to read but had read, because the figures were his, because the figures were the ones that would unmake him.

The fluorescent panels above hummed their flat, insectine hum. The station turned beneath them, a slow and imperceptible roll that lived in the soles of the feet rather than the eyes, a vibration in the alternating walnut and birch floor that Wren felt through his kneecaps. The smell was old paper and cold metal and something else, something faintly sweet and organic that he refused to identify. The scuff mark on the floor—item thirty-five, if he were cataloguing, which he was not, which he was—ran in a short, ragged line from the filing cabinet to the desk, and Wren's eyes tracked it the way a man tracks a crack in a foundation he has already decided to ignore.

"Dr. Wren."

The voice came from behind him, and it was not a question. It was a name, spoken with the particular flatness of a woman who had already decided what the answer would be and was merely confirming the shape of it. Inspector Elara Voss stood at the mouth of the Archive, her posture so still that for a moment Wren thought she had been there all along, that the space behind the filing cabinet had simply produced her. She did not pace. She did not frown. Her eyes were on the body, and then on Wren, and then on the body again, in that slow, measuring way that suggested she was not looking at the dead woman at all but at the geometry of the room around her.

"Where were you when the alarm sounded?"

The question was short. The pause that followed it was not. Wren felt the silence fill the Archive, felt the hum of the panels and the turn of the station and the faint tick of the filing cabinet's key, still in its cylinder, still warm from the hand that had turned it. He straightened his spine. He smoothed the front of his shirt with a gesture that was, he knew, a gesture, and he said, "In the archive. Reviewing the quarterly assay reports. The ones from the restricted shaft."

"Yes." Voss said the single syllable with the weight of a period at the end of a sentence that had not yet been written. "And what did you find in those reports?"

"Anomalies. Minor. The kind one expects when the sampling equipment drifts." He said it evenly. He said it the way one says a diagnosis to a colleague in a ward where the patient is no longer in the ward. "I was preparing a correction memo when the alarm sounded."

Voss did not blink. This was the thing about her that Wren had learned to respect and to dread in equal measure: the absence of the expected micro-expression. No nod. No shift of the weight. She simply looked at him for a duration that was, by any ordinary measure, an excess, and then she looked at the body, and then at the filing cabinet with its fresh scratch on the lock, and then at the small brass compass in the dead woman's hand.

She knelt. It was a slow, deliberate lowering, the knees bending in a way that suggested a woman who had knelt beside many bodies and had not yet found one that surprised her. Her fingers did not touch the compass. She held her hand above it, two centimetres of air between her fingertips and the green-tinged brass, and she looked at it. The light in the Archive was different from where Wren had seen it, or perhaps it was the same light and the angle was not. The compass, from this lower vantage, seemed smaller still. The needle, frozen at its meaningless bearing, caught the fluorescent glow and threw a single thin line of white across Margaret Ashworth's cheekbone. The engraving on the case was less legible from here, the pick and the gear blurred into a suggestion, a signature rather than a statement. And the hand holding it—the fingers were not quite as curled as they had been a moment ago, or perhaps they were, and the dead muscles had simply shifted in the cold, and the compass had slipped a millimetre, and that millimetre was the difference between a woman checking her bearing and a woman reaching for something she could not name.

"It is a compass," Voss said. Not a question. An observation filed under a heading that had not yet been created. "In a station that orbits a single point."

She looked up at Wren. Her eyes were level, and in them was not suspicion, exactly, but the administrative patience of a woman who had already opened a folder and was waiting for the name to be written in the margin.

"Murder," she said, and the word was flat, clinical, a word one might use to describe the weather. "I will need you to remain here until I have completed my initial examination. Do not touch anything. Do not leave the corridor."

"I understand."

"You do not understand. You will understand when I have finished." She stood. Her knees did not crack. She walked to the filing cabinet, ran her finger along the fresh scratch on the lock, and then withdrew her hand as though the metal had been warmer than it was. She did not touch the key.

The door to the Archive opened, and the room filled with the smell of sherry and the particular, expensive cologne that Felix Drummond wore like a second skin, the one that cut through the metallic tang of the station's recycled air and announced him three paces before his body arrived. He was talking already, to no one in particular, his words a rapid, slightly slurred stream that did not quite form sentences. "—terrible, absolutely terrible, and I want to say that I was in the conservatory, Tanaka will confirm, I was in the conservatory until the alarm, and then I came, I came straight here, and I want to say that I—"

"Felix." Voss's voice cut through the cologne with the precision of a scalpel through gauze. "Sit down."

He did not sit down. He stood in the middle of the Archive with his hands clasped behind his back and his eyes moving in a quick, darting pattern from the body to the filing cabinet to the door, and then to Wren, and then to his wrist, where his watch was ticking, and he checked the time. He checked it again. The minutes on the face of the watch were not minutes. They were a transfer window. They were a number that, once it passed,
