The Observatorium held its silence the way a held breath holds the shape of the lungs that made it—tight, rounded, and full of a pressure that, if released, would shatter the careful architecture of the room. Dr. Cassius Wren stood at the far end of the instrument, where the great refractor's brass housing curved upward like the flank of some slumbering animal, and he did not look through the eyepiece. He did not look at Mars. He looked down.

His right hand, the one that had been steady enough to calibrate spectrographic readings for eleven years aboard this station, was pressed flat against the alternating walnut and birch tiles at the telescope's base. The cold of the metal grating beneath the flooring seeped through the thin wool of his sleeve and settled in the metacarpal bones of his palm as though it intended to remain there. He swept his fingers forward, then back, then forward again, in the short, repetitive arc of a man reading Braille on a surface that had not been written in any language he could name. He was feeling for the ghost of a heel. For the drag. For the particular pressure-pattern that a body makes when it is moved against the grain of the floor, when the weight of it is not carried by its own two feet but by the hands of another person who is, in that moment, deciding very quickly that the weight must be moved.

Nothing. Or rather, something. A faintness. A roughness in the birch grain that might have been the tile's own manufacturing tolerance, or might have been the scuff of a shoe sole dragged three, four inches across the surface before being lifted. His thumb pressed into the roughness. He pressed it again. He pressed it a third time, and the pressure of his own thumbnail into the wood sent a small, sharp pain up his forearm that he welcomed, because the pain was a fact, and the fact was that he was here, and the fact was that the roughness was here, and the fact was that he had not, in the four minutes since he had set the body down in the far alcove behind the specimen shelf, managed to make the roughness go away.

He withdrew his hand. He looked at his palm. The skin was unmarked, the way it had been unmarked when he had crouched, and this was, he told himself in the flat, administrative tone he reserved for quarterly reports, satisfactory. He turned his attention upward, to the telescope itself. His fingers found the brass housing where it met the equatorial mount, and he traced the seam with his index finger, slow, deliberate, the way one traces a sentence in a contract for the clause that might, in the event of a dispute, become the only clause that mattered. The brass was warm from the station's internal heating, and the warmth was wrong, because the Observatorium was not heated; the station's thermal regulation did not extend to the dome, and the brass should have been cold, should have been the temperature of the void pressed against the glass above, and the fact that it was warm meant that it had been handled recently, by hands that had been warm, by hands that had been gripping, and the gripping had left its heat in the metal the way a fist leaves its shape in wet clay.

He ran his thumb along the lower rail of the mount, where the brass met the steel base plate. A scratch. A hairline, barely visible, running parallel to the seam. He tilted his head. He tilted it again. In the pale, sourceless light that filtered through the dome's glass, the scratch was ambiguous. It could be a manufacturing mark. It could be the edge of a geological sample pressed against the housing during a routine calibration. It could be the tip of a brass compass case, its mining-company engraving catching the metal as a hand, a dead hand, had been wrenched from around the instrument's housing in the final, brief, and terrible moment when the body had been more than a colleague and less than a problem to be solved. He touched the scratch with his fingertip. He withdrew his finger. He did not touch it again.

In the Grand Rotunda, three corridors away and one spoke-rotation to the left, Dr. Yuki Tanaka was doing the same thing with a different set of hands. Her knuckles were white against the brass railing, her fingers probing the joints where the rail met the baluster, looking for a seam, a compartment, a hidden hinge. She had not found one. She would not find one. But the searching was the point. The searching was the prayer. And below, in the Mining Archive, Felix Drummond had lowered his voice to the particular register of a man who is not confessing but is, in the architecture of his sentences, building a confession for someone else to hear, and Sera Okafor was listening with the stillness of a woman who has already written the article in her head and is now merely waiting for the subject to confirm the headline, and Captain Blackwood stood behind them both with his hands clasped at his lower back in the posture of a man who has already decided what he will say and is waiting for the precise moment to say it.

And Inspector Elara Voss was walking. Not quickly. Not slowly. With the particular, unhurried purpose of a woman who knows that the room she is walking toward will contain exactly the information she requires, and that the information will not move, will not change, will not become more or less damning by the speed of her approach. Her footsteps on the Spoke Gallery's polished floor were the only sound in the corridor, and they were measured, and they were final, and they were, in their evenness, a kind of verdict.

But in the Observatorium, there was no sound but the hum. The station's rotation pressed against the glass dome with a frequency so low it was felt rather than heard, a vibration in the molars, in the sternum, in the small bones of the inner ear that made the world tilt by a fraction of a degree and then settle, and settle, and settle, the slow, red eye of Mars turning below and above and around, indifferent to the small warm room where a man was examining a floor tile as though it had testified against him.

Wren's hands moved to the eyepiece. He did not look through it. He ran the pad of his thumb along the rim, feeling for the faint residue of skin oils, for the particular smudge that a second pair of eyes would have left, and finding only the clean, cold brass that he himself had polished that morning, before the morning had become the rest of the morning, before the file cabinet in the Mining Archive had been opened, before the geological hammer had been set down on the specimen shelf with a sound like a period at the end of a very long, very bad sentence. His other hand found the focus wheel and turned it a quarter rotation, listening to the internal gears, checking that they moved freely, that nothing was jammed, that the mechanism was as it should be, as it had been, as it would be after he had confirmed that it was as it should be. The gears moved. They were clean. They were, he told himself, clean.

He straightened. The motion took a moment, a creak in the small of his back that reminded him he was fifty-three, that his knees had begun to argue with him in the cold, that the body which had performed the act he was now auditing was the same body that ached in the rain and stiffened in the dark and would, in the end, fail in the way all bodies fail, and this thought, this small and irrelevant thought, struck him with a force disproportionate to its content, as though the universe had decided, in the quiet of the Observatorium, to remind him that he was meat, that he was temporal, that the brass would outlast him and the tile would outlast him and the scuff mark on the birch would outlast him, and that none of these things cared.

He wiped his right palm on the thigh of his trousers. The wool absorbed the faint moisture, the trace of floor-dust, the nothing that was not nothing. He turned. The telescope's great objective lens faced him, a dark circle of glass in its brass housing, and in that glassy, inverted image he saw himself. His own face. The face he could not control.

For one beat—two, perhaps, though it felt longer, felt like the space between one heartbeat and the next when the heart has forgotten its rhythm—his jaw tightened. The quick-tempered flush rose at his temples, that old blood, the blood that had made him snap at junior technicians, that had made him slam the door of the Mining Archive, that had made his hand close around the handle of the hammer with a grip that was not, in the architecture of the moment, a grip but a decision, a decision so fast and so total that the hand had moved before the mind had finished forming the sentence. The flush crept up toward his hairline. His eyes, in the lens, were very still. Very wide. The eyes of a man who has looked at the bottom of a well and is now calculating, with the precise arithmetic of a man who has spent his life calculating ore yields and mineral compositions and the exact percentage of iron in a sample, whether the well is deep enough, whether the bottom is solid, whether he can walk away from the edge without looking down.

Then the flush receded. The jaw unclenched. The eyes narrowed to the narrow, assessing slits of the Chief Geologist, the man who had presented his findings to the Interplanetary Trade Council with a confidence so total, so unassailable, that the Council had signed, and the Consortium had funded, and the colony had broken ground, and all of it had been, in the architecture of the truth, a number that did not add up, a column in a ledger that, once it passed a certain threshold, could not be un-passed. He straightened his collar. He adjusted the cuff of his left sleeve, a small, precise motion, the motion of a man putting on a face.

"Nothing," he said. The word was very quiet. It was not a question. It was not a conclusion. It was a statement of fact, offered to the room, to the telescope, to the tile, to the brass, to the small warm air that held the smell of machine oil and the faint, metallic tang of the dome's glass. "The room is clean."

His eyes moved. They found the scuff. The faint, ambiguous roughness in the birch grain, three inches from the telescope's base, where a heel had been dragged, or had not been dragged, or had been dragged by a shoe that was no longer in the room, on a foot that was no longer moving, in a body that was no longer, in any meaningful sense, a body but a shape, a weight, a problem that had been solved with a particular and irrevocable thoroughness. He looked at it. He looked at it for one second longer than a man looks at a manufacturing tolerance. One second longer than a man looks at a smudge on a lens. One second longer than a man looks at anything that is not, in the architecture of his careful, ordered world, a thing that should be there.

He turned. His footsteps on the alternating walnut and birch were soft, measured, the same measured cadence as Inspector Voss's in the corridor, the same purposeful evenness, as though the two of them were walking to the same destination, to the same verdict, to the same small, warm room where a man or a woman would stand and look at the floor and decide, with the full and terrible precision of a mind that has no other option, that the floor was clean, that the brass was

The brass railing was cold in a way that had nothing to do with temperature. Dr. Yuki Tanaka knew this the way one knows the particular chill of a leaf's underside in a greenhouse—by the quality of the resistance it offers to the fingertip, by the faint, almost sub-audible hum that vibrates up through the knuckle and into the wrist. She traced the railing's length with the pad of her index finger, slow and patient, reading the metal the way she read the venation of a *Nepenthes* leaf: searching for a seam, a joint, a hairline fracture that might indicate a concealed compartment. There was none. The brass was seamless, unbroken, and in its unbrokenness it disappointed her with a quiet, almost apologetic thoroughness that she did not quite understand, but which she filed away in the same careful drawer where she kept the names of every species she had catalogued in the Hydroponic Conservatory over the past eleven months.

Above her, the chandelier swayed. It was not a dramatic motion—nothing so theatrical as a pendulum's arc. It was the slow, hypnotic oscillation of the station's rotation, a gentle lurch of perhaps two degrees to the left and two degrees back, carrying with it a wash of pale light across the Rotunda's vaulted ceiling and a corresponding shadow that swept the polished floor in long, unhurried strokes. It was, Tanaka thought, a metronome. A very slow one. One that kept time for a room in which no music was being played.

She was still tracing the railing when the footsteps came. They were even in their cadence, neither hurried nor languid, and they stopped precisely four paces from where she stood. Inspector Elara Voss did not announce herself. She simply stood there, her hands resting at her sides, her expression carrying the particular blankness of a woman who has already decided what she will say and is now merely waiting for the other person to finish their sentence.

"Inspector," Tanaka said, and her voice was soft, almost apologetic, as though she were intruding on a private conversation by asking a question. "I just arrived from the Hydroponic Conservatory. I heard about the discovery. Can you tell me what happened here?"

Voss looked at her. Then she looked at the railing. Then she looked at the leather armchair in the centre of the Rotunda, where the cushion lay askew, its stuffing slightly displaced, as though someone had sat upon it and then, in some manner not yet determined, departed in a hurry. "Margaret Ashworth was found in the Observatorium," Voss said. "At approximately 09:40. The cause is not yet confirmed, but it is not natural." A pause. The chandelier swayed. "You are the Chief Botanist. You were in the Conservatory at the time."

"I was."

"Alone?"

"I was." Tanaka's fingers had gone still on the brass. She did not look at the cushion. She did not need to. She had already seen it from the corridor, and the sight of it—the way the fabric bunched and the seam split just slightly along the lower edge—had registered in her mind with the same quiet, clinical precision with which she noted a wilted petal or a misaligned graft. It was a fact. It was, she suspected, of some interest. But she said nothing more than that, and she let the silence settle between them like sediment in still water.

It was into this silence that Felix Drummond arrived.

He arrived the way a weather system arrives: all at once, and with a pressure change that one felt before one saw. The sharp, citrus-and-amber bite of his cologne cut through the Rotunda's recycled air with an almost aggressive sweetness, and he was already extending his hand, already speaking, already performing. "Inspector. Dr. Tanaka. I am most distressed, truly most distressed, by what has happened. I was in the Mining Archive, you understand, reviewing the quarterly projections, and I must say the implications for the consortium's dividend structure are—" He checked his watch. "—quite, quite troubling. I was only just finishing a column of figures when the alert came through, and I confess I have not yet had time to—" He checked his watch again, the same watch, in the space of a single sentence. "—to fully appreciate the magnitude of the loss."

His grip on Tanaka's hand, when it came, was slightly too firm. His tie, a shade of cerulean that seemed to insist upon its own brightness, sat a fraction of a degree askew, as though it had been adjusted in haste. He did not look at the cushion. He did not look at the railing. He looked at Voss, and he kept looking at Voss, and his eyes had the particular brightness of a man who is reciting a text he has memorised and is waiting, with some urgency, for the other person to signal that the recitation has reached its satisfactory conclusion.

"The financial records," he said, and the word *financial* fell from his lips with the careful, practiced weight of a man placing a stone on a scale. "I want to make it entirely clear that Drummond-Voss Capital Partners has no interest in the circumstances of the death. Our concern is, and has always been, the stability of the projections. The fluctuations in the ore-yield certificates, the—"

"Felix." Voss's voice was not loud. It did not need to be. It carried the same flat, level quality as the station's artificial gravity: constant, inescapable, and utterly indifferent to one's preference. "The fluctuations can wait."

Felix's hand, which had been gesturing toward the Mining Archive, toward the ledgers, toward the vast and reassuring architecture of compound interest, stopped mid-air. He let it fall. He smoothed his tie. He did not smooth it again.

Tanaka, meanwhile, had turned back to the railing. Her fingertip had found it: a single black hair, caught in the brass where the metal curved into the baluster's base. It was a very small thing. It was the sort of thing that one noticed and then did not notice, that registered in the periphery of the eye and then slipped away, like a word on the tip of the tongue. It did not match the hair of the woman who had been found in the Observatorium. Tanaka knew this with the same quiet certainty with which she knew that a particular shade of green in the Conservatory belonged to a *Ficus* and not a *Monstera*. She said, in her soft, apologetic voice, "Inspector, I believe this is of some interest." She did not point. She did not need to. She simply let her fingertip rest, very lightly, on the strand of hair, as though it were a specimen she had just pinned to a board and was now, with great care, stepping back to assess.

Voss looked at the hair. Voss looked at the cushion. Voss looked at Felix, who was still standing in the centre of the Rotunda with his hands clasped before him and his cologne spreading, patient and pervasive, through the still air. "Dr. Tanaka," she said, "thank you. I will note it." And then, to no one in particular, to the room itself, to the slow swaying of the chandelier above: "The cushion was disturbed. The hair is not the victim's. Someone was in this room, or in this chair, after the time of death and before I arrived." She paused. The chandelier swayed. "I am not yet in a position to say who."

Felix made a small sound. It was not quite a gasp and not quite a sigh. It was the sound a man makes when a wall he has been building, brick by careful brick, develops a crack in the third course. "But the records," he said. "The quarterly figures. Surely the priority is—"

"The priority," Voss said, "is the body. The records will still be there tomorrow. The body will not." She turned. Her movement was economical, without wasted gesture. She walked toward the corridor that led to the Observatorium, and her footsteps on the alternating walnut and birch were the same measured, even cadence they had been when she first entered, as though the four minutes of conversation had not altered the rhythm of her gait by so much as a fraction of a beat.

Felix reached for her hand. His fingers were already extended, the grip already forming, the cologne already rising to meet her. But Voss did not stop. She did not slow. She walked on, and her hand did not take his, and his fingers closed around nothing but the faint, recycled air of the Rotunda, and they stayed there, slightly curled, slightly open, a handshake offered to a woman who was already four paces away and then eight and

The first thing one noticed, in the absence of all other sound, was the whisper.

It was a small sound, barely a sound at all—the soft, dry friction of knuckles drawn slowly through the knotted silk of the Persian carpet, the pile parting and resettling like a curtain drawn across a window. In the Stakeholders' Salon, with its crystal decanters catching the thin, rust-coloured light that filtered through the portholes from the Martian sky beyond, with its long glass display cases and its low, architectural silence, the sound was almost obscene in its intimacy. A man's knuckles, scraping. The particular creak of a joint that had not been bent in that direction since morning. And beneath it, so distant and so muffled that it might have been the building of the mind itself, the deep, rhythmic cycling of the station's life-support, the great lungs of the Ares Meridian drawing and releasing the same recycled air over and over, a sound that had become, for those who lived aboard, as unremarkable as one's own pulse.

Dr. Cassius Wren was on his knees.

This was, in itself, a fact of such quiet enormity that it seemed to alter the geometry of the room. The charcoal trousers at the knees were darkening, the fine wool pressed flat against the silk pile, and his shoulders were drawn inward with a tension that was not, he would have insisted, physical. Professional, perhaps. The shoulders of a man reviewing a specimen under magnification. The shoulders of a man who had spent twenty-two years in geological laboratories, crouching over thin-sections of basalt and iron-oxide, who had never once in his career permitted himself to kneel on a carpet in a room built for the display of other people's wealth. And yet. And yet his fingers were in the pile, working, parting the tight knots of the silk with a care that was almost tender, almost surgical, and his jaw was working silently beneath the stubble of a morning's growth, and his eyes—his eyes did not look at the carpet. His eyes tracked the far wall. The porthole. The door. In that order. In that order. In that order.

He had checked the doorway twice before he knelt. Not once, not twice, but twice in the specific, deliberate manner of a man who is not checking for a person but checking for the *possibility* of a person, the theoretical silhouette that might materialize from the corridor's recycled air and see what he was doing, and the difference between those two checks was the difference between a professional verifying a lock and a man verifying that the world had not, in the last eleven minutes, produced a witness.

The metallic tang of the station's air sat at the back of his throat. Old wool. Synthetic dyes. The particular, faintly sweet rot of the carpet's under-padding, a smell that would have been invisible in a laboratory but here, in the silence, in the absence of brandy and conversation and the clink of glasses, was a smell one could almost taste. He pressed his fingers deeper. The pile was cool against his palms, cool in the specific way that a Persian silk is cool when it has been undisturbed for hours, when the air conditioning has settled into a uniform temperature that the human body registers as a kind of absence. His fingers moved in a pattern. Not random. Not the flailing search of a man looking for a lost key. A pattern. A grid. Left to right, then a half-inch shift downward, then left to right again. The pattern of a man who had, in the hours before the murder, in the hours when he had been standing in this very room with Margaret Ashworth's wine glass still warm in her hand and the faint, bitter almond on the rim, *known* where the documents were. Knew the exact square inch of carpet where he had pressed the edge of the assay report against the pile, where the silk had caught the corner of the paper, where a single fibre might have transferred, where a single drop—

No. Not a drop. He had been careful. He had been, in the language of his own professional review, *adequate*. The word sat in his mind with the flat, unsentimental precision of a grade assigned to a junior analyst's field report. *Adequate. Sufficient. Within acceptable parameters.* As though the murder were a measurement. As though Margaret Ashworth's silence, her stillness, the particular angle of her head where he had set it—

He did not finish the thought. He could not. The thought, once completed, would have a weight, and the weight would settle on his shoulders, and his shoulders were already carrying the full, specific architecture of the room's silence, and he could not—

His fingers found a knot that was slightly raised. A single thread, pulled free, catching under his thumbnail. He examined it. Silk. The carpet's own silk. He released it. He moved on.

In the Grand Rotunda, three corridors away, Dr. Yuki Tanaka stood very still beside the armchair, her hands clasped before her in the precise, almost liturgical manner she adopted when she was thinking, and Inspector Elara Voss knelt on the alternating walnut and birch floor with her fingers spread beneath the chair's front leg, feeling for the grain of the wood, for the faintest indentation, the smallest displacement of a single thread of upholstery. The hidden stiletto was already in the evidence bag. The question that remained, the question that Voss was asking the floor with her fingertips, was whether there had been a second object. A second weapon. A second set of hands. Her knuckles were pale. Her breathing was even. She did not look up.

And in the Mining Archive, where the filing cabinets stood in their long, institutional row and the air smelled of old paper and the particular mineral tang of the ore samples sealed in their display cases, Captain Blackwood stood with his back to the nearest cabinet, his posture rigid, his voice pitched at the exact frequency of a man delivering a eulogy he had not rehearsed. Sera Okafor stood three paces from him, her pen still in her hand, the tip dry, the notebook in her other hand open to a page she was not reading. And Felix Drummond, who had been standing between them with his cologne still sharp in the recycled air, his tie slightly askew, his expression fixed in the particular mask of a man performing financial grief for an audience of two, turned and walked. Not quickly. Not slowly. With the measured, purposeful cadence of a man who has decided, in the space of three sentences, that the Archive is no longer a safe room, and that the Grand Rotunda, where the Inspector is on her knees and the Botanist is standing very still, is—

Wren's fingers found the edge of the carpet.

He did not pull. He did not yank. He placed both palms flat on the silk, felt the weight of the under-padding, the thickness of the foundation, and then, with the slow, deliberate pressure of a man lifting a stone from a garden bed, he drew the corner upward. The silk parted with a soft, dry sound. Beneath it: the station's flooring. Smooth. Unmarked. The pale, composite surface of the Ares Meridian's interior, clean, seamless, bearing no trace of a torn page, no smear of blood, no dark fibre caught in a seam. Nothing.

He looked. He looked for the length of a full breath, two full breaths, the space in which the life-support cycled once and the Martian light shifted a fraction of degree across the porthole glass. Nothing. The floor was the floor. The carpet was the carpet. The absence was total, and the total absence was—

He replaced the corner.

His fingers worked the silk back into its original position with a precision that was not, could not have been, the precision of a man who had been searching. It was the precision of a man who had been *placing*. Who had, in some other hour, in some other configuration of the room, pressed this very corner down and smoothed this very thread and thought, *this is adequate, this is sufficient, the parameters are within tolerance.* And now he was smoothing it again, and the smoothing was the same motion, the same pressure, the same quiet, and the fact that the motion was identical to the motion of the hiding was the fact that his hands knew and his mind had not yet permitted itself to register.

He stood.

The movement was slow. The knees creaked in a way that was, at fifty-three, a small and private indignity, and the charcoal trousers were dark at the knees, and his shoulders were still drawn in, still carrying the architecture of the silence, and his hand—his right hand, the hand that had done the searching, the hand that had part the silk and pressed the knots and found the single raised thread—his hand trembled. A small tremor. A single, brief oscillation, the kind that a man in a laboratory would attribute to a minor spasm, a transient neurological event, nothing requiring notation in a field report. He caught it. He closed his fingers. He tucked the hand into the pocket of his lab coat with the flat, functional motion of a man stowing a tool.

He straightened his tie.

The glass display case to his left caught the Martian light and threw it back in a long, thin band across the floor, and in the reflected surface he saw himself. The jaw. The stubble. The shoulders. The particular, hollow set of the eyes. He straightened the knot. He exhaled. The breath was visible for a fraction of a second in the cool, recycled air, a small, private cloud, and then it was gone, absorbed, indistinguishable from the rest.

He did not move. He stood in the band of light with his hand in his pocket and his eyes on the far wall, and the silence of the Stakeholders' Salon pressed in on him from every side, the silence of a room that had held a woman's last conversation and a man

The Grand Rotunda held its silence the way a held breath holds the lungs, a low and almost subliminal pressure against the eardrums, the station's rotation a thrum felt more in the sternum than heard by the ear. The artificial amber light pooled on the curved floor in slow, viscous shapes, like old honey spread by an unhurried hand, and the recycled air carried that faint metallic sweetness one came to associate with enclosed space, with the knowledge that no window here opened onto a sky, no corridor led to a field, no door swung out onto a world beyond the next set of sealed panels. It was, in the most literal sense, a room with no outside. And in such a room, every word spoken was a word that could not be unsaid.

Felix Drummond's left hand rose to his wrist. The thumb pressed the bezel of his watch with a small, deliberate click. The wrist flicked back. He checked it again. And again. Four times in the span of a single minute, or perhaps two; the measurement of time in the Rotunda was a matter of the amber light's unchanging gradient and the distant, muffled pulse of the rotation. His right hand, meanwhile, gestured broadly at nothing in particular, at the curved wall, at the air, at the general concept of the room, as though the space itself required a polite introduction before one might address the matter at hand. The cologne arrived before the voice. Jasmine and amber, deployed in such quantity that it filled the narrow corridor between the two men like a hand pressed over a mouth, cloying and insistent, a fragrance that announced its presence with the confidence of a man who had not quite decided whether he was a guest or an accuser.

"Dr. Wren." The title landed with a careful, over-pronounced weight, the sort of emphasis a man applies to a word he has rehearsed in the corridor outside, testing its shape against the teeth. "Dr. Wren, I do hope the morning has been—how shall I put it—*conducive* to reflection."

Dr. Cassius Wren stood near the brass railing, his hands open and resting on its surface as though he were addressing a lecture hall of two hundred undergraduates rather than a single, cologne-drenched stakeholder in a sealed room. His posture was expansive, unhurried, the particular stillness of a man who has decided that movement would be a concession. But the knuckles of his right hand, where they gripped the railing's edge, were the pale, bloodless white of a man holding himself in check. He did not fidget. He did not shift his weight. He simply stood, and the standing was the performance.

"Felix," Wren said, and the name carried the easy warmth of a colleague, a friend, a man who had attended the same dinners and signed the same colonial charters. "You look as though you have been walking the spoke gallery in circles. Is there a matter that requires my particular attention?"

The question was open. The question was a door. And Felix Drummond, who had not entered the Rotunda without first composing in his mind a sequence of words that would sound like concern and not like threat, stepped through it with the eager, slightly stumbling grace of a man who has rehearsed the first sentence and not the second.

"Well, you see, it is rather this," he began, and his hands came up, both of them, palms open, a gesture of innocent disclosure. "The numbers. The quarterly figures. I have been looking at them, Dr. Wren, and I confess I find them—" He paused. He smiled. The smile was too bright, too wide, the smile of a man who has decided that brightness is a deflection. "Let us say that the *accuracy* of the figures is a matter of some interest to me. I do suspect there may be, shall we say, certain fluctuations in the market that have not been—how does one put this—*fully accounted for*."

Wren's jaw worked once. A single, small movement, the sort of thing a man does when a word has touched a nerve he did not know was exposed. He removed his right hand from the railing and replaced it with his left, as though the brass had grown warm, as though the metal might retain the impression of his grip.

"Fluctuations," Wren repeated. The word was flat, clinical, a geologist naming a mineral. "You refer to the ore-yield projections?"

"I refer to the *numbers*," Drummond said, and the emphasis on the second syllable was the emphasis of a man who has decided that precision is a shield. "The numbers, Dr. Wren. The ones that, if one were to—pardon me—*add them up*—might not, shall we say, arrive at the figure one expects. I am merely a shareholder with a rather fondness for arithmetic. A tedious habit, I am told." He laughed. The laugh was short, clipped, a sound that ended before it had quite begun.

In the corner of the Rotunda, where the amber light thinned to a pale, almost grey wash, Dr. Yuki Tanaka had been standing for what might have been a full minute, her attention fixed on the brass railing with the quiet, methodical attention she gave to everything, the way one studies a leaf for the pattern of its veins. She had not spoken. She had not needed to. Her presence in the room had been a kind of witness, a third point in a triangle that the other two men had not quite acknowledged. Now, as Drummond's voice filled the space with its careful, over-eager cadence, she turned. Her movement was small, almost apologetic, the soft rustle of her lab coat against the curved wall.

"I must attend to the seedlings," she said. The words were not a lie so much as a careful omission, a door closing quietly behind a woman who was walking toward a vault she intended to open and from which she intended to take what was not hers. "The germination cycle is—" She stopped. She did not finish the sentence. It did not need finishing. "I shall be in the Conservatory."

She walked to the corridor. Her footsteps on the alternating walnut and birch were soft, measured, the same measured cadence one might give to a garden path. She did not look back. The corridor swallowed her, and the Rotunda was, once again, a room of two men and a silence that had not been there a moment before.

Wren watched her go. His eyes tracked the line of her retreating figure with the particular attention of a man cataloguing a threat, noting the angle of her shoulders, the way her hands were already moving, already counting, already *taking stock* of something that was not in this room. Then his gaze returned to Drummond, and the expression on his face was the expression of a lecturer who has just been asked a question he did not expect, and who has decided, in the space of a single breath, that the answer must be *almost* true.

"Felix," he said, and his voice had the same open, unhurried quality, the same warmth, but something beneath it had shifted, a tectonic plate settling a fraction of an inch. "You are a careful man. I admire that. But I would ask you, gently, to consider that the figures you have been examining are the figures I have been *producing*. The ore-yield certificates, the assay reports, the projections for the restricted shaft. They are not, I would submit, a matter of *fluctuation*. They are a matter of *measurement*. And measurement, as you well know, is a discipline that tolerates a certain degree of—" He paused. He chose the next word with the care of a man selecting a reagent. "—*interpretation*."

Drummond's smile did not waver. It widened, if anything, the too-bright smile of a man who has heard the word he was fishing for and who now feels the line go taut in his hands. "Interpretation," he echoed. "Yes. I do suspect the reports might be, shall we say, *manipulated*." The word came out with a shrug, a full-shouldered, casual shrug, as though the word were a harmless butterfly pinned to a board, a specimen of no particular danger. He checked his watch. The smudge was no larger than a lentil.

It sat upon the cold brass of the telescope's mounting ring, a single grain of rust-coloured dust that might have been a speck of iron oxide from the station's own corroded fittings, might have been a flake of dried paint from the last maintenance cycle, might have been anything at all that a sensible person would brush away with the pad of a thumb and forget. The Observatorium was cool and faintly metallic, the air carrying the smell of old brass and the faint green ghost of chlorophyll that crept in from the hydroponic vents three decks below, and the crystal dome above admitted a thin, blood-orange light from the world of Mars pressing its red face against the glass. Every surface in the room wore that faint rust tint, so that the smudge on the brass was nearly invisible, a detail that would have vanished into the general amber wash of the afternoon.

Inspector Elara Voss did not brush it away.

She tilted her head the way a woman tilts hers when a sentence in a letter is missing one crucial word, when the grammar is correct but the meaning is not. Her fingertips, which had been tracing the velvet drape behind the telescope with the unhurried patience of a woman reading braille, came to rest on the brass ring itself. She did not touch the smudge. She held her finger a quarter-inch above it, as though the grain might evaporate at the warmth of her skin. The station hummed its low, perpetual rotation beneath her feet, a vibration so constant that one ceased to feel it until one pressed one's palm flat against the floor and remembered that the world was turning.

"It should not be here," she said. The words were addressed to no one. The Observatorium was empty save for the telescope, the drapes, the narrow bench of polished oak, and the particular quality of silence that belongs to a room where no one has spoken in some hours. She turned the telescope's azimuth ring a fraction of a degree, and the smudge did not move. It was not a loose particle. It was embedded, worked into the grain of the brass with the intimacy of a fingerprint, as though a thumb had pressed it there deliberately, or as though the hand that carried it had been open, and the dust had simply fallen, settled, and stayed.

Red Martian dust. On a sealed station. In a room that had not been opened to the void in four years.

She set her hand flat on the telescope's housing and felt the cold of the metal through her glove, and she was thinking, with the quiet precision of a woman who has catalogued two hundred and seventeen crimes in three continents, that a sealed environment does not accumulate foreign particulate. The ventilation system was closed-loop. The airlock cycled through three stages of filtration. For that grain to be here, it must have been brought here by a hand, or a garment, or a boot, and that hand, that garment, that boot must have been in contact with the surface of Mars, or with a container that had been, and the person who carried it must have been, at some point, on the ground of that red world.

She was still standing there, her shadow long and thin across the oak bench, when the door at the far end of the Observatorium opened with the soft pneumatic sigh of a pressure seal releasing, and Captain Theron Blackwood entered.

He did not walk so much as arrive, as though the space between the corridor and the room had simply collapsed. He was stillness made flesh, the uniform immaculate, the collar turned to a precise angle that suggested a compass bearing rather than a fashion choice. His eyes moved across the room in a single, economical sweep—the telescope, the drapes, the bench, the dome—and settled on the inspector.

"You found something," he said. Not a question. Six words.

The drop sat on the rim of the fountain like a single seed fallen from a branch not yet visible, a bead of crimson so perfect in its curvature that it might have been pressed there by a careful hand rather than shed by a wound. It caught the green-filtered light that fell through the conservatory's translucent panels and held it, a small dark planet suspended in the white ceramic's pale glow. There was no pool. There was no spray. There was no evidence of a struggle, of a fall, of the messy and undignified business of a body meeting the floor. Only that one drop, impossibly deliberate in its isolation, as though the fountain had been the last thing to touch a living body, and the body had walked on, upright, still breathing, still carrying the small hot weight of its own blood in its veins.

Dr. Yuki Tanaka's breath misted faintly in the humid air, a brief fog that dissolved against the warmth of the conservatory's perpetual spring. She was crouched on the engineered soil beside the fountain's base, her knees pressed into the dark loam, her hands buried to the second knuckle in the disturbed earth. Her eyes were not on the blood. Her eyes were on the soil beneath it, on the pattern of displacement, the particular angle at which the roots had been lifted and resettled, the faint impression of a sole that pressed into the silt with the specific weight and gait she knew, because she had walked it herself, in her mind, in the long hours between the station's artificial dusk and its artificial dawn, when the conservatory was empty and the only sound was the soft percussion of water over ceramic and the slow, patient respiration of ten thousand leaves. Her lips moved. Not in prayer. Not in a name. They moved in the small, automatic rhythm of classification, the way one reads the venation of a leaf for the first sign of blight, counting the parallel lines, noting the asymmetry, filing the observation in a drawer of the mind that has no lock but a very particular key.

The conservatory breathed around her. It breathed with the aggressive, almost territorial greenness of a hundred species pressed into three tiers of growing racks, their chlorophyll releasing its sharp, green, living scent into the recycled air. The humidity sat on her skin like a second shirt, damp and close, drawing the fine hairs on her forearms upright. The ferns in the lower bay unfurled their fronds in the perpetual warmth, and the condensation on the upper panels ran in slow, fat threads toward the drainage channels, each thread a small river in a world no larger than a dining room. The station hummed beneath all of it, that deep, subsonic thrum of the Ares Meridian's rotation, the constant reminder that they were not on a planet, that gravity here was a negotiation rather than a law, that the ground beneath her knees was a thing that had been built and bolted and was not, in any true sense, the earth. And yet the soil was soil. And the blood was blood. And the pattern in the dirt was a pattern she had made, in her mind, a hundred and twelve times, counting each iteration the way a musician counts scales, each one a small rehearsal for the performance she had not yet given.

She did not call it a theft. That word was too crude, too commercial, too much like the language of the Stakeholders' Salon where men in pressed shirts discussed the ore-yield certificates as though they were discussing the price of grain. She called it a relocation. A reassignment. A protection. The gene-seed samples in the vault behind the third growing rack were not, in her taxonomy, property. They were a genetic library, a living archive of the only orchid specimen that existed in the sealed environment of the station, its roots wrapped in a silk ribbon that some member of the crew had tied with the tenderness of a grandmother swaddling a newborn. To move them was to protect them from the men who would grind them into assay reports, who would reduce a ten-thousand-year evolution to a column of figures in a financial projection notebook, who would sign their names to a document that said this living thing is worth four hundred and twelve million credits and therefore may be processed, extracted, and sold. She had rehearsed the excavation. She had mapped the root system in her mind with the same precision she applied to the mycorrhizal networks in the lower bay. She knew exactly how deep to go, at what angle to sever the lateral roots, how to lift the central taproot without disturbing the fungal symbionts that made the specimen viable. She knew the pattern of the displaced soil because she had made it, in her mind, a hundred and twelve times, and the pattern before her now, in the wet dark earth beside the fountain, was that pattern. It was her pattern. The same angle. The same depth. The same particular lift of the lateral roots, the same faint impression of a sole that pressed with the specific gait of a left-dominant woman who carried her weight slightly forward, the way she did, the way she had always done since she was a girl in the hydroponic bays of the Kyoto orbital station, pressing her knees into the dirt to feel the roots.

And this was the thing that made her hands tremble. Not the blood. Not the knowledge that Margaret Ashworth, the dead woman, the chairwoman of the consortium, had been alive and upright and bleeding in this room a few minutes before Yuki's own arrival, because the drop was still wet, still undiluted, still holding its perfect convex shape on the ceramic rim as though the fountain had not yet had time to dissolve it into the trickling water below. Not that. The tremor came from the recognition, the terrible, precise, taxonomic recognition that she could not distinguish her own future excavation from the one before her. That the soil did not know, and would not know, whose hands had disturbed it, whose intention had shaped the pattern. That if she were to stand now, to press her thumb to the disturbed earth and say, I did not do this, the words would be true and would also be a lie, because the pattern was hers, because the pattern would be hers, because the ghost of her planned relocation was already in the dirt, braided into the present crime like a root system threading through foreign soil, and no amount of forensic botany could separate the two.

She did not call for help. This was the part that surprised her, the part that sat in her chest like a cold stone, because she had always believed herself to be the kind of woman who would ring the station's alert chime, who would stand in the corridor with her arms at her sides and say, there is a body, there is blood, there is a pattern in the soil that I can identify to the nearest centimetre. But to announce the disturbance of the conservatory's engineered earth was to announce the disturbance of the vault behind the third growing rack. To announce the pattern was to announce the map. And the map was hers, and the map was the only thing between the orchid and the men who would grind it into a number, and she would not give it up, she would not give it up, she would not give it up, the three repetitions of the thought arriving in her mind with the flat, mechanical regularity of the fountain's water striking the ceramic base, over and over, a rhythm that was not quite a heartbeat and not quite a prayer.

Her fingers worked the soil with the same gentle, deliberate touch she gave a seedling in its first week of germination, the pads of her thumbs pressing the displaced silt back into the furrow, the tips of her index fingers tracing the lateral root channels with a care that was almost tender, almost reverent, the way one handles a thing that is still alive and still growing and still, in its slow and patient way, becoming. Her jaw was set. Her shoulders were rigid beneath the softness of her hands, a contradiction that would have struck an observer as deeply unnatural, the body of a woman who speaks to orchids in the quiet hours and who holds a dead woman's blood in the shape of a single, perfect, still-wet bead on a white ceramic rim. She did not look up. She did not look at the ferns, or the condensation, or the three tiers of growing racks where the specimens breathed their green, patient breath. She looked only at the soil, and at the pattern, and at the small dark impression of a sole that was, and was not, and would be, her own.

It was in the fourth furrow, the one that ran at a slight angle beneath the fountain's drainage channel, that her fingers met the fragment. A root. A single, thin, fibrous root, no longer than her little finger, its tip split and dry, its colour a pale, waxy yellow that belonged to no species in the conservatory's registry, no orchid, no fern, no chlorophyll-green thing that she had catalogued in the three years of her tenure aboard the Ares Meridian. It was not from the lower bay. It was not from the upper panels. It was not from the vault, or the growing racks, or the three hundred and forty-seven registered specimens that made up the living inventory of the station's green heart. It was from somewhere else. From somewhere she did not have a name for, a place that did not exist in her taxonomy, a root that had no parent plant in any soil she had ever touched.

Her fingers withdrew. The flinch was small, almost imperceptible, a tightening of the tendons in her wrist, a brief stillness of the breath that had been fogging the humid air. She pressed her thumb over the fragment, felt its dry, fibrous texture against the pad of her skin, and tucked it into the hollow of her palm, where it lay against the warmth of her hand like a small, pale secret. She stood. The motion was slow, deliberate, the knees unfolding one after the other, the back straightening, the shoulders dropping a fraction from their

***

The smell came first, as it always did in the Mining Archive: cold, metallic, the particular tang of oxidised iron that clung to the fingertips and would not wash away, no matter how many times one scrubbed the knuckles against the steel. It was the smell of a room that had been sealed for years, where the air recycled through the same narrow ducts and the light fell flat and white from the ceiling panels, lending every face a shade of grey that no amount of colour could quite restore. Inspector Elara Voss stood with her back to the display board, arms folded across her chest, and she did not turn around when Felix Drummond's footsteps stopped three paces behind her. She simply spoke, in the flat, conversational cadence of a woman asking for the weather on a morning walk.

"I need to know your whereabouts during the last hour, Mr. Drummond. Any alibis or suspicious movements?"

Behind her, she could feel the man shifting his weight from one foot to the other, a small, continuous oscillation, as though he were trying to remember which leg was which. The iron samples on their steel pins swayed in the faintest degree, a pendulum motion born of the station's slow centrifugal rotation, and the sound they made against the board was a soft, rhythmic

***

The light in the Observatorium did not fall so much as it insisted. It poured through the crystal dome in a hard, refracted column that split the afternoon into a dozen competing angles, each one a blade of pale gold laid flat across the floor, across the brass fittings of the telescope mount, across the upturned face of the woman who stood with her back to the great instrument and her fingertips pressed, very gently, against the velvet of the eastern drape. There was a quality to the illumination that made every shadow a verdict. Nothing in the room was permitted a soft edge. The dust motes suspended in the shaft of Martian sunlight moved with the slow, purposeless grace of small planets in transit, and the subsonic thrum of the station's centrifugal rotation pressed against the teeth, a vibration felt less with the ear than in the molars, a low and patient grinding that said: you are turning, you are always turning, and there is nowhere to stop.

Dr. Cassius Wren stood near the base of the telescope, his hands clasped behind his back in the particular way that suggested he was holding himself together by the architecture of the gesture rather than by any genuine comfort in the posture. His jaw worked. A slow, lateral grinding, the left masseter flexing and releasing, flexing and releasing, as though the muscles had developed a private argument with the bone. His eyes moved. Not wandering. Not searching, in any honest sense of the word. They tracked in a methodical sweep from left to right, from the crystal dome down to the floor, from the floor up to the drape, from the drape to the porthole frame where the seal had been, very slightly, broken. A thread of condensation. He noted it. He noted the smudge of red dust on the brass mount that Inspector Voss had isolated the previous hour, and he noted the absence of any other disturbance, and he noted the body of Margaret Ashworth, which lay where it had fallen, her posture unchanged since the discovery, her platinum locket still clasped at her throat, her expression fixed in the particular vacancy that death bestows on a face that had, in life, been capable of considerable sharpness. He noted these things the way a man notes the items on a receipt, confirming each line, ensuring the total is correct, ensuring that no one has altered the sum.

And then his eyes reached the woman by the drape, and the sweep stopped.

It stopped the way a pendulum stops when the hand that holds its string decides, mid-arc, that the experiment has served its purpose. Dr. Wren's gaze fixed on Dr. Yuki Tanaka with the sudden, absolute attention of a man who has been listening for a particular word in a long and tedious sentence and has at last heard it. She was examining the velvet. Not glancing. Not touching it the way one touches a curtain to part it and look through. Her fingertips were pressed into the fabric with a pressure that left faint, crescent-shaped indentations in the pile, and her head was tilted at an angle that suggested she was reading the weave the way a geologist reads a stratified cliff face. Her shoulders were hunched, not with cold, for the Observatorium held its temperature steady within a fraction of a degree, but with a contained, inward tension, as though her spine had become a question mark and she was trying to straighten it into a period.

She turned, then, and ran a single finger along the seam where the velvet met the crystal. The cold of the glass bit into her fingertip. She did not withdraw her hand. She held it there, feeling the temperature drop, feeling the smoothness of the join, feeling for the hairline fracture or the concealed latch that her fingers, trained by years of separating root systems from engineered soil, would have recognised the instant they found it. She was looking for a compartment. She was looking for a way through. The distinction between those two purposes was, to the casual observer, non-existent, and this was precisely the point.

In the Stakeholders' Salon, three corridors away and a full rotation of the station's slow centrifugal turn to the south, Captain Theron Blackwood held the crystal decanter up to the light and turned it a quarter-degree, examining the cut pattern along its neck for any imperfection, any scratch that might indicate a second glass had been set down beside it. The sherry glass sat in his other hand, and he was sniffing the residue at the rim with the grave, almost liturgical attention of a man who has made a career of reading the small language of objects. He had not yet found what he was looking for. He would not find what he was looking for. But the searching was, for him, a form of prayer, and the prayer was for a sin that was not his own.

In the Mining Archive, Felix Drummond was talking. He was always talking, in the particular register of a man who has rehearsed his sentences in a mirror and is now performing them for an audience of one. Sera Okafor listened with the selective attention of a journalist who has already decided which fragments will appear in the dispatch and which will be permitted to dissolve into the general texture of the record. She nodded at the appropriate intervals. She asked a question that was not quite a question. Drummond's sweat was visible now, a fine sheen along his hairline, and he wiped his forehead with the back of his hand and then, recognising the gesture as a concession, lowered his hand to his side and resumed his recitation of the ventilation schedule with a slightly more authoritative cadence.

Inspector Elara Voss walked. She walked with the particular purposefulness of a woman who has already formed her hypothesis and is now crossing the room to confirm it, her footsteps measured, her eyes forward, her hand resting lightly on the brass railing of the Grand Rotunda as though the metal might, if she let go, decide to leave. She was walking toward the Observatorium. She was walking toward the two people who stood in it, each of them performing the role of investigator with the earnest, slightly desperate precision of a man or woman who is very afraid that the investigator's coat, once donned, will reveal the stains beneath.

But in the Observatorium, there was no one to perform for. The dome above them was a hemisphere of perfect, merciless transparency, and through it the sky of Mars lay in its rusted, patient red, and the stars were not yet visible, for it was midday, and the sun hung low and white and utterly indifferent. The room was designed to look outward. To look at the stars, at the deep and ancient geometry of the cosmos, at the vast and empty theatre in which the station turned its slow, mechanical rotation. And yet the two people in it were looking inward. Wren was looking at the walls, at the floor, at the body, at the dust, at the condensation, at the smudge, at the broken seal, at every small and damning detail that might, in the hands of a less careful investigator, have been made to mean something. Tanaka was looking at the seams, at the joins, at the hidden architecture of the room, at the places where the crystal met the velvet and the velvet met the steel frame, memorising the coordinates with the quiet, methodical precision of a botanist mapping the vascular system of a leaf. Each of them was building a map. Each of them was building it for a purpose that the other could not see and would not, in any honest version of events, have understood.

Wren watched her. He watched the way her shoulders hunched and released. He watched the way her eyes darted, not with the lazy curiosity of a tourist but with the focused, almost predatory attention of a woman tracking a scent. He watched the pressure marks her fingers left in the velvet and the way she smoothed them out, as though the fabric might, if left unsmoothed, record the fact of her touch. And he felt, in the grinding of his own jaw, in the slow lateral flex of his masseter, the particular hot shame of a man who has committed a thing and is now standing in the room where the thing will be committed again, in a different form, with a different weapon, and is scanning the walls for the evidence of a crime that has not yet occurred because the crime has already occurred and the evidence is his own hands and his own mind and the forty-seven documents in the Mining Archive that bear a name which is not his but which he has signed.

He read her anxiety. He read it the way he read the condensation on the porthole frame, the way he read the smudge on the brass mount: as a fact, as a datum, as a line item on a receipt that must be accounted for. She was tense. She was in a state of heightened, almost visible agitation. She was, in his reading, a woman who had been in a conversation, a difficult conversation, with someone whose name he did not yet know but whose presence he could feel in the room the way one feels a draught in a sealed corridor, a thin and cold movement of air that means a door has been opened somewhere and has not been closed. He read her as a co-conspirator. He read her as a woman who knew, who had seen, who had been in the room when the assay reports were altered and the numbers made to agree with a future that the ore had not yet produced. He read her as a threat. And the reading was wrong, or rather, it was wrong in the specific and consequential way that a man who is guilty of one crime will always misidentify the guilt of another, because the guilt of another is not the same guilt, and the motive is not the same motive, and the body that she is hiding is not the body that he is hiding, and the compartment she is searching for will open onto a vault of gene-seed samples and not onto a safe containing the original ore-yield certificates.

Tanaka withdrew her hand from the velvet. The crescent marks remained, faint, in the pile. She turned. She turned slowly, the way a woman turns who has been listening to a frequency just below the threshold of hearing and has at last decided that the sound is real and that she must address it. She met Wren's eyes. He did not look away. She did not look away. The space between them was perhaps four feet, and in that four feet the crystal dome above threw a slow, prismatic arc that moved across the floor in a crawl of pale colour, a rainbow that was not a rainbow, a spectrum that had no source, a band of light that belonged to no one and that neither of them moved to break.

"You've been looking at the walls, Dr. Wren," she said. Her voice was very soft. Not a whisper, exactly. A near-whisper. The kind of voice that a woman uses when she is speaking to a colleague in a room where there are no other colleagues and where the walls are made of crystal and the sky is made of vacuum and the only person who can hear her is the man who is, at this moment, very carefully not blinking. "Not the floor."

He did not answer. His jaw worked once, twice, and then stilled. The subsonic thrum of the station pressed against

***

The crystal decanter sat upon the satin tablecloth as though it had always been there, as though the station's slow rotation had not, in the last three hours, shifted the angle of the amber light by perhaps two degrees, enough to send a new fracture of gold across its cut facets. And in that fracture, Captain Theron Blackwood saw himself broken into four. The leftmost version was a man of indeterminate age, his jaw set, his eyes narrowed to the particular slant of a man who is not looking at a glass of sherry but at the space between the glass and the table, the space where a thumbprint might or might not rest. The second version was older, more tired, the lines at the corners of his mouth deepened by the low light. The third and fourth were mere shadows, ghostly afterimages, the kind one sees in a mirror after the person has already turned away.

He did not touch the decanter.

This, in itself, was a statement. A man who has been in command for as long as Blackwood has been in command does not reach for a thing until he has decided what the reaching will mean. He circled the table instead, one full revolution, his boots making no sound on the alternating walnut and birch floor, his hands hanging at his sides with the relaxed precision of a surgeon who has already chosen the incision. His fingers were clean. Dry. The pads slightly calloused at the tips, the way they become after years of gripping a ship's wheel or a command console, where the surface is smooth and the grip must be absolute. He wore no gloves. This was not an oversight. A man who wears gloves is a man who fears what his own hands might confess. Blackwood had no such fear. He had already accounted for his print. It was in the report. It was in the narrative. It was, in fact, the least of his concerns.

The silence of the Stakeholders' Salon was not the absence of sound. It was the presence of a very specific, very small sound, beneath the threshold of comfortable hearing: the micro-tick of the station's gyros, the faint mechanical exhalation of the Ares Meridian turning in her slow orbit above the rust-coloured plain of Mars. That tick was the only voice in the room. No one else was here. No one else breathed. The body of Margaret Ashworth lay where it had been found, beneath the low ceiling of the salon's far alcove, and Blackwood had not looked at it once. He had no need. He had written the sentence about her three days ago, in the privacy of his study, in a journal whose last entry read, in his own careful hand, *I will not sign the extension.* The sentence was finished. The body was merely the punctuation.

He stopped at the near side of the table, where the glass stood. A glass of sherry, the stem thin as a finger, the bowl holding a measure of dark amber liquid that had not been stirred. Had not been touched. The surface was perfectly still, a small dark mirror reflecting the low amber glow of the salon's lighting, and in that reflection the ceiling panels and the distant porthole and the faint, cold suggestion of the Martian sky beyond. The sherry had oxidised slightly in the sealed glass. One could smell it if one leaned close: a sharp, sweet ghost, the perfume of a wine that has been waiting too long in a room where the air does not move. Blackwood leaned close. He inhaled, once, a single shallow draw through the nose, the way a sommelier might, except that a sommelier is looking for the wine and Blackwood was looking for the absence of the wine. The absence of a hand. The absence of a thumb.

"Fingertip," he said. The word was clipped, barely above a whisper, addressed to no one, to the glass, to the air, to the small dark mirror of sherry that showed him nothing. "Left thumb. Or right."

He said it the way a man says a line he has rehearsed in the bath, in the dark, in the three hours between waking and the rest of the station's shift. It was not a question. It was a confirmation. The physical world was being asked to cooperate, and Blackwood intended to note, in his report, that it had.

His right hand rose. Slowly. The motion was so deliberate that it seemed almost ceremonial, the fingers extending one by one, the thumb parting from the index with the careful separation of a man who is choosing which of his own fingerprints will be the one to touch the glass. He did not wrap his fingers around the stem. He did not grip. He rested the pad of his right index finger against the very lowest point of the stem, where the glass meets the foot, and he lifted. The glass rose from the tablecloth with a faint, almost inaudible whisper of fibre against crystal. He held it up to the amber light.

The motion was exact. Practised. There was a quality to it that spoke of repetition, of a hundred previous tilts in a hundred previous contexts, a hundred previous rooms where a man had held a glass to the light and searched for the one small detail that would anchor a story already written. The light passed through the sherry, through the thin glass, and scattered across the tablecloth in a small, warm pool. And in that pool, in the space where the light bent and the crystal caught and the dark liquid held its stillness, Blackwood saw what he had come to see.

His jaw tightened. Not a clench. Not a spasm. A tightening, the way a knot is drawn taut on a rope, the slow and inevitable drawing of tension through a line that was already under strain. His eyes did not widen. His brows did not rise. There was no surprise in his face, and this was the thing that made the moment, for anyone who might have been watching, so profoundly and quietly terrible. He had not been surprised. He had not been hoping. He had been confirming. The verdict had been delivered in a room that no longer existed, in a conversation that had been conducted in the past tense of a future that was already, for him, merely administrative.

He set the glass down.

This, too, was a performance of care. His fingers released the stem in the same order they had closed, the index finger the last to part, the pad withdrawing with a faint, almost imperceptible drag. He aligned the base of the glass parallel to the base of the decanter, the two vessels now sitting in a neat, deliberate row, as though he were restoring a still life to its original composition, as though the arrangement had been disturbed by an intruder and he was, in his capacity as captain, merely putting the scene back to rights. The tablecloth smoothed beneath his palm. The sherry did not ripple. The amber light held its slow, gallery-still glow.

And then he turned.

The turn was slow, unhurried, the shoulders rotating on the axis of the spine, the head following last, the way a man turns who is not fleeing but concluding. In the brief profile that the amber light caught before he was fully turned away, the reader might have seen relief. A softening. The exhalation of a man who has found what he sought and may now set it down. But it was not relief. It was the cold, flat satisfaction of a man who has checked a name on a list. The name was already there. The checkmark was the only new thing. He did not pocket the glass. He did not wrap it in cloth, or seal it in an evidence bag, or make a note in the small leather journal that rode in his breast pocket. He did not need to. He already knew where the print was. He already knew whose it was. He already knew the name that would appear in the next paragraph of the next report, the name that would sit in the file beside the word *embezzlement* and the word *sedative* and the word *Ashworth*, and the name would be the one he had chosen before the glass had left the table, before the light had bent, before the jaw had tightened.

He walked to the far side of the salon, where the porthole framed a sliver of the Martian plain, rust and silence and the slow, indifferent turn of a world that owed no one an explanation. He stood there for a moment, his reflection thin and pale in the glass, and then he turned and walked toward the door, his boots making no sound, his hands hanging at his sides, his fingers clean and dry and calloused, bearing the prints of a man who has already written the sentence and is merely waiting for the rest of the station to read it.

Somewhere across the Ares Meridian, in the Observatorium with its crystal dome and its velvet drapes, a geologist was scanning the room for a crime he had not yet committed, and a botanist was pressing her fingers against the fabric searching for a compartment that would fund a betrayal she had already rehearsed. In the Mining Archive, a stakeholder was sweating through his collar while a journalist fed a detective fragments of a story that did not add up. And in the Grand Rotunda, a woman in a dark coat was walking with the purposeful, unhurried stride of someone who knows exactly which door she is looking for, even if she has not yet decided what she will do when she finds it.

But in the Stakeholders' Salon, the sherry glass sat parallel to the decanter, the stem bearing its two sets of fingerprints, the sedative residue faint and sweet in the sealed air, and the only sound was the micro-tick of the gyros, counting out the seconds in a room where a verdict had been confirmed, not discovered, and where the man who had confirmed it was already walking away, already turning the page, already in the next room, already in the next sentence of the story he had been writing since before the body was cold.

***

The thumb pressed into the worn velvet of the armchair cushion with a pressure so slight that it might have been mistaken for a resting finger, a casual touch, the kind one gives to a piece of furniture one has sat in a hundred times. But Inspector Elara Voss was not resting. She was listening with her fingertips. Beneath the pile, beneath the faded burgundy thread that had begun to fray at the inner edge, her pad felt what the eye would never have perceived: a seam that did not run straight. A whisper of a gap, no wider than a hair's breadth, where the stitching had been picked apart and re-secured with a thread so fine it was nearly invisible against the fabric. A manufacturing defect, one might say. A quirk of the station's upholstery workshop. Anything, in fact, that would permit a reasonable person to look away.

Voss did not look away.

Three paces behind her, Dr. Cassius Wren stood with his arms loosely crossed over his chest, offering the Grand Rotunda a perfectly calibrated expression of mild, scholarly interest. He had arrived twenty minutes prior, drifting in from the Observatorium with that particular gait of a man who wishes to be seen arriving rather than simply found already present. His tie was knotted with geometric precision. His smile, when he offered it, was the smile of a man who had rehearsed it before a mirror and found it satisfactory. "Charming little furnishings," he had said, gesturing at the row of armchairs arranged in their semicircle beneath the broad, even illumination of the Rotunda's ceiling panels. "One almost forgets we are three hundred kilometres above a red desert. One might think one were in a drawing room in Sussex."

His eyes, however, had not been on the furnishings. They had been on her hands.

The Rotunda was, by design, a room without secrets. The light fell in a wide, unbroken wash from the overhead diffusers, casting no shadow into which a guilty conscience might retreat. The floor's alternating walnut and birch panels caught the glow and scattered it evenly, so that every surface was exposed, every object legible. Margaret Ashworth's body still lay where it had been found, draped in the grey thermal sheet the medics had placed over her, her platinum locket visible at her throat like a small, cold eye. The wine glass beside her, still faintly warm, still bearing that particular bitter-almond trace on its rim, sat on the low table as though it had been set there only moments ago. The station's rotation pressed its subsonic hum against the hull, a low, constant pressure in the bones, a reminder that they were sealed in, that the air they breathed was the same air, that no one could simply walk out into the void and be gone.

Voss's fingers found the seam.

She did not announce it. She did not turn. She simply continued the motion, her thumb and forefinger working the thread apart with the unhurried patience of a woman unpicking a hem, and the gap widened by a millimetre, then two, then enough to admit the tip of her index finger. The interior was a small cavity, no larger than a matchbox, lined with a felt that had the faint smell of old leather and iron-gall ink. And there, tucked into the felt with the particular tenderness of a mother tucking a child in, was a journal. Small. Leather-bound, the cover the deep umber of a thing that had been handled often, worn smooth at the corners where thumbs had pressed and turned. A clasp of tarnished brass. No title on the spine. No name.

The flinch was not a flinch. Or rather, it was a flinch, but so brief, so microscopic, that a lesser observer would have attributed it to a spasm in the shoulder, a tick in the jaw, the natural twitch of a man standing too long. Dr. Wren's left hand, which had been resting on his crossed right arm, lifted. Not all the way. Not to his face, not to his throat. It drifted, a slow, almost imperceptible migration, toward his breast pocket, where it lingered for a half-second that should not have existed, a half-second in which the muscles of his forearm tightened and the tendons along his wrist stood out like the strings of a plucked instrument. Then the hand resumed its position. The smile did not waver. But the jaw, that carefully set, erudite jaw, had tightened by a degree. A single degree. The degree that separates a man who is surprised from a man who is afraid.

Voss withdrew her fingers. She did not reach in. She stood very still for a moment, her back to him, her shoulders level, her breathing unchanged, as though she had merely been examining the stitching for a loose thread. Then she turned.

"Doctor."

"Yes?" The word was light. Conversational. The tone one might use to acknowledge a colleague passing in a corridor. His eyes, however, had dropped to the armchair. To the gap in the seam. To the small, dark shape of leather that was no longer quite hidden.

She reached in. The motion was slow, deliberate, the fingers unfolding one after the other as though she were performing a minor surgical procedure. The journal came free with a soft, dry whisper of felt against leather. She held it at arm's length, between them, in the space where the light was thickest and the shadows thinnest. The brass clasp caught the diffused glow and threw back a small, hard point of brightness.

She said nothing.

The silence that followed was not empty. It was full, the way a held note is full, the way the Rotunda's hum was full, a pressure building behind the eardrums. The iron samples in the Mining Archive, three corridors away, were swaying in their faint pendulum arc. In the Hydroponic Conservatory, a woman was crouching before a fountain, her fingers trembling over a single drop of blood. In the Crew's Mess, a captain was asking questions he already knew the answers to. In the Mining Archive, two people were talking in low, careful voices about numbers that did not add up. But here, in the even light, in the sealed air, in the three paces between the woman who held the journal and the man who had watched her find it, the silence was a living thing, and it was looking at both of them.

"A curious place to tuck a diary, Doctor." Voss's voice was level. Dry. The voice of a woman commenting on the weather, on the quality of the sherry, on the particular shade of burgundy in the upholstery. She turned the journal a quarter-rotation so the worn spine caught the light. "One does not hide a diary in a chair unless one expects it to be read by someone who will not ask permission."

Wren's mouth opened. Closed. Opened again. The smile was still there. It was, in fact, slightly wider than before, as though the muscles of his face had been given a new instruction by some small, desperate committee in the back of his skull. "Inspector, I assure you, I had no idea this piece of furniture contained—"

He stopped.

Not because he had run out of words. Not because the sentence had reached its natural conclusion. He stopped because something had shifted in the quality of the silence, some subsonic change in the station's rotation that no one else could feel but which he, for one unguarded instant, registered as a threat. His left hand was at his breast pocket again. This time it did not drift away. His fingers curled around the edge of his shirt, just above the pocket, and held. The journal hung between them, small and dark and utterly silent, its contents unread, its ownership unclaimed, its secrets pressed flat against the felt lining like a held breath.
