And the question that neither of them would speak, that hung in the even light of the Grand Rotunda like a thread of condensation on a porthole glass, the question that would not resolve itself until someone broke, was this: whose hands had written the figures in those pages, and whose hands had hidden the book in the chair, and whose name would be in the Interplanetary Trade Council's filing cabinet by the time the shuttle docked at the colony, and whose name would be crossed out in red ink, the way a name is crossed out in a guest book when the guest has stopped being a guest and become, instead, a problem that must be disposed of.

The hum of the station pressed on. The iron samples swayed. The wine glass cooled by a fraction of a degree. And Dr. Cassius Wren stood three paces back with his hand on his pocket and his smile in place and his sentence unfinished, and Inspector Elara Voss held the journal at arm's length and said nothing more, because in the particular economy of this room, in this sealed and spinning and lightless-of-shadow place, the thing that had been said was already too much, and the thing that had not been said was the only thing that mattered.

***

The Crew's Mess at thirteen hundred hours was a room holding its breath.

Not the dramatic, theatrical silence of a held note in a concert hall, but the smaller, more persistent quiet of a space that has been emptied of its purpose. The recycled air carried a faint sweetness—coffee, long since cold, the particular ghost-smell of a cup set down in a hurry and never finished. Margaret Ashworth's last cup. The dregs had been wiped from the saucer by some orderly hand, but the smell had worked itself into the grain of the plastic table, into the weave of the vinyl seat, into the very metal of the bulkhead where the ventilation grille exhaled its slow, chemical sigh. It was not a strong smell. It would not have registered to a stranger. But Captain Theron Blackwood was not a stranger, and he had not entered the room to smell coffee.

He did not sit. He did not look toward the far corner where the body had been laid, covered with a sheet of institutional grey, its edges tucked beneath the trestle legs with the careful economy of a man who has been told to make a thing disappear and has made it disappear with the same efficiency he brought to everything. His eyes went immediately to the wall paneling—the riveted, slightly dented aluminium that ran the full length of the mess in alternating panels of brushed steel and painted white—and his jaw tightened with the particular rigidity of a man who has already decided what the wall will tell him.

He checked the door. Once. Then again, because the first check was protocol and the second was need. The corridor beyond was empty. The station's slow centrifugal rotation pressed against his molars, a low and constant thrum that he had long since stopped hearing consciously but which now, in the absence of any other sound, rose to the surface of his awareness like a pressure change before a storm. The ring-light overhead cast no true shadows. Everything was evenly, mercilessly visible. There were no corners in which a thing might hide, no darkness in which a detail might soften. It was, Blackwood thought, a terrible light for a man who wished to believe he was looking for something.

He ran his fingers along the scarred table surface. Not quickly. Not with the broad, sweeping gesture of a man cataloguing. His fingertips traced the gouges and scratches with a slow, deliberate pressure, the way a man reads a letter he suspects contains a single word he is searching for and will find it or will not and the finding of it will not change what he has already written in his reply. The table was old. The scratches were old. A crew number here, a knife-mark there, the small archaeology of two years in a sealed tin can orbiting a red planet. None of it was new. None of it was useful. He knew this. He ran his fingers along the surface regardless, because the running of his fingers was the shape of the searching, and the shape of the searching was what he would describe in his report if anyone asked, and the description would carry the weight of thoroughness even though the substance was entirely absent.

In the Grand Rotunda, three decks above, Dr. Cassius Wren stood with his hands in his pockets and his expression arranged into something approximating scholarly interest, watching the Inspector walk toward the Observatorium with the unhurried certainty of a woman who has already decided where the body will be found and is merely confirming the geography for the record. Wren's eyes tracked her departure, then moved to the empty space where the leather journal had been, then to the two or three faces that watched him with the particular wariness of people who have learned, in the last six hours, that the person in the room with them might be the one who poured the poison. He said nothing. He did not need to. The silence was his own, and he had always been comfortable in silence.

Back in the mess, Blackwood's fingers had moved from the table to the bulkhead. He pressed his knuckles against the rivets, feeling the cold metal bite into the pads of his fingers, and he thought—briefly, once, the name arising in his mind like a word on the tip of the tongue that you do not need to finish saying because you already know the sentence—Wren. One name. One thought. Then the thought was gone, replaced by the next small action: the tilt of his head, the narrowing of his eyes as he scanned the paneling for a smudge, a mark, a thread of ink that might have been left by a hand that had been writing in a ledger at an odd hour. There was no smudge. There was no thread. The paneling was clean, or as clean as paneling was ever clean aboard a station that breathed recycled air and tasted faintly of ozone and machine oil. He touched it again. He touched it a third time. The touching was not the finding. The touching was the performance of the finding, the physical proof that he had been here, that he had looked, that he had not neglected this particular surface in his inventory of the room.

The empty chairs were a presence. Four of them, pulled back from the table in the casual disorder of a meal interrupted. The vinyl seats were cold. The small personal effects that might have sat on the armrests—a folded napkin, a pen, a ration card—were absent, removed by the same orderly hand that had wiped the saucer. Blackwood looked at the chairs. He did not look at them for long. They were furniture. They were the absence of people. And the absence of people was, in this room, at this hour, the only evidence that mattered, because the absence meant that whoever had sat in those chairs had left, and the leaving had been clean, and the cleanliness of the leaving was the thing that did not fit. A guilty man does not leave a room clean. A guilty man leaves a tremor in the glass, a hair on the collar, a half-finished sentence hanging in the air like smoke. This room had none of those things. And so the guilt, by the simple arithmetic of Blackwood's mind, must reside in the one person who had not sat in any of those chairs, who had not been present at the meal, who had been elsewhere in the station at the hour the coffee went cold and the cup was set down and never lifted again.

In the Hydroponic Conservatory, Dr. Yuki Tanaka knelt beside a row of soil trays, her fingers working the engineered earth with the careful, searching pressure of a woman looking for a compartment that was not there. The orchid specimen sat on its pedestal, roots wrapped in their silk ribbon, watching her with the patient indifference of a thing that does not know it is evidence. She did not find the compartment. She would not find the compartment. But her hands kept moving, and the moving of her hands was its own kind of prayer.

In the Mining Archive, Felix Drummond and Sera Okafor stood on opposite sides of the steel filing cabinet, their conversation a thin and careful thing, each word placed with the precision of a man threading a needle in the dark. Drummond's voice was steady. Sera's was steadier. They were not talking about the archive. They were talking about the shape of the story that would be told, and the shape of the story required a certain arrangement of facts, and the facts, as it happened, were already arranged.

Blackwood withdrew his hand from the bulkhead. He stood very still for a moment, his reflection visible in the polished steel panel beside him—older than he had been a year ago, thinner, the lines around his mouth deeper, a man holding a station together with his teeth and the particular authority of a rank that no one on this station had elected. He looked at the reflection. The reflection did not look back. He turned from the wall.

He had found nothing. There was no misplaced ledger. There was no smudge of ink. There was no thread of handwriting, no scrawl of a number, no single physical fact that he could pin to a page and say, here, this is the thread, pull it and the whole tapestry comes apart. The table was clean. The bulkhead was clean. The air smelled of old coffee and machine oil and nothing else. He had searched the room with the thoroughness of a man who had already written the conclusion and required only the gesture of searching to give the conclusion its weight. And it had the weight. It had always had the weight. The searching was not for the finding. The searching was for the permission to stop.

He straightened his tunic. The movement was small, precise, the way one adjusts a collar before entering a courtroom. He did not look back at the grey shape in the corner. He did not look at the empty chair where she had sat. He walked toward the door with the even, unhurried pace of a man who has completed a task, and the task, in the particular economy of his thinking, had been the confirmation, not the discovery. The absence of any other explanation was, for Captain Theron Blackwood, the explanation. The silence of the room was the testimony. And the one name that had risen in his mind, once, briefly, like a word on the tip of the tongue, had been the only name that fit the silence.

He opened the door. The corridor beyond was empty, lit by the same shadowless ring-light, carrying the same low thrum of the station's eternal spin. He stepped through. The door closed behind him with a soft, final click, and the Crew's Mess settled back into its held-breath quiet, the ghost-smell of coffee rising slowly from the vinyl seats, the four empty chairs holding their shapes in the even light, the grey sheet in the corner waiting for the next person who would have the courage, or the obligation, to look at it.

And in the Grand Rotunda, Dr. Cassius Wren stood very still, his hands in his pockets, his expression arranged into something approximating scholarly interest, and he did not know—could not know, in the sealed and spinning and shadowless dark of this place—that in a room three decks below, a man had touched a table and a wall and found nothing, and in the finding of nothing had confirmed everything, and was already walking away, already turning the page, already in the next sentence of the story he had been writing since before the body was cold.

***

The phosphor grow-lights hummed their low amber note, a sound less heard than felt, a vibration that settled behind the teeth and in the hollow of the sternum like a second heartbeat slightly out of time with one's own. In the Hydroponic Conservatory, where the recycled air carried the mineral tang of damp loam and the green, almost metallic sweetness of chlorophyll breaking down under artificial sun, the light fell in long warm slabs across the soil trays, gilding the tips of the dwarf wheat, the broad leaves of the hydroponic kale, the trailing tendrils of the climbing beans that had been coaxed to grow in a place where no soil had ever known a single natural rain. The station's rotational spin pressed its half-degree of gravity into the knees, a faint creak in the joints, a gentle insistence that the body was still, for the moment, a body and not merely a collection of organs suspended in a tin can.

Dr. Yuki Tanaka knelt in the second row, her back to the central service corridor, her palms flat on the rim of a soil tray as though steadying herself against a gentle current. Her fingers—pale, the cuticles trimmed to a precise half-millimetre, a faint crescent of dark loam beneath the thumbnail of the right hand—parted the root mass of a dwarf wheat specimen with the unhurried deliberation of a surgeon separating tissue. She did not touch the roots. She did not brush the soil from the stem. She simply held them apart, a careful two-millimetre gap, and looked. Not through. Not at. *For*. One thought, clean and singular, passed through her mind with the flatness of a fact already known: she was not here to check for blight.

She released the roots. They sprang back together with the faintest elastic whisper. Tanaka drew a breath through her nose, slow, measured, the kind of breath a woman takes when she has decided, in the quiet architecture of her own mind, that the next three minutes will be the most important three minutes of her week, and that the quality of that breath would determine whether her hands remained steady. Her shoulders were relaxed. This was the detail that, to an observer, would have appeared the most natural thing in the world. A woman tending her plants. A woman who had spent eleven years coaxing chlorophyll from a sealed environment, who knew the particular weight of a healthy seedling in the palm, who could identify a nitrogen deficiency by the angle of a single leaf-tip. A woman, in other words, who had every reason to be kneeling in the dirt, hands in the soil, face turned to the green.

But the hands did not move as a botanist's hands move. A botanist's hands are generous. They ruffle. They cup. They press a thumb into the soil to feel for moisture, for the cool density of a root ball, for the small living architecture of a mycorrhizal network. Tanaka's hands moved in straight lines. They parted, lifted, tested, replaced. They moved the way a hand moves when it is looking for a seam, a hinge, a place where two things that are meant to be one have been made into two.

She rose. Her knees creaked with the rotational pull, a small mechanical sound, and she straightened to her full height with the particular economy of movement that spoke not of age but of control. The amber light caught the curve of her jaw, the flat, serene plane of her cheekbones, the dark eyes that did not widen, did not dart, did not perform the small involuntary checks of a person scanning a room for threats. Her face was the face of a woman who had already decided, hours ago, in the privacy of her quarters, in the blue-dark quiet before the station's artificial dawn, exactly where she was going, exactly what she was looking for, and exactly what she would do if she found it. And exactly what she would do if she did not.

She walked to the third row.

The third row was the oldest planting in the conservatory, the dwarf wheat and the broad-leaf kale and the single, improbable orchid specimen whose roots were wrapped in a silk ribbon of a shade so faint it might have been cream or might have been the particular grey of a winter sky seen from a great distance. The soil in these trays was darker, more compacted, the surface bearing the fine white crystalline residue of the mineral supplements she had applied herself, three weeks ago, in a routine that was part of her professional duty and part of something else, something she did not name, something that lived in the space between the act and the intention. The grow-lights here were a shade warmer, more orange, and the air was thicker with the smell of wet earth and the faint, almost imperceptible bitterness of the orchid's roots, a scent that reminded her, without her permitting herself to think it, of the particular quality of the soil in the valley where she had grown up, before the stations, before the colony, before the long and slow negotiation between a woman and the thing she had become.

She knelt again. The soil tray in question was the seventh from the left, the one whose rim bore a hairline fracture along the lower edge, a fracture she had caused herself, deliberately, with the tip of a scalpel, on a night when the conservatory was empty and the grow-lights were dimmed to their overnight amber and the only sound was the deep, subsonic thrum of the station's gyros counting out their endless, purposeless seconds. Her fingers found the seam. Not the fracture. The seam. The place where the base-plate, the thin aluminium disc that formed the underside of the tray, met the soil bed. There was a gap. A gap that should not have been there. A gap that was, in the precise taxonomy of her own making, the difference between a tray of wheat and a vault.

Her fingers closed on the plate.

The metal was cool. It had been cool for three weeks. It had been cool through the long rotation of the station, through the slow grind of the colony's mining cycle below, through the hours in which she had lain awake in her narrow bunk and watched the ceiling and counted the days until the courier shuttle would make its approach to the station's docking ring and the package, small and unremarkable, would pass from her hands into the hands of a buyer who did not know her name and did not need to. The gene-seed samples. The proprietary cultivars. Eleven years of work, compressed into vials no larger than a thumbnail, worth more than the station's annual operating budget, worth more than the mining colony's first three years of projected yield, worth, in the particular and ruthless arithmetic of the off-world terraforming consortium that had made the approach, a number she had not spoken aloud to any living person.

The plate lifted.

It lifted with a small, dry click, the sound of a mechanism releasing, a sound so faint it might have been the settling of the soil, the micro-shift of a root tip, the ordinary creak of a living thing adjusting to the pressure of a hand. The compartment beneath was a hollow no deeper than two finger-widths, lined with a thin layer of inert foam that she had pressed into place with the same careful, surgical precision she now brought to the lifting of the plate. And the hollow was empty.

The foam was undisturbed. The foam was clean. The small, precise indentations where the vials had rested, the four shallow depressions in the pale material, were bare. There was no residue. No trace of the faint blue-green sheen that the sealed vials had cast in the amber light. No smell of the preservation medium, that particular cold-and-sweet note that clung to the fingers for an hour after handling. Nothing. The compartment was a small, clean, empty room, and in that emptiness was a silence so absolute, so total, that it seemed to press against the eardrums, to fill the space behind the eyes, to occupy the whole of the conservatory with its single, implacable fact.

Tanaka held the plate. She held it for a long time. The amber light fell on the metal surface and held it there, warm and still, and her fingers did not tremor. This was the thing. This was the detail that, in the economy of this sealed and spinning and shadowless place, was the most damning and the most revealing. Her fingers did not tremor. Her jaw did not tighten. Her breathing, which had slowed to the shallow, measured rhythm of a woman in deep concentration, did not quicken. She held the plate and she looked at the empty foam and she thought, with the flat, clean precision of a woman who has spent her life reducing complex systems to their component parts, *not here, not this row, not this tray, and if not here, then where, and if not where, then who, and if who, then when, and if when, then how much time remains before the shuttle, before the courier, before the window closes and the number becomes, in the particular and irreversible arithmetic of a sealed station in the void above a dead world, nothing at all.*

She set the plate back. The click was the same. The same small, dry release. She pressed the soil over the seam with the flat of her palm, smoothing, pressing, erasing the hairline fracture, the gap, the two-millimetre difference between a tray of wheat and a vault. Her fingers moved in straight lines. Her shoulders were relaxed. Her face was the face of a woman who had been tending her plants, who had found nothing unusual, who was now rising to check the irrigation schedule for the next cycle.

She straightened.

The conservatory wall curved behind her, a broad sweep of tinted glass that caught the amber grow-light and the green of the leaves and the small, pale shape of a woman in a soil-stained smock, and in that curved glass, in that warm and gold and living mirror, her reflection stood perfectly composed, perfectly still, her hands at her sides, her expression arranged into the particular serenity of a person who has just discovered that the thing she was most afraid of has happened and has decided, in the space of a single unblinking moment, that the next thing she does will be the thing that matters, and that the thing she does not do—the trembling, the staring, the small, human, animal sound that rises in the throat and cannot be swallowed—will be the thing that undoes everything.

Her eyes did not blink.

The grow-lights hummed. The dwarf wheat swayed in the faint thermal current rising from the soil. The station turned, and turned, and turned, and the half-degree of gravity pressed gently, patiently, into the soles of her shoes, and the orchid in the third row breathed its cold, sweet, indifferent breath into the recycled air, and the conservatory went on, green and living and utterly unaware, in the long amber hum, of the small and terrible emptiness that had been made and smoothed and sealed and hidden in the space between a woman's hands and the soil.

***

The first thing one noticed, in the Observatorium of the Ares Meridian, was the silence. Not the absence of sound, precisely, for the station hummed and ticked and breathed its low subsonic thrum against the fillings of one's molars, and the heating elements in the telescope's brass housing gave off a faint ozone tang that sat on the back of the tongue like a half-remembered word. But the silence was of a different order. It was the silence of a room that had been made for looking outward, for the vast and patient turning of worlds, and which now held, instead, five people who were very much looking at one another.

Inspector Elara Voss stood before the great instrument with her left hand raised, the gloved fingertips tracing the curve of the brass eyepiece housing in the unhurried, deliberate manner of a jeweller turning a stone to find the flaw. Her thumb stopped. She did not withdraw her hand. She simply stood there, the half-degree of artificial gravity pressing her boots flat to the deck, and outside the observation glass Mars hung in its slow rotation, vast and rust-coloured and utterly indifferent to the small tin can that circled it, and the colour of the planet matched, with an almost offensive precision, the faint ochre smear that clung to the brass where her thumb now rested.

She turned. Her face held nothing. Not surprise, not triumph, not the particular brightness that a lesser investigator might have allowed herself. She looked at the four people arranged in the narrow space between the telescope and the porthole—Dr. Wren, who had positioned himself slightly too close to the instrument, his hands clasped behind his back in a posture of false and practised authority; Captain Blackwood, who was shifting his weight from one foot to the other and touching the knot of his collar as though it had become suddenly and personally offensive; Mr. Drummond, who was adjusting his cufflinks in the manner of a man who has just remembered that he has a meeting in another room and is therefore in a state of perpetual, low-grade urgency; and Dr. Tanaka, who was very still, almost statue-still, her gaze fixed on the telescope mount with the quiet, focused attention of a botanist studying a

***

Mars hung in the black beyond the curved glass wall like a rusted coin pressed flat against the dark, its rust-coloured surface catching the faint amber of the station's interior lighting and returning it as something almost tender, almost maternal, which was a cruelty in itself, because the planet below was indifferent to the small dramas of the creatures sealed in this glass bubble above its head. The low thrum of the rotation pressed up through the floor and into the soles of bare feet, a vibration so constant that one stopped noticing it until one stopped, and then it was there, that patient mechanical pulse, the heartbeat of the machine that kept them all alive and kept them all together and kept them all, inescapably, in the same room.

It was Drummond who broke the silence, and he broke it the way one breaks a plate at a dinner table where everyone has been pretending not to be hungry. His voice came from the left side of the dome, too loud, too eager, carrying the particular resonance of a man who has rehearsed his opening line in a mirror. "Captain, I must insist we review the quarterly ledger before the Council session convenes." He said *insist* the way other men said *remark*. His hands were broad and open, gesturing at the air as though the quarterly ledger were a physical object he could pick up and set on the telescope mount, and his fingers, as they described their invisible arc, brushed the diamond-encrusted cufflinks at his wrists with a small, unconscious tap, as if checking they were still there, as if the weight of them against his skin was the only thing keeping him tethered to the floor.

Blackwood stood with his hands clasped behind his back, the knuckles white, the tendons in his forearms standing out like the roots of a shallow tree. His jaw worked once, twice, a small grinding motion that might have been the teeth or might have been the memory of teeth. He did not look at Drummond. He looked at the glass, at Mars, at the rusted coin, as though the answer to the quarterly ledger might be written in the oxidised iron of the planet's surface. "I will review the matter," he said, and the words were clipped, precise, the kind of sentence a man constructs in a workshop and bolts shut before anyone can see the seams. "In due course. The Council session is not yet confirmed."

"It is in three days, Captain." Drummond's voice had not dropped. It had not dropped because it had not been raised, which was the point. He was not raising his voice. He was simply being very, very loud, in a register that made the recycled air feel thinner, that made the amber light seem to pull back from the corners of the dome. "And the investors will expect a preliminary accounting. I am merely suggesting we not be caught unprepared." He touched his cufflinks again. The tap-tap-tap of diamond on metal was small and precise and entirely out of proportion to the size of the room.

And then, from the far side of the Observatorium, where the potted fern stood in its grey ceramic pot with its fronds uncurled and patient, Tanaka spoke. Her voice was quiet. It was the volume of a woman reading a label on a seed packet, of a woman describing the germination rate of a particular orchid to a colleague who had asked, politely, whether she might borrow a cutting. "Captain," she said, and the word carried no weight, no insistence, no drummond-broadness. It was simply a name, offered. "I have noted certain inconsistencies in the hydroponic division's supply allocations. They do not correspond to the projected consumption figures in the quarterly report. I wondered whether you had seen the variance." She said *wondered*, and the word was so gentle, so almost apologetic, that it might have been a question about the weather. Her hands rested on the fern's fronds, fingers spread, thumbs pressing lightly into the soft green tissue, as if the plant were a railing she needed to hold onto, as if the living thing beneath her palms were the only fixed point in a room that was spinning, slowly, out of alignment.

Blackwood's head turned. Not toward Tanaka. Toward the glass. Toward Mars. His eyes slid along the curved surface with the particular lateral movement of a man tracking something he does not wish to see, and in the sliding there was a flinch so small that only Tanaka, who was watching with the quiet, focused attention of a botanist studying the angle of a leaf's venation, registered it. The averted gaze. The half-degree of the neck turning away. The jaw tightening again, that small grinding, and the hands behind his back pressing harder, the knuckles going from white to the pale blue of a bruise forming beneath the skin. "I am aware of the supply figures," he said, and his voice had dropped a register, as though he were speaking to the glass rather than to the woman, as though the answer he was giving was meant for the rusted coin and not for the room. "They are being reconciled."

"They have been being reconciled," Tanaka said, and the words were so soft that Drummond, three paces away, did not hear them. "For six weeks."

In the particular silence that followed, the waltz from somewhere in the station's deeper corridors reached the Observatorium in a thin, tinny thread, a melody so distant it was more rhythm than sound, and the thrum of the rotation pressed up through the floor and into the soles of everyone's feet, and Mars turned, slowly, its rusted face shifting a fraction of degrees, and none of the four figures in the dome moved.

It was Wren who moved first. He came from the far side of the room, where he had been standing with his hands in his pockets and his expression arranged into something approximating scholarly interest, and he said, "I believe the investigation will be better served by my presence in the Grand Rotunda. The physical evidence, such as it is, will not rearrange itself." He did not look at Blackwood. He did not look at Drummond. He looked, briefly, at the telescope mount, and his gaze lingered on the brass with a fraction of a second's hesitation that was not quite recognition and not quite avoidance, and then he was walking, his footsteps soft on the alternating walnut and birch, and the glass dome's threshold swallowed him, and the corridor beyond was already taking him toward the Rotunda, toward the body, toward the narrative he was trying to walk into before it was walked into for him.

Sera Okafor, who had been standing near the porthole with her arms crossed and her journalist's stillness, turned to Voss. "The financial discrepancies are troubling," she said, and her voice was level, professional, the voice of a woman filing a copy for the morning's dispatch. "I have noticed inconsistencies in the reports that could indicate manipulation. Have you seen anything unusual?"

Voss, who had been watching Blackwood's back with the patient, cataloguing attention of a woman examining the handwriting of a dead woman's last letter, turned her head a fraction. "Sera Okafor, I have noticed some inconsistencies in the financial reports. They do not add up, and I am concerned about the accuracy of the numbers, especially with the recent fluctuations in the market." She paused. The waltz ticked. "Have you noticed anything unusual in your communications with the stakeholders?"

Sera's arms uncrossed. She looked at Blackwood, who was now facing the corridor, his back to the room, his hands still clasped behind him, the knuckles still blue-white. "The communications are... forthcoming," she said, and the ellipsis was not in her voice but in the word itself, a small gap, a missing syllable, a number that did not appear in the column.

Blackwood turned. Not toward any of them. Toward the corridor, toward the Stakeholders' Salon, toward the next room where the investors would be sitting in their upholstered chairs and pouring their brandy and asking their questions, and where he would be able to arrange the next sentence of the story he had been writing since before the body was cold. "I will review the matter," he said again, and the words were identical to the first, the same bolts, the same seams, and he was walking, and his footsteps were heavier than Wren's, more deliberate, the gait of a man carrying something behind his back that was not a thing but a shape, a structure, a frame, and the corridor took him the way it had taken Wren, and the glass dome was smaller now, the amber light more concentrated, the waltz a little louder.

Tanaka watched the back of his collar. The

***

The Mining Archive smelled of compressed data-sheets and the faint, dry mineral tang of iron oxide, a scent that settled in the back of the throat like the memory of a stone ground to powder. The amber glow of the data-readers cast everything in a flat, even light that permitted no shadows, no corners in which a thought might hide, and the station's slow rotation produced a sub-audible thrum that one felt not in the ears but in the soles of the shoes, in the small bones of the ankle, a vibration so constant and so low that it became, after a time, indistinguishable from one's own pulse. It was, in short, a room designed for the preservation of information, and it was, at this particular moment, the least safe room on the Ares Meridian.

Sera Okafor spoke before the door's pneumatic seal had fully disengaged, her voice pitched at the precise register of a colleague raising a minor concern over tea, though there was no tea, and the concern was anything but minor. "The financial discrepancies are troubling." She did not look at Elara Voss as she said it. She looked, instead, at the row of steel filing cabinets that lined the far wall, her fingers already moving, tapping the edge of a data-sheet with a pen she had not yet uncapped, as though the sentence were a hypothesis she was testing against the ambient temperature of the room. Her eyes, however, had tracked Elara's face from the first syllable, and they tracked it still, cataloguing the micro-expression that might or might not have crossed the Inspector's features in the interval between the word *troubling* and the silence that followed.

Elara Voss's hands were on the desk. They had been on the desk, palms down, fingers extended and perfectly still, since the moment she had seated herself, and they remained so. There was no fidgeting, no drumming, no unconscious rearrangement of the small objects that cluttered the surface: a crumpled quarterly earnings report with a single line struck through in black marker, a financial projection notebook open to a column of figures that did not, by any arithmetic she had yet been able to identify, add up. Her hands were simply there, planted, as though they had been poured in concrete. She regarded Sera with the patient, slightly weary attention of a woman who had been asked the same question in three different phrasings that afternoon and was now hearing a fourth.

"Sera Okafor," she said, and the use of the full name, the full title that followed it unspoken but understood, carried the particular weight of a woman addressing a file rather than a person. "I have noticed certain inconsistencies in the financial reports. They do not add up. I am concerned about the accuracy of the numbers, particularly in light of the recent fluctuations in the market." She paused. The thrum in the floor continued, patient and indifferent. "Have you noticed anything unusual in your communications with the stakeholders?"

The question was delivered with the even, measured cadence of a reading, as though the words had been set down in a notebook and were now being recited for the record. And perhaps they had. Elara Voss did not improvise. Elara Voss catalogued. And in the cataloguing, one suspected, a certain name had already been filed, alphabetised, cross-referenced, and placed in a drawer that was not, at this moment, open.

Sera's pen tapped. Turned. Tapped again. She adjusted the collar of her jacket with both hands, a small, precise gesture that had the appearance of restlessness but the architecture of a shield. "Communications," she repeated, and the word came out lighter than it had gone in, as though she were testing its weight, feeling for the hook. "You mean the routine reporting channels? The quarterly summaries sent to the Consortium board?" Her thumb found the cap of the pen and worked it, round and round, a small mechanical ritual. "Because what I have seen, Inspector, are numbers that appear to have been adjusted. Rounded, perhaps. Or selected. There is a difference between a discrepancy and a curation, and I confess I have not yet determined which I am looking at."

She said this with the easy, slightly amused tone of a journalist describing a mildly inconvenient source, and the ease was itself the most telling thing in the room. Elara did not blink. Her palms remained down. The amber light of the data-readers held them both in its flat, impartial glow, and beneath them, the station turned, and turned, and turned, and somewhere far below, in the colony they could not see but could feel in the slight drag of the gravity well, a woman lay still in a room that was no longer hers, and every question asked in this archive was, in some quiet and inescapable way, a question asked of that stillness.

There were forty-seven documents. The number lived in the space between them, unspoken, a shared and unshared secret, a room they were both standing in and neither would name. Sera knew the number. Elara knew the number. They knew, each of them, that the number contained a name, and that the name was, in a particular and inconvenient arrangement of columns and dates, their own. And so the conversation continued in the careful, circumspect language of two women who were not, strictly speaking, interrogating one another but were instead, and with considerably more danger, measuring the depth of the water on either side of the channel between them, each trying to determine how far the other could see before the current would pull.

Sera's pen found the edge of the data-sheet again. "The stakeholders," she said, and the word was softer now, almost tender, the way one might say the name of a patient one has been treating for some time. "They are, as you know, a particular group. Each with their own ledgers, their own expectations, their own small and private arithmetic. I have been in communication with several of them. Routine matters. Scheduling. Press releases. The usual machinery." She turned a page. Then another. The pen tapped. "I would be surprised if the Inspector had not seen the same correspondence."

It was a trap, and Elara Voss knew it was a trap, and the knowing was not, she suspected, the most interesting part. The interesting part was that the trap was also a question, and the question was also an answer, and the answer was also a card held face-down in a hand that was, at this moment, perfectly still, palms down, fingers extended, on a desk in a room that smelled of iron oxide and compressed data. Elara had seen the correspondence. Elara had seen the channels that ran between Sera Okafor and certain parties in the Stakeholders' Salon, channels that were not, in the strictest and most literal sense of the word, routine. Elara had seen them, filed them, cross-referenced them, and placed them in a drawer. And the drawer was not open. And the card was not played. And the calm that settled over Elara's features in the three seconds that followed was the calm of a woman who had already decided what she needed and was now, with the unhurried precision of a surgeon selecting an instrument, waiting for the precise moment at which to reach for it.

"I have seen a great many things, Miss Okafor," Elara said, and her voice was no louder than the hum in the floor, no more demanding than the amber light. "I have seen the reports. I have seen the projections. I have seen the ledger, silk-covered, with the names half-erased, which I note was found in a drawer that was not, I believe, the drawer in which it was originally placed." She paused. The thrum continued. "I have not, however, seen the full extent of what you have been compiling. I confess the matter is of considerable interest to me, and I should welcome the opportunity to examine it in greater detail. At a later time. When the circumstances are, perhaps, less—" She did not finish the sentence.

***

The silence in the Stakeholders' Salon had a particular quality, a quality that Captain Theron Blackwood had noted on his first evening aboard the Ares Meridian and had never quite been able to articulate to anyone. It was not the silence of an empty room. It was the silence of a room that had been *arranged*—every glass in the crystal decanter's shelf set at precisely the same angle, every corporate crest on the far wall hung at the same height, every strand of the damask drapery falling in a fold that had been smoothed and held in place by the faint, constant pressure of the station's rotation. The hum of the spin pressed against the hull like a slow, patient heartbeat, and in that heartbeat the recycled air moved with the sterile warmth of a well-maintained engine, carrying the faintest ghost of old wood polish and the colder, more honest smell of brushed titanium beneath the veneer.

Blackwood stood at the credenza. His fingers ran along the top edge, knuckles pale, the tendons in his forearms standing out with the particular tension of a man who has been holding his breath for longer than is strictly necessary. He did not hurry. One does not hurry when one is doing one's duty, and duty, in his estimation, was a word that had grown rather heavier in the last six hours.

"Nothing in the drawers. Nothing in the cabinet." He said it to the mahogany, to the row of identical crystal tumblers catching the amber light, to the small brass plate on the credenza's face that read *Ares Meridian Stakeholders' Assembly, Est. 2147*. He said it the way one speaks an order to a subordinate who has failed to follow it. "Then where did you put it, Wren?"

The question hung in the air for a moment, absurd and intimate, a man talking to a ghost in a room that was not, in any meaningful sense, a graveyard. He dismissed it. He did not dismiss it. He ran his thumb along the seam where the credenza's top panel met the frame, feeling for the faintest irregularity, the hairline gap that would indicate a false back, a hidden compartment, a space no wider than a finger's width. There was nothing. Of course there was nothing. He had checked it twice already.

He turned. The salon stretched before him in its full, gilded quiet: the long table with its place settings for eight, the silver serving dishes still polished to a mirror sheen, the two Persian carpets whose patterns had been chosen by someone with a taste for restraint and a budget that made the word *restraint* a lie. The porthole on the far wall showed the rust-brown curve of Mars, vast and indifferent, its thin atmosphere a smear of butterscotch against the black. Blackwood looked at the planet the way one looks at a bill one has not yet opened. He looked away.

His shoulders were rigid. This was, he reflected with a cold and clinical detachment that he found more comforting than it had any right to be, a military posture. Shoulders back. Weight even. Economy of movement. He had been taught it on the Academy grounds on Luna, in the grey November light, by an instructor who had once pulled a man's rank for the crime of shifting his weight from one foot to the other during a inspection. Blackwood had not shifted his weight in twenty-three years. He did not intend to start now, in a room that smelled of beeswax and cold metal, with the door to the corridor sealed and the lock engaged and the corridor beyond it empty.

He moved to the table. Not quickly. One does not move quickly when one is cataloguing. He set his palm flat on the tabletop, felt the grain of the wood through the varnish, and then he lifted the silver tray that held the crystal decanter and the two tumblers. Beneath it, the wood was bare and clean. He replaced the tray. He lifted the napkin ring, checked the space beneath. He turned the decanter in his hand, felt the weight of it, set it back. Each action was a small, precise ceremony. Each action was also, he knew with the flat certainty of a man who has rehearsed the sequence in the dark of his cabin while the rest of the station slept, a performance. A *thorough* performance. The kind of performance that would look, to an Inspector who arrived in the next ten minutes with her grey eyes and her patient, cataloguing gaze, like the conscientious work of a captain doing his duty in the wake of a murder and a suspected embezzlement.

The truth, as it so often was, was considerably less dramatic than one might expect.

He did not know, precisely, what he was looking for. Or rather, he knew the *shape* of what he was looking for. A document. A page. A small, flat thing that could be tucked behind a panel or folded into a seam or pressed into the hollow space beneath a decorative molding. Something that, in the right hand, in the right moment, in the right light, would read as *evidence*. Something that would say, in the quiet, unassailable language of figures and dates and signatures, that Dr. Cassius Wren had moved the colony's ore-yield funds through a chain of accounts that did not, strictly speaking, exist. Something that would make the Inspector's grey eyes settle on Wren's face and stay there, and stay there, and stay there.

He checked the decorative panel above the fireplace. He checked it again. He ran his fingers along the molding, felt the grain, felt the faint vibration of the station's rotation traveling up through the deck plating and into the soles of his boots. He checked the space behind the small framed print of the colony's founding ceremony, the one that showed a dozen figures in pressure suits standing on red dust, their faces turned toward a sky that was not quite a sky. He set the frame back. He checked the space behind it again.

His eyes went to the door. Then to the corner. Then to the porthole, where Mars turned its slow, rust-colored face. Then back to the credenza. Then to the table. Then to the door. The rhythm of it was mechanical, the rhythm of a man checking the perimeter of a position under fire, except that there was no fire, and no enemy, and no one in the corridor to fire at him. There was only the hum, and the amber light, and the particular quality of the silence that said *no one is here, no one will be here, no one will corroborate what you have just done, and that is, in its way, the most important thing of all.*

He thought of the Inspector. He did not think of her face. He thought of her *method*. The way she filed things. The way she catalogued. The way she had stood in the Observatorium with her hands clasped behind her back and her eyes moving from one evasive answer to the next with the quiet, patient attention of a woman reading a dead woman's handwriting. If she arrived and found the salon undisturbed, she would file that. If she arrived and found the salon *disturbed*—a panel lifted, a drawer pulled, a decanter set at a slightly different angle—she would file that too. And the second filing was the one he needed. The second filing said: *the Captain searched. The Captain searched thoroughly. The Captain found something.* And what the Captain found, in the Captain's telling, was Wren's.

It was, he admitted to himself in the clipped, order-giving grammar of his own internal voice, a rather neat piece of work. Neat and clean and entirely, in the technical sense, a lie. But it was a lie that kept the station funded. A lie that kept the crew fed. A lie that kept the Ares Meridian spinning in its slow, patient orbit above the red dirt, turning and turning and turning, because if the board stripped his command, if the colony's accounts were opened and the forty-seven documents were read and the numbers were *added up*, the station would be decommissioned. The crew would be scattered. The mining operations would halt. And the people in the mess hall, the people in the hydroponic bay, the people who had no names on the stakeholder list and no seat at the long table with its silver serving dishes, those people would be the ones who paid for the arithmetic.

He did not enjoy this. He noted that he did not enjoy this with the same flat, clinical detachment with which he had noted the grain of the wood and the weight of the decanter. He was not a man who enjoyed. He was a man who *did*.

He moved to the far wall. The corporate crests. The Ashworth Mining Consortium, its gold leaf still bright. The Drummond-Voss Capital Partners, their interlocking initials in brushed steel. The small, unassuming plate for the Colonial Authority, its seal worn smooth by years of hands that had touched it and moved on. He ran his fingers along the base of the Ashworth crest. He lifted the edge of the steel plate. He checked the space behind it. He set it back.

And then he stopped.

He stopped in the middle of the motion, his hand still raised, his fingers still touching the edge of the steel plate, and he simply *knew*. The knowing came the way a tide comes: not all at once, not with a crash, but with a slow, inevitable pressure that built behind the sternum until the only rational response was to stop moving. He knew where it was. He had always known where it was. He had known since the moment he had set it there, hours ago, in the blue half-light of the early shift, when the salon had been empty and the station had been quiet and the only sound had been the hum and the faint, cold trickle of the fountain in the corner. He had known because he had put it there. He had known because he had chosen the spot with the particular care of a man selecting a firing position. And he had known, in the back of his mind, in the part of his mind that did not enjoy, that he would come back. That the search would be a search in name only. That his fingers would find the seam, and the panel, and the small, flat thing tucked behind the molding with the ease of a man reaching for a glass he had set down on a table an hour before and could still feel the weight of in his palm.

He set the steel plate back. He did not go to the molding. He did not need to. He simply stood there, in the amber light, in the gilded silence, and let the knowing settle into his bones like the station's rotation settling into his feet.

Then he moved. Not quickly. One does not move quickly. He crossed the salon in four measured steps, his boots marking the alternating walnut and birch floor with a soft, even rhythm. He set his hand behind the decorative panel. His fingers found the seam. His fingers found the small, flat thing.

He closed his hand around it.

He

***

The droplet was singular. Not a pool, not a smear, not the generous arterial spray one might expect from a body struck in the head or the chest. One perfect crimson bead, clinging to the white ceramic lip of the fountain with the tenacious, almost decorative precision of a jewel set in a ring. It caught the pale green wash of the grow-lamps above and held it there, a garnet in a setting of porcelain, and for a moment the only thing that moved in the Hydroponic Conservatory was the slow, meditative trickle of water over the fountain's inner basin, lapping and lapping against the ceramic like a tide in a very small, very quiet sea.

Dr. Yuki Tanaka stood before it. Her reflection was warped in the still water beneath the droplet—her face stretched, her eyes doubled, her grey lab coat rippling in a current that did not exist. She did not speak. She did not call out. She did not perform, for the benefit of any unseen witness, the appropriate exclamation of a woman who has found blood where blood ought not to be. She simply reached down. Her thumbnail pressed the droplet with a faint, wet click, and the bead split, sliding down the ceramic in a thin red thread that dissolved into the water below. The metallic tang rose to her tongue before she could stop it, that cold copper taste she had known a hundred times in the laboratory, the taste of hemoglobin breaking down in solution. She swallowed it. She wiped her thumb on the hem of her coat, a small, mechanical gesture, the way one wipes a chisel clean before the next cut.

Around her, the conservatory breathed. Ten thousand leaves exhaled their chlorophyll into the humid air, a green, vegetal exhalation that pressed against her cheeks and dampened the hair at her temples. The drip-irrigation pipes ticked their slow, arrhythmic count along the ceiling trusses—tick, tick, tick—a sound like a clock in a house where no one lived. The grow-lamps hummed their low, electric hymn, and the faint ozone smell of their discharge mingled with the loam-dark scent of engineered soil and the sweet, almost cloying perfume of the orchids in their glass cases. It was, she thought, the smell of a cathedral after the candles have gone out. Vast. Green. Silent. And slightly, irrevocably wrong.

In the Grand Rotunda, three corridors away and a full rotation-cycle distant, the atmosphere was of a different order. Dr. Cassius Wren had approached Inspector Elara Voss with the easy, slightly slouched posture of a man who wished to appear unburdened by the morning's events, and he had said, with the casual precision of a geologist noting a vein in a rock face, "The atmosphere in here is quite tense, isn't it? I can't help but wonder what everyone is hiding." It was a question, of course. Or rather, it was not a question at all. It was a stone dropped into a still pond, placed there to see which ripples reached the Inspector's face. She had received it with the particular stillness of a woman who has catalogued a dead woman's handwriting and found, in the slant of the letters, a confession she had not expected. Around them, Felix Drummond had leaned against the marble balustrade with his arms crossed, his expression arranged into the mild, pained concern of a man watching a chess match he had already lost, and he had turned to Wren and said something—something about the colony's quarterly figures, something about the weight of shared responsibility—that was, in its careful phrasing, a shield held up to deflect a blow he had not yet seen coming. Voss, meanwhile, had knelt beside the leather armchair, her fingers tracing the seam of the cushion with the patience of a woman reading a letter she suspected to be a threat, and she had found nothing. Or rather, she had found the absence of something, which in her experience was often more eloquent than the thing itself.

But the conservatory did not care about the Rotunda. The conservatory cared only about the tick of its pipes, the green breath of its leaves, and the small red thread still dissolving in the fountain's water, spreading its faint pink halo outward in concentric rings that touched the ceramic wall and faded.

Tanaka knelt. Not crouched—kneeled, both knees pressing into the engineered soil with a deliberate, almost reverent slowness, as though she were tending a bed of seedlings rather than investigating a crime. The soil was cool and damp beneath her palms, and it clung to her fingers with the soft, granular adhesion of well-tilted earth. She traced the rim of the fountain with her fingertips, following the ceramic's smooth curve, and then her hand slid forward, past the base, past the small mossy terraces where the irrigation channels ran, and stopped.

Stopped, because the soil here was wrong.

She had known this soil. She had mixed it herself, in the small laboratory off the east corridor, adjusting the pH with a careful hand, calibrating the mineral content to the precise specification required by the gene-seed vaults that stood in their glass cases just beyond the fountain's shadow. She had planned, in the quiet hours between one rotation and the next, to return here with a trowel and a set of sealed vials. She had planned to break the casing, to lift the rare specimens from their cradles of nutrient gel, to press them into the vials with the steady fingers of a woman who had performed a thousand delicate grafts. She had planned to sell them. Not to the station. Not to the colony. To the terraforming corporation on Phobos, whose agent had written to her in a cipher she had not needed to break because the message was, in its essence, a simple and very expensive question. She had planned to do this in three days. Perhaps four. After the rotation shifted the vaults into the shadow of the station's spine, where the cameras would not see.

But the soil was already turned.

She looked down. A section of earth, no larger than her open palm, had been disturbed. The granules were loose, freshly broken, and in the centre of the disturbance there was a faint impression—a crescent, a slight drag, the ghost of a shoe sole pressed into the moisture. She pressed her thumb into the impression and felt the soil give way, soft and yielding, as though it had been turned only minutes ago. The smell was different here. Not the loam-dark sweetness of the rest of the conservatory, but something sharper, something that carried the faint, acrid undertone of heated glass and the ozone tang of the grow-lamps, as though the casing beneath had been broken and resealed in haste.

Her hands were steady. She wanted to note this, the steadiness, with the same clinical detachment she would apply to a leaf whose edges were browning at the tips. She catalogued the blood on the fountain by shape: a single bead, hemispherical, consistent with a capillary break, a small wound, a finger pressed too hard against a sharp edge. She catalogued the soil by texture: granular, slightly clumped, the mineral content consistent with the east-bed mix she had prepared herself. She catalogued the impression by depth: two centimetres, perhaps three, the pressure of a single step, not a drag, not a struggle. Data. Shapes. Origins. If she treated it as data, she did not have to treat it as a person's death. She did not have to sit in this green silence and think the name of the woman whose body was somewhere below, in the colony, in the red dust that was not dust but the pulverised bones of a world.

In the Crew's Mess, Captain Theron Blackwood was already moving, his heavy boots thudding against the alternating walnut and birch floor with the purposeful rhythm of a man who had a list to cross off and a frame to build. He was gathering information, or rather, he was performing the gathering of information, asking the same three questions of three different crew members in the same order, watching their faces for the particular flinch that indicated a lie. In the Mining Archive, the stacks were silent, and the forty-seven documents in the open drawer still bore the dead woman's name in neat, orderly rows, and the air smelled of old paper and the faint, sweet decay of a secret that had been kept too long. And in the corridor leading to the Grand Rotunda, Sera Okafor was walking with the measured pace of a journalist who had learned, in three years aboard the Ares Meridian, that the most important information was the information no one was yet asking for.

But the conservatory did not care about the Rotunda, or the Mess, or the Archive. The conservatory cared only about the trowel.

Tanaka found it where she left it, in the small tool rack beside the gene-seed racks, its handle worn smooth by her own grip. She drew it from the rack with the ease of a woman taking up a pen, and she knelt again, and she dug. Two inches. Three. The trowel bit into the soil with a soft, granular whisper, and the earth fell away in small, dark clumps that caught the grow-light and scattered tiny motes of mineral dust into the humid air. She expected, in the back of her mind, the cold surprise of a bone, or the hard edge of a weapon, or the soft thread of a torn garment. A thread. A single, dark thread that would tie the blood on the fountain to a body in the colony, and the disturbed soil to a hand other than her own.

The trowel struck glass.

Not the soft give of soil, not the hollow ring of a hollow space. Glass. Solid, seamless, and warm. She pressed her palm flat against the casing and felt the heat of it, a low, residual warmth that had no business being there in the cool, shaded belly of the conservatory. The glass was warm as though it had been handled recently. As though someone had broken in, taken what they needed, and resealed the casing with a heat tool, and then walked away, leaving the soil disturbed and the glass still breathing its small, artificial warmth into the green air.

She pulled her hand back.

She looked at the fountain. The blood, which had been a crimson bead, was now a faint pink ring in the water, spreading slowly, almost beautifully, like a drop of ink in a glass. She looked at the soil. The disturbed patch. The shoe impression. The warmth of the glass beneath her trowel. And she understood, with the slow, terrible clarity of a woman watching a plant she has tended for years turn, leaf by leaf, toward a light she did not know was there, that the secret she had been keeping was no longer a secret. That the soil she was investigating was the soil she had meant to dig. That the crime she was cataloguing was, in part, the crime she had planned. That the warm glass beneath her fingers was not evidence of a stranger's intrusion but of a collision—two hands, two intentions, two small violences meeting in the dark soil of a greenhouse on a station orbiting a red and silent world.

She smoothed the dirt flat. Not with the trowel—with her palm. She pressed her hand into the turned earth and drew it across the surface in one long, slow stroke, and the granules settled, and the shoe impression faded, and the small, disturbed patch became, again, indistinguishable from the rest of the bed. She smoothed it the way one smooths a blanket over a sleeping child. The way one erases a word from a page before anyone else can read it.

And then she whispered a single word. It was very small. It was barely a word at all, more a shape made by the lips, a breath shaped into a sound. "No one." It could have been a prayer. It could have been a denial. It could have been a threat addressed not to another person

***

Inspector Elara Voss had not yet looked up. Her fingertips pressed into the worn velvet of the armchair's cushion with the particular tenderness of a woman examining a patient's pulse, feeling for the crease, the thread, the faint discoloration that might betray a chemical, a poison, a truth. The fabric was old, well-loved by the station's rotating gravity, and it yielded beneath her touch with a soft, almost organic sigh. Behind her, in the amber half-light of the Rotunda where the brass fittings caught the recirculated glow and threw it back in long, lazy arcs, the body of Margaret Ashworth lay where it had fallen. Or rather, where it had been placed. The distinction was one that Inspector Voss had not yet permitted herself to make, and she would not make it now, not while her fingers worked the seam of the cushion and the centrifugal hum of the Ares Meridian pressed its constant, low note against the soles of her shoes.

"The atmosphere in here is quite tense, isn't it?"

The voice arrived from somewhere to her left, unhurried, almost conversational, as though the speaker were commenting on the barometric pressure before a rain squall. Dr. Cassius Wren stood in the middle of the Rotunda's broad floor, where the alternating walnut and birch panels curved gently upward toward the central axis, and he regarded the assembled company with the easy, open expression of a man who had rehearsed his alibi in a mirror and found it wanting nothing. His hands were open at his sides. His shoulders were loose. He did not shift his weight, did not touch his face, did not perform the small, involuntary gestures that betray a guilty man's need to rearrange himself in space. He simply stood, and smiled, and the smile was so perfectly calibrated that it seemed less a facial expression than a structural feature, as natural and load-bearing as the brass railing along which a single strand of black hair hung at a precise, unnatural angle, defying the gentle lean that the station's spin imposed upon all loose matter.

"I can't help but wonder," he continued, addressing the room rather than any one person, "what everyone is hiding."

He said it with a smile. He said it to the air. And in the air, Felix Drummond's cologne—sandalwood and something sharper, something that had been applied with the confidence of a man who believed that if he smelled expensive enough, no one would think to look closely—cut through the silence like a hand reaching for a door handle that was not there.

Inspector Voss did not turn. She completed her examination of the cushion's seam, felt the stitching hold, felt nothing beneath it, and only then rose to her full height with the slow, deliberate economy of a woman who had decided that her stillness was a weapon. She turned. She looked at Dr. Wren. She did not fidget. She did not lean forward. She simply looked at him until the looking became a pressure, a weight, a small and patient violence.

"Dr. Wren," she said. "I believe you were in the Mining Archive before the alarm. Could you tell me more about your movements, and what precisely you were doing there?"

The question was clean, professional, and entirely the wrong question. In her mind, the architecture of the crime was already built: a financial irregularity, a misappropriation of funds, a body that had been the unfortunate byproduct of a very expensive argument over very expensive numbers. Margaret Ashworth had been, in Inspector Voss's working theory, a woman who had discovered too much about where the colony's money had been going, and had been silenced for it. The wine glass on the side table beside the armchair—still warm, she would note, still holding its faint trace of bitter almond—was, in her mind, the instrument of a poisoned negotiation. The black hair in the railing was a detail to be filed, cross-referenced, set aside.

Wren inclined his head. "The Mining Archive," he repeated, as though tasting the words. "Yes. I was reviewing the mineral assay reports for the southern shaft. Routine work, really. The colony's yield projections for the next quarter required verification, and I found that a certain number of the figures did not, in my estimation, quite add up." He paused. He smiled again. "You understand, Inspector, when one is dealing with ore compositions and geological formations, the margin for error is a matter of considerable professional sensitivity. I was simply doing what any conscientious geologist would do. Checking the numbers."

He said it as though he were describing the weather. As though the Mining Archive were a garden he had been tending, and the forty-seven documents bearing the dead woman's name were nothing more than a particularly stubborn weed.

"Conscientious," Felix Drummond said. The word came out slightly too loud, slightly too quick, as though it had been sitting on his tongue and he had been waiting for a gap in the conversation in which to deposit it. He stood near the Rotunda's curved wall, where the portholes offered a thin, rust-colored sliver of Mars beyond the glass, and he was touching his left cuff. Then his right. Then, with a small, unconscious grace, the back of his neck. "I must say, Dr. Wren, I share your concern about the accuracy of the numbers. There have been recent fluctuations in the market that are, shall we say, rather unsettling. One wonders whether the figures we have been presented with are, in fact, the figures we have been *meant* to see."

He was touching his left cuff again. Then the cologne bottle in his breast pocket, where his fingers lingered a moment longer than necessary. Then the back of his neck. The same sequence. Twice. A man in a suit two sizes too expensive, performing the small rituals of a man whose quarterly dividends were about to be audited and who had discovered, with the cold, dawning horror of a man who has stepped into a puddle he did not know was there, that the water was much deeper than he had imagined.

"Mr. Drummond," Inspector Voss said, and her voice was quiet, and it was not kind. "Your concern is noted. I shall bear it in mind."

She turned back to Wren. He was watching Felix with an expression of mild, academic interest, the way one watches a particularly agitated insect in a glass jar. His eyes did not blink. His jaw did not tighten. He simply watched, and in his watching there was an inventory, a quiet and methodical cataloguing: who would flee, who would be caught, who would be the most convenient suspect when the investigation turned and the wrong theory crumbled like dry soil.

Sera Okafor stood a little to the side, near the brass railing where the black hair hung in its precise, gravity-defying angle. She held a small notebook in her left hand and a pen in her right, and the pen was turning. Round and round between her thumb and forefinger, a small, metronomic motion that had nothing to do with thought and everything to do with time. Her eyes had moved, in the last two minutes, from the warm wine glass on the side table to the strand of hair in the railing and back again, and back again, and each time her gaze passed over them it lingered a fraction longer, the way a jeweler's eye lingers over a stone that might be genuine or might be glass. Her mouth, meanwhile, produced a small, agreeable sound.

"Yes, yes, quite, one does wonder about the numbers, very interesting indeed," she said, to no one, to the room, to the slow indifferent spin of the station around its axis. Her pen turned. Her eyes did not.

Captain Blackwood stood near the far wall, where the Rotunda's curvature brought the floor gently upward, and he was watching all of them with the particular stillness of a man who has already decided what he is going to say and is waiting for the precise moment to say it. Dr. Tanaka, the botanist, stood slightly behind him, her hands clasped before her, her eyes fixed on the armchair where Inspector Voss had been searching. She had been in the Hydroponic Conservatory an hour ago, kneeling in the soil,

The knuckles were white. Pressed flat against the laminate surface of the mess table, spread wide as though he were trying to feel the grain of the material beneath, as though the answer might be written in the molecular structure of the polymer. Captain Theron Blackwood did not look up. He did not need to look up. The proximity chime at the door had not sounded. It had not sounded in eleven minutes. In eleven minutes, no footfall had disturbed the alternating walnut and birch plating of the deck, no shadow had fallen across the flat, institutional wash of the overhead panels, no breath had disturbed the antiseptic film that still clung to the air like a second skin.

He was alone.

The station hummed beneath him. That low, omnipresent thrum of the Ares Meridian's rotation, the sound one forgot until one stood very still and listened, a vibration less heard than felt, rising through the soles of his boots and settling in the teeth. Beneath that hum, fainter, the recycled air system breathed its slow mechanical respiration. And beneath both, the faintest metallic tang. Antiseptic. The ghost of a chemical solution scrubbed into laminate and steel. The smell of a body that had lain here, on that very table, and had since been carried away. The smell of what had been, lingering in the molecules like a name one cannot quite forget.

Blackwood crouched. His spine remained rigid, the vertebrae stacked in a column that spoke of twenty-two years in command, of a man who had learned long ago that a bent back was a concession. He pressed his thumb along the seam where the table's edge met the bulkhead, feeling for a gap, a hairline fracture, the telltale give of a panel that had been detached and reattached. His fingers were dry. He had wiped them on his trouser leg a moment ago, and the faint lint that clung to them now caught in the flat overhead light like something alive.

"Where the hell did you put it, Wren."

The words were clipped, barely above a whisper, addressed to no one and to everyone. A mutter. A prayer. He did not repeat them.

He rose to his full height and turned, his movement in a single straight line, no wasted gesture, no pivot of the hips. He did not pace. He did not run his hands through his hair or press a palm to his forehead in the manner of a man who has lost something and cannot say what. He walked to the nearest drawer. The mess had six tables, four wall-mounted storage units, a recycling alcove set into the far bulkhead, and a narrow service corridor that led to the galley. He had already cleared the service corridor. He had already cleared the galley. He was, in the precise and surgical sense of the word, thorough.

The first drawer resisted. Not locked, merely swollen with the slight thermal differential of the station's rotation cycle, the wood expanding by a fraction of a millimetre. He applied pressure with the heel of his palm, steady and unhurried, and it yielded with a soft click. Inside: a crate of ration packets, a pair of chipped ceramic mugs, a folded napkin still bearing the faint ring of a glass that had been set down in haste. He lifted each item in turn, set it aside, examined the base of the drawer, the back panel, the little metal clip that held the drawer rail in its channel. Nothing. He replaced the items in the order he had removed them. The second drawer. The third. The same liturgy. Lift, examine, replace. His hands moved with the patience of a man defusing a mechanism, each motion deliberate, each joint articulated.

It was in the fourth drawer that his fingers paused. Not because he found something, but because he found a displacement. The ration packets had been shifted. Two of them sat at an angle that did not correspond to the way a crew member set them down after a meal. They had been moved. Pushed aside. And beneath them, the drawer's base bore a faint impression, a ghost of a rectangle, as though a flat object had rested there and been removed. He pressed his index finger into the impression. The laminate was cool. The impression was shallow. He withdrew his finger and stared at it for a long moment, the flat light above casting no shadow, no depth, nothing for the eye to rest upon.

A thought moved through him, cold and precise, the way a blade moves through water. Had someone been here before him? Had someone, in the chaos of the last hour, in the confusion of the body's discovery and the subsequent scattering of the crew into the Rotunda and the Archive and the Conservatory, had someone been here, in this room, in this drawer, and found what he was looking for? Or had they not found it, and he was the second to search, and the second to come up empty?

He did not allow the thought to complete itself. He moved to the next unit.

The mess was silent in a way that was not true silence. The station was not silent. It could not be silent. The rotation, the air recyclers, the distant thrum of the magnetic coupling that held the Ares Meridian in its orbit around the rust-coloured sphere below. But the mess was silent in the way a held breath is silent, in the way a room is silent when the person who has just left it has taken the sound with them. And Blackwood was aware, with the particular acuity of a man who has spent his career in the company of other people, of the absence. The absence of Sera Okafor's low murmur, of Felix Drummond's restless tapping, of the Inspector's measured, patient questions. They were in the Grand Rotunda, he knew. He could feel their presence the way one feels a change in barometric pressure, a shift in the atmospheric weight that has nothing to do with the weather. Wren was there, performing his charm, his effortless charm, deflecting the Inspector's questions about the Mining Archive with the smoothness of a man who has rehearsed the deflection a hundred times in the mirror. Drummond was there, too, his concerns about the accuracy of the numbers bleeding through his careful diction like ink through thin paper. And Tanaka had moved, or was moving, toward the Observatorium, drawing her evidence to her like a gardener drawing water to the roots.

None of them were here. None of them would think to be here, at fifteen hundred hours, in the room where the body had lain, in the room where the antiseptic still stung the nostrils. That was his trust. That was his architecture. He had built his position, his command, his careful and meticulous redirection of the station's investigations, upon the assumption that no one would come to the mess. That the mess was a place of routine, of ration bars and lukewarm coffee, of the small social rituals that kept a crew of twelve from eating one another in the slow weeks of a Mars orbit. No one searched a mess. No one ransacked a mess.

He lifted the panel above the recycling unit. It was a thin sheet of composite, hinged at the bottom, and it swung open with a faint metallic groan that seemed, in the silence, almost obscene. Behind it, the recycling bin sat in its alcove, its interior a pale, utilitarian grey. He reached in. His fingers brushed the rough inner wall, the compressed pellets of processed waste, the faint chemical residue of the disintegration cycle. He did not find what he was looking for. He withdrew his hand, wiped it on his sleeve, and turned to the next surface.

The service corridor. The galley. The wall-mounted units along the port bulkhead. He moved through them in sequence, a man following a route he had laid out in his mind before he entered the room, each surface a node in a network, each drawer a question to which he already knew the answer. The answer was: not here. Not here. Not here. And the not-here accumulated, layer upon layer, a sediment of negative space, until the room itself became a shape defined by its absences.

It was behind the recycling unit that his fingers closed around the edge of something.
