There is a particular quality of silence that belongs to a sealed room. Not the absence of sound, precisely, but the presence of a sound so constant and so low that the ear ceases to register it as sound at all. The Observatorium of the Ares Meridian had such a silence. The centrifugal drive hummed beneath the deck plating at a frequency just below the threshold of hearing, a vibration felt more in the sternum than in the eardrums, and the recycled air whispered through its ducts with a softness that might, in a quieter moment, have been mistaken for breathing. It was into this silence that a single smudge of rust-coloured particulate had been laid, and it was this smudge that, in the end, would prove more eloquent than any confession.

It sat upon the brass mount of the great refracting telescope, a faint smear no wider than a thumbnail, catching the pale light that filtered through the observation port in a way that made it look almost deliberate. A smudge of red. Not the grey-brown residue that accumulated on the station's surfaces over months of habitation, nor the pale chalk of calcium deposits from the water recyclers. Red. The specific, unmistakable red of iron-oxide regolith, the colour of the world below the station, a world that no person aboard the Ares Meridian had set foot upon in the last eleven months.

Dr. Yuki Tanaka's fingertip hovered a centimetre above the smudge. She did not touch it. One does not touch a wound one has not yet identified, and this small smear of foreign matter, in her estimation, was precisely that. She stood beside the telescope with the particular stillness of a woman who has spent her professional life tending living things, her fingers slightly parted, her posture a careful equilibrium between curiosity and restraint. The faint smell of hydroponic soil clung to the hem of her coat, a green and mineral scent that did not belong in a room of brass and glass, and she did not bother to brush it away.

"That is not station dust, Inspector." Her voice was soft, almost reverent, the way one might speak to a seedling one has just uncovered beneath a layer of mulch. "That is Mars."

The word landed in the Observatorium with the flat, definitive weight of a small hammer striking a nail. Inspector Elara Voss, who had entered the room three minutes prior with the unhurried purpose of a woman walking through a garden she had already mapped, turned her head slowly toward the telescope. She did not fidget. She did not adjust her collar or smooth her hair. She simply looked, and in the looking, she saw what Tanaka had seen: the red, the impossible, the small violation of a sealed environment.

"Who else has been here," Voss said. It was not a question. It was a statement awaiting confirmation, or denial.

Before either woman could answer, the door to the Observatorium opened, and Captain Theron Blackwood entered.

He moved the way a man moves when he has already decided what the room will be. His boots struck the deck plating with a measured, almost metronomic cadence, and his hands were clasped behind his back, fingers interlocked so tightly that the knuckles showed white. He was a tall man, broad through the shoulders, and he filled the Observatorium the way a captain's chair fills a bridge: by occupying the space as though it had been built to his dimensions. His eyes swept the room once, quickly, cataloguing. The telescope. The observation port. The two women. He did not linger on any of them.

"Doctor Tanaka," he said. His voice was clipped, military, a sentence delivered as though it were an order and an apology simultaneously. "Inspector. I trust you are making progress."

"Captain." Voss's reply was a single syllable, neither warm nor cold.

Blackwood's gaze settled, briefly, on the telescope mount. On the smudge. His jaw tightened, a small muscular contraction that might have been a swallow, or might have been the suppression of a question he had decided, in the half-second between seeing and speaking, was not one he would ask. He turned instead to Voss.

"I need to know your whereabouts just before the alarm sounded," he said. Short. Flat. The words arrived in a single breath, as though he were reading them from a card. "Were you in the Mining Archive?"

The half-beat before the phrase. The slight, almost imperceptible drag on the words, as though they had to be pulled from somewhere beneath the surface. Voss looked at him for a moment. Then she looked at the telescope. Then back at him.

"I was in the Grand Rotunda, Captain. I have been in the Grand Rotunda for the better part of an hour." She said this the way one states a fact about the weather. Unremarkable. Not to be questioned.

Blackwood nodded. His hands, behind his back, shifted. The fingers unclasped, then reclasped. A small, involuntary repetition, the kind of gesture that betrays a man who is thinking not about the question he has just asked, but about the question he is afraid someone else will ask.

It was at this moment that the door opened again, and Sera Okafor entered the Observatorium.

She moved differently from Blackwood. Where his entrance was a declaration, hers was a negotiation. She smoothed the collar of her jacket as she crossed the threshold, a small, self-soothing gesture, and her eyes moved across the room with the quick, assessing sweep of a woman who is building a sentence in her mind and has not yet decided where the verb will land. She carried her recording device at her hip, and her fingers kept returning to it, a light tap,

The sandalwood struck first. It was not a violent intrusion—nothing so crude as a shout or a slammed door—but a slow, cloying tide that rolled through the Stakeholders' Salon and settled over the low amber lighting like a second atmosphere, thicker than the station's recycled air, sweeter, and altogether more suffocating. Felix Drummond stood by the window facing the Martian plain, his back to the room, and for the third time in as many minutes he straightened his cufflinks with a pair of fingers that seemed to have forgotten the natural geometry of a handshake. The little golden discs caught the light and threw it back in tiny, indifferent stars.

Dr. Cassius Wren watched him from the far end of the long table, where a glass of sherry sat untouched beside a crystal decanter that had not been poured from in some considerable time. The geologist was still. He had been still since the morning, since the body had been found in the Mining Archive with its warm wine glass and its strand of black hair, and his stillness had the quality of a predator that has already decided where to strike and is merely waiting for the prey to present its throat. His fingers rested on the edge of a leather folio, and beneath that stillness, a pen clicked. Once. Twice. A small, mechanical sound, no louder than a knuckle cracking, but in the hush of the salon it had the weight of a hammer on an anvil.

"Drummond," Wren said, and his voice carried the particular cadence of a man who has prepared a question the way a surgeon prepares a scalpel—coolly, with the full knowledge of what tissue it will part. "I trust you have had the opportunity to review the quarterly assay summaries?"

It was, of course, not a question about summaries. It was a question about the numbers. The raw core samples. The figures that Wren had spent three sleepless nights in the laboratory altering, smoothing, inflating the iron and chromium yields until they matched the projections Margaret Ashworth had demanded for the Interplanetary Trade Council filing. A question about whether the man in the sandalwood had cross-referenced the assay data against the geological records, or whether he had simply signed the dividend cheques the consortium mailed each quarter and tucked them into the same drawer where his Earth-side gambling debts accumulated like sediment in a riverbed.

Drummond turned. He turned slowly, the way a man turns when he is not certain he wishes to be seen turning, and his hand found the edge of the door handle behind him. He held it there for a moment—fingers spread, thumb pressing the cold metal—then withdrew it as though the handle had burned him. He shifted his weight from one foot to the other. Adjusted his tie. Adjusted it again.

"I have glanced at them," Drummond said. "A bit. The figures are, shall we say, *robust*." He let the word hang in the air between them, a small fish on a hook, and watched Wren's face with the patient, appraising gaze of a man reading a ledger. "One wonders how robust. One wonders whether the accuracy is, in the final analysis, *accurate*."

In the Grand Rotunda, three corridors away, Dr. Yuki Tanaka knelt beside the armchair and ran her fingers along the seam where the leather met the base, feeling for the telltale give of a hidden compartment. Captain Blackwood stood behind her, his hands in his pockets, saying nothing, his jaw working in the way it worked when he was composing a sentence he intended to use later, in a room where other people would not hear. The station hummed around them—the deep, subsonic thrum of the rotation, the whispered fragments of crew voices in the adjacent corridor, the soft tick of the chronometer marking each passing second with the indifference of a metronome in an empty concert hall.

And in the Observatorium, Sera Okafor pressed her face close to the brass mount of the telescope and tilted her head, studying the smudge of red Martian dust that had no business existing in a sealed environment. Captain Blackwood had appeared at the doorway, and she had not looked up. She had simply said, very mildly, "It is peculiar, isn't it? A thread of condensation where there should be none. As though someone had been here. As though someone had *left*." He had not answered. She did not expect him to.

Back in the Stakeholders' Salon, the silence had thickened. Wren set his sherry glass down on the table. He set it down a fraction too hard, and the crystal rang against the wood with a small, clear note that both men heard and neither acknowledged. The tendons in his neck stood out, thin as wire, and he pressed his tongue against the inside of his cheek in the way he did when he was calculating—when he was weighing how much truth he could concede before the conversation ceased to be a conversation and became an interrogation.

"You speak of accuracy," Wren said, and his voice was the voice of a lecturer addressing a seminar of second-year students, patient and faintly condescending. "I must ask, Drummond, what precisely do you mean by that word in the context of the consortium's quarterly filings. Are you referring to the geological assay figures, or are you referring to the dividend distributions? Because I confess the distinction is not always, in my experience, as clear as one might wish."

Drummond smiled. It was a small smile, the kind a man gives a waiter who has brought the wrong wine. "I am merely an investor, Dr. Wren. A small shareholder. I look at the numbers. The numbers look at me. And I find that the two of us do not always agree on what the numbers *are*." He reached for his tumbler of brandy, which sat at his elbow, gone cloudy amber in the low light. He lifted it. He set it down. He did not drink. "There is a discrepancy. A small one. A fluctuation. The sort of thing one might attribute to market conditions, or to the vagaries of the Martian ore, or to a simple clerical error. But I find myself, in the present climate, less inclined to attribute it to clerical error."

Wren's pen clicked. Once. He let it rest. "And you wish to resolve this discrepancy. Now. In this room. With me."

"I wish to *understand* it," Drummond corrected, and the emphasis on the single syllable was a blade drawn slowly from its sheath. "Understanding is a more polite word. Less accusatory. More in keeping with the spirit of collegiality that I believe the consortium has always prized." He glanced toward the door. He glanced toward the door again. His hand drifted, almost unconsciously, toward the handle, and withdrew. "One does not wish to be *seen* understanding, does one? Not with the station in the state it is in. Not with the Inspector walking the corridors and the journalist tapping her little recording device at her hip."

In the Rotunda, Inspector Elara Voss arrived with the purposeful, unhurried gait of a woman who has already decided what she will find and is merely confirming the shape of the evidence to match her expectation. She stopped beside the armchair, where Tanaka was still kneeling, and said, "Dr. Tanaka. Have you found anything of interest?" Tanaka looked up. Her face was calm, but her eyes were not. "The seam is intact," she said. "But the leather beneath it is worn in a pattern that does not correspond to ordinary use. Someone has been sitting in this chair. Repeatedly. Recently." Voss crouched. She ran her thumb along the worn patch. The station ticked around them.

And in the Salon, the two men stood on either side of the long table like chess players who have each moved a single piece and are now studying the board for the implications of the other's move. Neither had sat down. Neither would sit down. To sit was to concede a position, and neither man had yet decided which position he wished to concede.

"The note," Drummond said.

The word fell between them like a stone into still water. Wren's jaw tightened. The muscles in his neck stood out again, and he pressed his thumb and forefinger together in a small, unconscious gesture, as though he were trying to hold something in place that was threatening to slip.

"The note," he repeated. Flatly. As though the word were a foreign term he was translating in real time.

"Yes. The note." Drummond's voice had dropped. It was no longer the voice of a colleague, a shareholder, a man discussing market fluctuations over sherry. It was the voice of a man who has been lying awake at two in the morning, staring at the ceiling, doing the arithmetic of his own ruin. "Margaret's private note. The one she tucked into the consortium ledger before—" He stopped. Swallowed. "Before the, well. Before the unfortunate event in the Archive." He reached for his brandy again. Set it down. Did not drink. "I have not had the chance to read it. I confess I have been... preoccupied. With other matters. With the accuracy of certain figures. But I suspect, Wren, that it concerns more than one set of figures."

There. There is a seam here that was not in the original schematics.

The whisper was so faint it might have been the hum of the ventilation system, that perpetual sterile drone that had become the station's second heartbeat. But it was not. It was a woman's voice, low and self-directed, the kind of murmur one might offer to a recalcitrant plant root before coaxing it free of the soil. And the hands that spoke it were not the hands of a detective, nor the hands of a stakeholder with ink-stained fingers and a quarterly report to justify. They were the hands of a botanist. Soft-palmed. The cuticles slightly ragged from weeks of work in the hydroponic trays. The nails short, practical, with a faint greenish tinge at the tips that no amount of scrubbing in the recycled water would quite remove.

Dr. Yuki Tanaka was on her knees on the polished floor of the Grand Rotunda, and she was not looking for a murder weapon.

Her right hand had slipped beneath the armchair's frame, fingers spread, pressing along the junction where the leather upholstery met the brass strapping. The motion was not random. It was not the clumsy, sweeping search of a woman who had never before hidden anything. Her thumb found the seam with the unhurried certainty of a finger tracing the vein of a leaf, and her other hand—left, the one with the slightly longer middle finger, the one that had spent three years cradling seedlings no larger than a thumbnail—pressed a specific point on the underside of the chair's crossbar. A point that, in the station's original blueprints, corresponded to nothing. An empty space in the architecture of the furniture. A space that, in the architecture of the last four weeks, had held something else entirely.

The faint click of a hidden latch registering under her fingernail was swallowed by the Rotunda's vast, curved acoustics. The sound traveled upward, struck the domed ceiling, and returned as nothing more than a whisper of pressure against the eardrum. No one would have heard it. No one would have known to listen.

Except, of course, that someone was listening.

Inspector Elara Voss had been standing at the far end of the Rotunda for perhaps thirty seconds. Perhaps forty. She had entered from the Spoke Gallery corridor with the particular, unhurried gait of a woman who had already decided what she would find and was merely confirming the shape of it. She wore no expression. Her grey coat was buttoned to the throat despite the station's climate control, and her hands were clasped loosely behind her back, thumbs resting in the crook of her elbows. She was watching the botanist's hands. Not her face. Not the hair that fell forward in a dark curtain, hiding the eyes, hiding the mouth, hiding whatever calculation was being performed behind the temples. Her hands. The way the right index finger hooked a thread of upholstery and pulled. The way the left hand's palm flattened against the underside of the crossbar with a pressure that suggested not searching but confirming. Not *where is it* but *is it still there.*

The green smell of hydroponic overflow drifted up from the lower vents, that faint vegetal sweetness that clung to the Rotunda's underbelly like a memory of soil. It was the smell of the Conservatory, of the trays and the misting cycles and the particular humidity that Dr. Tanaka's division maintained at precisely sixty-two percent. It was, in the sterile, recycled air of the Grand Rotunda, a small and intimate intrusion. A reminder that beneath the brass and the crystal and the cold geometry of the station, there was dirt. There was growth. There were hands that knew the difference between a healthy root and a rotting one.

Voss did not move. She did not speak. She watched the botanist's right hand withdraw from beneath the chair with a small, satisfied withdrawal, the fingers closing briefly in a fist before opening again, as though releasing a breath.

"You will forgive me," Voss said.

The voice was quiet. Dry. The tone of a woman reading a label on a jar of preserved specimens. Not accusatory. Not yet. Merely present.

Dr. Tanaka's head did not turn. Her hair remained a curtain. Her shoulders, visible above the collar of her work jacket, were rigid in a way that suggested not surprise but the particular stillness of a person who has been observed completing a task she intended to finish before being observed.

"Of course," she said. And then, after a pause measured in heartbeats: "I was examining the frame. Checking for signs of forced entry. The murder, Inspector. One cannot simply look at the wine glass and the hair and call the work finished."

"No," Voss agreed. "One cannot."

She walked forward. The floor did not announce her footsteps; the Rotunda's polished surface absorbed them, returned them as a faint vibration rather than a sound. She stopped two paces from the kneeling figure and looked down. The angle was deliberate. It placed her eyes at the level of the botanist's hands, still resting on the floor, fingers spread, palms up, in a posture that was almost an offering. An almost.

In the Observatorium, three decks aft and two corridors to port, Sera Okafor was running her fingertips along the brass mount of the great telescope with the careful, cataloguing touch of a woman building a case file from fragments. The red dust sat there in a thin smear, incongruous against the cold metal, a thread of cinnabar in a world of grey and white. She had found it twenty minutes ago, or perhaps an hour ago; time had become elastic in the station since the body in the Mining Archive. Beside her, Captain Blackwood stood with his hands in his pockets, his jaw working in the particular rhythm of a man who was rehearsing a sentence he did not yet trust himself to finish.

"The dust is inconsistent," Sera said, her voice carrying the flat, professional cadence of a journalist filing a dispatch. "Sealed environment. No external contamination. Which means someone brought it up from the colony. Recently."

Blackwood's jaw stopped working. He looked at the dust. He looked at Sera. He looked at the dust again. "Noted," he said. And then, because the silence was becoming a shape in the small room, a shape with edges: "Noted. And the drapes?"

"Checked. Nothing. Clean." She withdrew her hand, turned it over, examined her fingertips. They were clean. They should have been clean. She turned the hand over again, as though the act of looking might produce a result different from the first. "Clean."

In the Stakeholders' Salon, the air was thicker, warmer, shot through with the low hum of the station's rotation and the murmur of a dozen small anxieties pressing against the walls. Dr. Wren stood by the window, his back to the room, his reflection ghosted in the glass. Felix Drummond paced. Not quickly. Not with the frantic energy of a man who has nothing to lose. With the measured, circular gait of a man who is counting the number of steps between himself and the door, and finding the number insufficient.

"The numbers," Drummond said, and the word came out clipped, as though he had bitten the back of it. "The numbers in the quarterly report. The redacted line. I find I am... concerned about the accuracy."

Wren did not turn from the window. "Accuracy is a word one uses when one has already decided what the inaccuracy is."

"Yes," Drummond said. And then, more softly, as though speaking to the glass: "Yes. Precisely."

The two men stood in the warm, gold-lit salon, each watching the other's reflection in the window, each waiting for the other to blink first.

In the Grand Rotunda, Voss had not moved. She was still looking at the botanist's hands. The tone of a woman filing a report in a voice that had been carefully calibrated to sound like curiosity rather than calculation.

The Grand Rotunda turned slowly beneath their feet, the great curvature of the station's interior sweeping the light from the overhead panels in a long, languid arc. Beyond the reinforced observation glass, Mars hung in the black firmament like a rusted coin pressed against the eye of the void—patient, indifferent, eternal. The fountain in the centre of the rotunda murmured its thin, perpetual trickle, and the sound of it, in the silence that had settled over the six remaining souls of the Ares Meridian, was almost unbearable in its ordinariness.

Inspector Elara Voss stood at the head of the long mahogany table, her hands resting flat upon its polished surface as though she were steadying the room itself. Around her, the others sat in a semicircle that spoke of no particular arrangement save the accident of where each had been standing when the summons came. Dr. Yuki Tanaka had her hands folded in her lap, her knuckles white. Felix Drummond's cufflinks caught the light as his fingers turned them, round and round, a metronome of small tics. Captain Blackwood sat with his spine rigid, his jaw set in that particular way that suggested he had already composed his own eulogy for the evening and was waiting, with great professional patience, for the rest of the cast to take their marks. Sera Okafor had her notebook open but had not written a word in eleven minutes.

And Dr. Cassius Wren sat at the far end, his geological coat still buttoned to the throat, his expression that of a man who had been waiting for a train that he had, perhaps, already missed.

Inspector Voss let the silence stretch. It was, she had learned in twenty-three years of service across three colonies and two orbital stations, the most useful instrument in any investigator's kit. It let the guilty man rehearse his defences. It let the innocent feel the weight of suspicion settle upon them like a cold draft. And it let the room remember, in the quiet, what it had lost.

"Margaret Ashworth," Voss said, and the name fell into the rotunda with the flat finality of a gavel, "died on the morning of Time Step thirty-seven. The station's internal chronometer places the hour at nine-fifteen. The location is the Mining Archive, third sub-level, behind the sealed door that requires a secondary biometric key. She was found at four minutes past ten, slumped against the filing cabinets, a geological hammer driven through the left side of her chest. The wound was singular. The angle was downward and inward, consistent with a standing assailant of moderate height delivering a single, decisive blow. There was no struggle. No defensive wounds. Margaret was, in the language of the coroner's preliminary note, 'taken by surprise, and by someone she did not expect to be the one who struck.'"

She paused. Let the image settle.

"Now. I am not here to entertain speculation. I am not here to invite you to offer alibis, to suggest that perhaps the ventilation schedule was malfunctioning, or that perhaps a draft from the hydroponic bay carried spores into the archive and the poor woman simply... perished of some botanical accident." She glanced, with the faintest tightening of the mouth, toward Dr. Tanaka, who met the look and returned it with a steady, quiet nod. "I am here to tell you what happened. And then I am here to tell you who did it."

Felix Drummond's cufflinks stopped turning.

"The first thing I wish you to understand," Voss continued, "is that this was not a crime of passion, nor of robbery, nor of political ambition in the grand sense. It was a crime of preservation. A man looked at the life he had built—twenty-two years of geological surveying, three published monographs on Martian regolith composition, a position of considerable authority on this station—and he looked at the woman standing before him in that archive, and he calculated, with the cold arithmetic of a man who has spent his life reading the strata of the earth, that she was the single fault line that would bring the whole edifice down. And he chose, in that instant, to close the fault. Permanently."

She reached into the leather satchel at her side and withdrew a small, cloth-wrapped bundle. She set it upon the table with a soft thud.

"The weapon. A geological hammer, standard issue from the surveying division. Its head was found in the archive, resting against the base of the filing cabinet, its face stained. The forensic team confirmed iron particulate consistent with human haemoglobin. The handle bore a single partial print, which matched the right hand of the Chief Geologist. I will not insult you by suggesting there was any ambiguity in that match."

Dr. Wren's mouth opened. Closed. Opened again. Nothing came.

"But a weapon, in itself, proves only that a hand held it. It does not prove that a hand swung it. And so I must walk you through the architecture of the afternoon, or rather, the morning, in which this was done, and I must show you how every apparent alibis in this room was, in fact, a curtain drawn slightly too carefully over a very ordinary window."

She turned, slowly, to Felix Drummond.

"Mr. Drummond. You were in the Mining Archive at nine-fourteen. You told me, when I first questioned you, that you had been reviewing the ventilation schedule. You fidgeted with your cufflinks. You avoided my eyes when I asked you why a senior stakeholder of Drummond-Voss Capital Partners would be consulting a ventilation schematic in a sub-level archive at that hour of the morning. And yet, when I examined the archive, I found, tucked behind the second row of sample drawers, a navigational chart. The route was marked in red ink, a single bold line leading from the station's lower docking corridor to the restricted shaft of the mining colony below. The ink was still slightly tacky. It had been drawn, I would estimate, no more than an hour before the murder. And the hand that drew it was not yours, Mr. Drummond. The stroke was too controlled, too vertical, the way a man who maps ore veins for a living marks his work. It was the hand of a geologist."

Drummond exhaled. His shoulders dropped a fraction.

"And the brandy," Voss continued, not looking at him. "A glass, half-full, gone cloudy amber on the table beside the archive's reading lamp. I had it tested. Aconite. A very small quantity, enough to produce a sensation of numbness in the lips and a mild disorientation, but not, in a healthy adult, enough to kill. It was a distraction. A means of ensuring that whoever sat in that reading chair, sipping their drink and reviewing their documents, would be slightly dazed, slightly slow to react, when the door opened behind them. Margaret Ashworth sat in that chair. And the glass was set down by the man who would, four minutes later, pick up the hammer."

She let that land.

"The black hair on the brass railing of the archive's upper gallery. It did not match Margaret. It was dark, coarse, and bore a slight chemical residue consistent with the fixatives used in geological sample preparation. The red Martian dust on the telescope mount in the observation deck—impossible in a sealed environment unless carried in on a boot or a coat from a decontamination-adjacent corridor, which, as you all know, is precisely where the geological surveying team keeps their field gear."

Captain Blackwood shifted in his seat. "Inspector, I spoke with Margaret that morning. About the financial discrepancies. The assay numbers. I was... concerned."

"Yes, Captain. And I am grateful for that. Your concern, your nervousness, your avoidance of eye contact—all of it pointed me toward the financial records. And it was there, in the leather-bound journal I found in the hidden compartment of the armchair cushion in this very rotunda, that the true shape of the matter revealed itself."

She held up the small journal. Its cover was worn, the leather soft with handling.

"Margaret Ashworth was not, as several of you assumed, a passive observer of the colony's financial affairs. She was the auditor. The independent auditor appointed by the Interplanetary Trade Council to verify the mineral assay reports submitted by this station's scientific division. And in this journal, she had recorded, in her own precise hand, a series of discrepancies. Assay values inflated by as much as forty percent. Rare-earth concentrations reported where spectroscopic analysis showed only trace quantities. A pattern so consistent, so methodical, that it could not be the product of error. It was the product of a hand that had, over the course of eighteen months, quietly rewritten the geological truth of this colony to serve a very different purpose."

Dr. Tanaka's eyes were very still. "The hydroponic yield projections," she said softly. "They were calibrated to the assay numbers. If the mineral values were false, the entire economic model for the colony's first harvest cycle was... fabricated."

"Precisely," Voss said. "And Margaret intended to present her findings to the Trade Council on the following morning. The session was scheduled. The documentation was prepared. She had, in short, already done the work. All that remained was the telling."

She set the journal down. Picked up the cloth-wrapped hammer again, then set it back. The gesture was deliberate, almost tender, as though she were handling something that had belonged to a friend.

"The pruning shears in the hydroponic bay, their blades wet with chlorophyll and something darker. The small sidearm in its walnut case, one round missing from the cylinder. I will not pretend these were not part of the morning's choreography. The shears were meant to be found in the archive, to suggest a botanist's involvement. The sidearm was meant to be discovered in the communications officer's quarters, to suggest a different suspect, a different narrative. They were props, Dr. Wren. Staging. You are a man who has spent his life reading the layers of the earth, and you applied that same patience to the layers of a story you wished others to tell."

She turned.

The rotunda had gone very quiet. The fountain murmured. Mars turned, slow and red, beyond the glass.

"Dr. Wren."

The name fell differently than the others had. It fell like a stone into still water.

Cassius Wren looked up. His face was, in that moment, remarkably composed. The kind of composure that is not the absence of fear but its very architecture, the rigid frame within which the fear is held, for now, at bay.

"Yes, Inspector."

"At nine-fifteen, you were in the Mining Archive. Margaret Ashworth was seated at the reading table, the glass of brandy before her, the aconite making her slow, her thoughts a little thick. She was reviewing the final pages of her audit. She looked up. She saw you. And you saw her see you."

Voss's voice did not rise. It did not need to.

"You did not raise the hammer in anger. You did not raise it in fear, though fear was certainly there. You raised it because in that moment, the life you had built, the career, the authority, the quiet dignity of a man who had spent two decades bent over the bones of another world, was ending. And you were not willing to let it end in a courtroom, in a penal colony, in the cold and the shame of it. So you took the hammer from the surveying rack at the back of the archive, and you crossed the room, and you struck."

A silence. Complete. Even the fountain seemed to hold its breath.

"You then wiped the handle. You placed the navigational chart behind the drawers, the red line a small, deliberate misdirection pointing the eye toward the colony shaft, toward a narrative of theft rather than fraud. You set the brandy glass where it would be found. You walked out of the archive, up the gallery, and you let the black hair fall from your hairbrush onto the railing where the light would catch it. You were, in every particular, a man who understood that a crime, like a geological formation, is only as convincing as the layering of its evidence."

Dr. Wren's hands were in his lap. His fingers were very still.

"I must ask you one question, Dr. Wren, and I will ask it plainly, because you have earned the plainness of it. Why Margaret?"

Wren's jaw worked. A muscle in his cheek twitched, once, twice. And then, very slowly, the composure cracked. Not dramatically. Not with a shout or a lunge. It cracked the way a fault line cracks in the deep strata, silently, with a pressure that had been building for a very long time.

"Because she was the only one who knew," he said. His voice was low, hoarse, the voice of a man speaking to himself as much as to the room. "The others—the Council, the stakeholders, Drummond-Voss—they would have seen a discrepancy. A number that was slightly high. A concentration that seemed generous. They would have questioned. They would have investigated. But Margaret—Margaret had the spectroscopic logs. She had the raw data from the bore samples. She had the truth, Inspector. Not a suspicion. Not a hunch. The truth, in numbers, in graphs, in the cold language of atomic composition. And she was going to hand it to the Council on a Tuesday morning, in a conference room, with a glass of water and a perfectly reasonable expression on her face. And I would have been... what? Disgraced. Stripped. Sent to the penal settlement on Deimos, to pick rock for the rest of a life I had no intention of spending in a cell."

He looked up. His eyes were dry. That was, somehow, worse than tears.

"I did not hate her. I want you to understand that. I did not hate her. I respected her work. I respected her precision. And that is precisely why it was so... efficient. She was not a threat in the way a thief is a threat. She was a threat in the way a truth is a threat. And I could not survive a truth."

Felix Drummond was staring at the table. Captain Blackwood's face had gone the colour of old paper. Sera Okafor's pen was still, her notebook open to a blank page, and she was looking at Wren with an expression that held equal parts horror and the particular, aching recognition of a journalist who has finally found the story and wishes, with all her heart, that she had not.

Dr. Tanaka reached across the table and, very gently, closed her own notebook.

Inspector Voss did not gloat. She did not smile. She stood for a long moment, her hands flat on the mahogany, and she looked at the man who had, four hours ago, been the Chief Geologist of the Ares Meridian, and then she said, in a voice that was no different in tone from the one she had used to discuss the ventilation schedule or the brandy glass, "Dr. Wren, you are under arrest. You will be secured in the station's detention hold pending transfer to the jurisdiction of the Interplanetary Trade Council. You will have the right to counsel, and you will have the right to a fair hearing. But you will not have the right to leave this station, or this orbit, or this life, as you knew it."

She turned to Captain Blackwood. "Captain. Two of your crew. Secure Dr. Wren's person. Remove his coat, his instruments, anything that might be construed as a means of harm. And seal the geological surveying division. No one enters or exits until I give the order."

Blackwood rose. His movements were stiff, formal, the movements of a man performing a duty he had rehearsed a thousand times in simulation and never once in the flesh. He crossed to Wren, placed a hand on the geologist's shoulder. Wren did not resist. He did not, so far as any of them could see, move at all. He simply sat, and the hand on his shoulder, and the rotunda, and Mars, and the fountain, and the six of them, were all that existed in the small, rotating world of the Ares Meridian.

"Inspector," Blackwood said, quietly, "the detention hold is on sub-level four. I will escort him personally."

"Thank you, Captain."

Wren stood. His legs were, he might have known, a little unsteady. He buttoned his coat, though it was already buttoned. He smoothed the front of it, a small, automatic gesture, the gesture of a man who had spent his life smoothing the surfaces of things, making them even, making them presentable, making them true.

He walked toward the door. At the threshold, he paused. He did not turn around.

"Margaret was a good auditor," he said. His voice was very small. "I would like you to know that. I would like someone to know that, before the Council, before the hearing, before the rest of it. She was good. And I was... afraid."

Then he walked out, and Captain Blackwood followed, and the door sealed behind them with a soft, pneumatic sigh.

The rotunda exhaled.

Felix Drummond removed his cufflinks and set them on the table, one by one, with the careful deliberation of a man laying down a small, unnecessary armour. Dr. Tanaka pressed her fingertips to her lips and held them there, breathing, breathing, as though the air in the rotunda had grown thin and she was trying, by sheer will, to fill her lungs with something other than the memory of the archive, the hammer, the gasp.

Sera Okafor closed her notebook. She did not write the word *murder*. She did not write the word *geologist*. She wrote, in her small, precise hand, the words *the truth, in numbers*, and then she underlined them, and she left the page there.

Inspector Voss stood at the head of the table. She looked at the fountain. A single drop of water, thin as a thread, had caught on the rim and was slowly, slowly dissolving into the trickle below. She watched it go.

"Margaret Ashworth," she said, and her voice was softer now, stripped of its procedural edge, "was not merely an auditor. She was the conscience of this station. The one person in this room, in this orbit, in this entire colony, who believed that the numbers should be the numbers, that the rock should be the rock, that the truth of the ground beneath one's feet was not a matter of opinion or ambition or fear. And she was killed for it. In a filing room. With a hammer. At nine-fifteen in the morning, while the rest of us were having our coffee and pretending that the world was a reasonable place."

She looked around the table. At the botanist, the stakeholder, the captain, the journalist. At the empty chair where Wren had sat, now bare, now only a faint impression in the cushion, a small dent in the upholstery that would, in time, spring back.

"We will go on," she said. "The station will rotate. The colony will mine. The Trade Council will convene, and the records will be examined, and the truth will be spoken, because that is what Margaret intended, and that is what she died to ensure. And we will do it properly. We will do it with the numbers as they are, and the rock as it is, and the truth as it is. Not because it is easy. Not because it is comfortable. But because a woman in a filing room believed it was the right thing to do, and she was not wrong."

She picked up her satchel. She set it over her shoulder.

"The Grand Rotunda will be sealed for the night. The fountain will be drained and cleaned. The armchair will be replaced. And in the morning, the light will come in through the observation glass at the same angle it did this morning, and Mars will be where Mars is, and the station will turn, and the world will go on, as it must, in the quiet and the gravity and the long, slow rotation of things."

She paused at the door.

"Good evening to you all. Try to sleep. And if any of you should, in the night, find yourselves reaching for a hammer, a glass of brandy, or a navigational chart in red ink, I would very much prefer that you simply set them down, and pour a cup of tea, and let the morning come."

She left.

The rotunda turned. The fountain murmured. Mars hung in the black, patient and red and utterly indifferent to the small, bright, fragile world spinning in its orbit, where a woman had died for the truth, and a man had killed her for the lie, and the rest had gathered in a circle of chairs and listened, and remembered, and carried the weight of it forward into the long, slow turning of the night.
